Professional APEX
The Services Guide
Data, Schemas, Orchestrations, and Agent Composition
Volume II · Data & Architecture
A Deep Dive Into the 38 Cataloged Services Across Seven Practices

The middle volume of the APEX Trilogy — paired with the Sellers Guide (Volume I · positioning APEX-accelerated Services) and the Deployment Guide (Volume III · deploying Services on the Microsoft Platform). This book is about the data and orchestration that make every Service real, and how the APEX framework accelerates the building of those Services: client SOR systems landed into Bronze, conformed to Silver, served from Gold, called by Agents through MCP, and orchestrated by n8n on the laptop or Azure Logic Apps Hybrid in production.

WROX-STYLE EDITION · SERVICES VOLUME
Keven Markham · VP Enterprise Transformation · Deloitte's Microsoft Technology & Services Practice · May 2026

How to Use This Book

What APEX Is — read this first

APEX is a framework that accelerates the building of services and agents on the Microsoft Platform. It is the architectural substrate Deloitte's Microsoft practice uses internally to deliver Services faster, more consistently, and with more rigorous governance. APEX is not a product and is not sold. What clients buy are Services — the 38-Service catalog enumerated in Part VI, each a productized envelope with a KPI commitment, scope, persona model, and two-contract Independence-compliant commercial structure. The Service is the contractable unit; the framework is the means.

When this book says "APEX," read "the APEX framework." When it says "Service," read "the productized envelope clients actually buy." When it says "Service architect uses APEX to build RC-E2E-03," read "the architect uses framework primitives — medallion · MCP · LEDGER · agent runtime · orchestration parity · trust envelope — to build the Service the client contracted for." The distinction is structural, not stylistic; the two-contract Independence model (client ↔ Microsoft for platform · client ↔ Deloitte for Services) makes the Service the contractable unit, not the framework.

The APEX library is now three volumes. The Sellers Guide answers how to position APEX-accelerated Services; the Deployment Guide answers how to deploy those Services on the Microsoft Platform; this volume — the Services Guide — answers the question between them: what data drives each Service, how the APEX framework shapes it, and how Agents compose over it through orchestration.

It is the volume the architects ask for after the seller has won the deal and before the deployment lead opens Bicep. It is also the volume the agent maintainers, schema owners, and MCP catalog editors return to repeatedly. If the Sellers Guide is the menu and the Deployment Guide is the kitchen wiring, this book is the recipe — and like recipes, it lives or dies on its handling of ingredients.

Audience

  • Service architects designing a new service composition or onboarding a new client into an existing Service — the catalog deep dives in Part VI are the reference, with Parts II–IV providing the data substrate.
  • Data engineers building the Bronze → Silver → Gold pipelines per Service — Parts II, III, and IV are the core; Appendix A indexes every schema referenced in the book.
  • MCP catalog editors exposing Gold-mart access to agents — Chapter 13 plus Appendix B (the full MCP tool catalog) are the reference.
  • Orchestration authors writing the workflow JSON per scenario — Part V plus Appendix D.
  • Agent maintainers who need to know which schemas an agent reads, which MCP tools it calls, and which Service envelopes it serves — every Part VI chapter has the per-Service composition map.

How the Book Is Organized

Part I — Foundations of Service Data. The data model the rest of the book builds on: why Services are data-first, the Bronze → Silver → Gold medallion in Microsoft Fabric, the canonical schema families (SCML, MERML, PROML, EHRML, ECRML, etc.), and how MCP exposes Gold to agents.

Part II — Bronze Tier · Client Data Sources. The source-of-record inventory by industry (POS, ERP, EHR, CRM, IoT telemetry, etc.), Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence as the real-time hub, ingestion pipelines + tokenization, PII classification, and the mi-apex-merch-pii-unlock identity.

Part III — Silver Tier · Canonical Models. The conformance layer: how raw Bronze rows become canonical Silver entities, the schema library deep dive, and Microsoft Purview as the lineage and quality control surface.

Part IV — Gold Tier · Decision-Ready Data. Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse patterns for serving Gold, per-Service Gold marts, and the MCP tool surface that makes Gold reachable from agents.

Part V — Orchestrations. The workflow design patterns (DAG, fan-out, HITL, fan-in), n8n on the laptop, Azure Logic Apps Hybrid on Microsoft platform, and cross-Service composition (e.g., RC-E2E-03 reading SCML.Lot owned by RC-E2E-09).

Part VI — Service Catalog by Practice. One chapter per practice covering all 38 Services. Every Service profile follows the same template — data sources, silver schemas, gold marts, agents composed, MCP tools, orchestration shape, KPI attribution path. RC-E2E-03 (Pricing & Revenue Decision Service) is the worked example throughout.

Note — How to read this book non-linearly

If you are working on a specific Service, jump to its profile in Part VI first; that profile names the schemas, MCP tools, and orchestration patterns it depends on, with cross-references back to Parts II–V. If you are designing infrastructure for any Service, start with Part I, read Parts II–IV in order, and treat Part VI as the catalog of consumers of what you are about to build.

Conventions

This volume inherits the conventions of the other two: callouts for Note · Warning · Best Practice · Try It Out · Objection · Independence; code blocks labeled by language; Microsoft platform components named at the resource-type level (Microsoft.Fabric/capacities, Microsoft.OperationalInsights/workspaces, etc.). Where the Deployment Guide spells out "Azure Container Registry" rather than "ACR," this book follows the same disambiguation.

Schema names follow the APEX framework convention — uppercase prefix (SCML, MERML, PROML) followed by an entity name in PascalCase: SCML.Lot, MERML.Markdown, PROML.Pricing. Bronze tables are prefixed br_, Silver with the schema name itself, Gold with g_: br_pos_transactions → SCML.Inventory → g_inventory_position_daily. The pattern is uniform across all 38 Services.

Independence — Same boundaries apply

The two-contract structure from the Deployment Guide carries through this volume. Client SOR data flows under the client's Microsoft platform contract; schemas, MCP tool definitions, and orchestration JSON are Deloitte-authored artifacts shipped to the client tenant via the same mirror boundary that ships agent images. Throughout this book, the relationship is described as "Deloitte's Microsoft practice," "DMTSP," or "Microsoft-native deployment." Never partner, alliance, or strategic alliance.

Source Material

This book is downstream of:

  • APEX-Scenario-Chains.xlsx — 38 unique service IDs across 7 practices · 724 scenarios · Scenario Library and Featured Chains sheets.
  • APEX-comprehensive-solutions-reference.md / .docx — schema definitions and entity-relationship details for all canonical schema families.
  • APEX-Stacked-Architecture-Narrated.html — the conceptual stack (data plane → semantic plane → agent plane → runtime plane) that this book makes operational per Service.
  • APEX-Agentic-Merch-Component-Architecture.html — the component-level reference that links service composition to deployment topology.
  • Professional APEX Sellers Guide — KPI envelopes, persona briefs, commercial structures.
  • Professional APEX Deployment Guide — Microsoft platform substrate, Bicep modules, image pipeline, two-contract boundaries.

Where this book gives a schema definition or an MCP tool signature, the sources above are authoritative. Where they disagree, they win and this book will be reconciled in the next edition.

Welcome to the Services Guide. The data model is on the next page.

Part I

Foundations of Service Data

A Service in APEX is not a product feature; it is a productized envelope around a flow of data. Bronze rows from client systems become Silver canonical entities, get joined into Gold decision-ready marts, are exposed to agents through MCP tools, and drive orchestrated decisions whose outcomes return to the audit chain. This part settles the four foundations that the rest of the book operationalizes: why services are data-first, what the medallion tiers do, what the canonical schema families look like, and how agents actually reach the data through MCP.

Chapters in this Part

  • Chapter 0Foreword: Why Services Are Data-First
  • Chapter 1Bronze → Silver → Gold
  • Chapter 2Canonical Schema Families
  • Chapter 3MCP and the Service Tool Catalog
Chapter 0

Foreword — Why Services Are Data-First

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Frames the Service as a data flow first, an agent composition second, and a Bicep deployment third.
  • Sets out the three rules that make every Service profile in Part VI legible.
  • Explains why a Service's KPI envelope is anchored on the data model, not on the agents.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • A Service is a flow of data first; agents and orchestration are stations along the flow.
  • Three rules: every Service has the same data shape · Bronze/Silver/Gold are not optional · agents read only Gold.
  • The KPI envelope lives on the data model, not the agents — build the KPI Gold mart first.

0.1 The Service Is the Data Flow

Walk into any APEX engagement and you will hear three different short answers to the question "what is a Service?" The seller will say it is a productized envelope with a KPI commitment. The deployment lead will say it is a Bicep module that composes Container Apps over Layer 1. The agent maintainer will say it is a set of agent images and prompts. All three answers are correct. None of them is the answer that gets a Service to ship.

The answer that gets a Service to ship is: a Service is a flow of data, with the agents and the orchestration as the stations along the flow. Bronze rows arrive from client SOR systems. Silver conformance produces canonical entities the agents understand. Gold marts make those entities decision-ready. MCP tools expose Gold to agents. Orchestrations call agents in sequence. Decisions get attributed back to the audit chain. The KPI envelope is the measurable difference between the input flow and the output flow.

Every other view — commercial, deployment, agent — is downstream of the data view. If the data flow is wrong, the most beautiful Bicep in the world will deploy a Service that misses its KPI. If the data flow is right, even crude orchestration usually produces the audited outcome the Sellers Guide promised.

0.2 The Three Rules

Three rules thread every chapter of this book and every Service profile in Part VI. State them up front so the rest is legible.

0.2.1 Rule 1 — Every Service has the same data shape

All 38 Services in the catalog land Bronze, conform to Silver, serve Gold, expose MCP tools, run orchestrations, and write LEDGER rows. The shape is uniform. What differs from Service to Service is which SOR systems land into Bronze, which canonical schemas the Silver layer uses, which Gold marts get built, which MCP tools get registered, which agents compose, and how the workflow branches. The shape is invariant.

This is what makes the catalog feasible. A Service architect onboarding HLS-E2E-04 (Prior Authorization) does not learn a new architecture; she fills in the same template with HLS-specific schemas (EHRML, CMSML) and HLS-specific orchestration. The architect onboarding TMT-TEL-RAA-04 (Revenue Assurance Variance Close) fills in the same template with TMT-specific schemas (BRMML, GLML).

0.2.2 Rule 2 — Bronze, Silver, Gold are not optional layers

It is tempting, when a client SOR exposes data in a clean shape, to skip Bronze and land directly into Silver. It is also tempting, when Silver looks Gold-shaped already, to skip the Gold mart. Both shortcuts produce Services that work for one client and break on the second.

Bronze is the immutable record of what the client system actually said. Silver is the canonical conformance to APEX schemas. Gold is the decision-ready denormalization for an agent to query in O(1). Each tier exists because each carries a different commitment: Bronze commits to fidelity, Silver commits to conformance, Gold commits to query latency. Collapsing tiers collapses commitments.

Warning — The "we'll just land directly into Silver" trap

This sentence appears in every engagement. Reject it. The next client's SOR will be different from this client's SOR; the Bronze layer is what absorbs that variance. Without Bronze, every new client onboarding mutates Silver, and the schema library stops being a library.

0.2.3 Rule 3 — Agents do not query Bronze or Silver

Agents in APEX read Gold. Always. The MCP tool surface is built against Gold marts, and the agent's prompts assume Gold-shaped facts. There are two reasons for this rule, both load-bearing:

  • Latency. Bronze is high-fidelity but high-fan-out — joining Bronze rows for one decision can scan hundreds of millions of records. Silver is canonical but still normalized; an agent traversing Silver joins five tables to assemble a single fact. Gold is denormalized for the question the agent asks; an agent gets the answer in one query.
  • Audit. The LEDGER row records which Gold mart the agent read and at which row hash. That hash is reproducible — the row is in Gold and the row is in the lakehouse and the lakehouse is in Microsoft Fabric. If agents read Bronze or Silver directly, the audit reconstruction loses a hop and gets fragile.

The exception that proves the rule: the mi-apex-merch-pii-unlock identity (introduced in the Deployment Guide) does reach into a PII-tokenized Silver column at HITL-approval time, but only after writing a LEDGER row that says it is about to. That bypass exists for one specific commitment — re-identifying a tokenized customer for a regulatory artifact — and it is auditable.

0.3 Why the KPI Envelope Lives on the Data, Not the Agents

The Sellers Guide commits a Service to a KPI envelope. RC-E2E-03 commits to GM +3.2pp, DoH −28%, Markdown-to-Clear +41%. Notice what those numbers measure: they are differences in the data. Margin per SKU per store, days-on-hand per SKU per store, markdown lifecycle counts. Those numbers are realized by Gold marts that compute them, not by agents that talk about them.

This matters for how the architecture is sequenced. When you onboard a new client into RC-E2E-03, the first thing you build is the SCML and MERML Silver layer over the client's POS + ERP + competitive-pricing feeds, and the Gold marts that compute the KPI baseline. Only after those exist do you compose the agents over them. Agents that decide markdowns without a Gold-mart-computed baseline have nothing to attribute their decisions against; the KPI envelope cannot be measured without the data view.

Best Practice — Build the KPI Gold mart before the agents

For any new Service onboarding into a tenant, the first deliverable is a Gold mart that emits the Service's KPI envelope from the existing data. If that mart is built and the numbers come out flat, the agents have not even run yet — and the data architecture is already proving where the value is hiding. This is the cheapest and earliest commercial signal in the engagement.

0.4 What This Book Is Not

This is a Service-and-data book. It is not:

  • A commercial book — read the Sellers Guide for envelopes, ECIF eligibility, persona briefs, and the Wave model.
  • A deployment book — read the Deployment Guide for Bicep modules, the three-layer cake, the two-registry boundary, and Day-Zero onboarding.
  • A data-platform book about Microsoft Fabric internals — Microsoft's Fabric documentation covers the engine; this book covers what to put on it.

If you are asking "what does this Service sell?" you want the Sellers Guide. If you are asking "how does this Service deploy?" you want the Deployment Guide. If you are asking "what data does this Service read, what shape does it produce, and how do agents traverse it?" — you are in the right book.

Key Takeaways

  • A Service is a data flow first; agents and orchestration are stations along the flow.
  • Three rules: every Service has the same data shape; Bronze/Silver/Gold are not optional; agents read only Gold.
  • The KPI envelope lives on the data model, not the agent — which is why the first deliverable in an onboarding is the KPI Gold mart.
Chapter 1

Bronze → Silver → Gold — The Medallion in Microsoft Fabric

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Defines each medallion tier — fidelity (Bronze), conformance (Silver), decision-readiness (Gold) — and what each commits to.
  • Maps each tier to its Microsoft Fabric and Microsoft platform components by resource type.
  • Establishes the per-tier write/read contracts that the rest of the book references.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • Bronze = fidelity (raw SOR, append-only, no agent reads). Silver = conformance (canonical APEX schemas). Gold = decision-readiness (denormalized, agent-queryable).
  • Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse hosts Bronze + Silver; Warehouse + Direct Lake hosts Gold; KQL DB handles streaming Bronze.
  • LEDGER (Azure SQL/Postgres) and Cosmos DB sit outside the medallion — different planes, different commitments.

1.1 The Three Tiers, Side by Side

Bronze · fidelity

What it is — the immutable record of what the client SOR system actually said.

No conformance, no enrichment, no joining. One row in equals one row stored.

Silver · conformance

What it is — Bronze rows mapped to canonical APEX schema entities.

SCML, MERML, PROML, EHRML, etc. Conformance, deduplication, type stabilization, foreign-key resolution.

Gold · decision-ready

What it is — Silver entities denormalized into agent-queryable marts.

One mart per question agents ask. O(1) reads. KPI baselines materialize here.

1.2 Microsoft Fabric Mapping

Each tier maps to a specific Microsoft Fabric resource type. The MVP and the Deployment Guide treat Microsoft Fabric capacity as a tenant-shared Layer 1 resource (Microsoft.Fabric/capacities), with per-Service workspaces inside it.

TierMicrosoft Fabric componentStorage formatPrimary access
BronzeMicrosoft Fabric Lakehouse · raw zone · or Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence Eventstream + KQL DB for streaming sourcesDelta Parquet (batch) · Delta + checkpointed events (streaming)Append-only writes from Microsoft.DataFactory pipelines or Eventstream sinks
SilverMicrosoft Fabric Lakehouse · curated zone · partitioned by date and entityDelta Parquet · Z-ordered on common join keysSpark notebooks (transformation) · Microsoft Fabric Warehouse (T-SQL) · Microsoft Purview lineage
GoldMicrosoft Fabric Warehouse · Gold marts · or Microsoft Fabric Direct Lake for Power BIDelta Parquet served via Warehouse · materialized viewsMCP tools (agent-facing) · Power BI (operator-facing) · semantic models

The non-Fabric paths exist for specific commitments: Azure Database for PostgreSQL Flexible Server or Azure SQL Database hosts the LEDGER (append-only audit, hash-chained) because per-row insert latency and WORM enforcement are not what Lakehouse optimizes for. Azure Cosmos DB hosts agent session state when an HITL approval has to survive a multi-hour wait without burning a Container App revision. The Bronze/Silver/Gold pipeline does not write to either; the agents and orchestration do.

1.3 The Per-Tier Contracts

1.3.1 Bronze contract

  • Write. Append-only. No updates, no deletes. A correction is a new row with a corrected timestamp.
  • Schema. Whatever the SOR sent. Type-cast minimally; preserve every field even if APEX never reads it.
  • Retention. Bronze is the source of truth in case of any downstream rebuild. Retain ≥ 1 year for STAGE; ≥ 7 years for PROD (often longer for regulated industries).
  • Read. Only by Silver-build pipelines. Agents do not read Bronze.
ADR · architecture decision
ADR-001-01 · 2026-04-15 · accepted

Decision: Bronze is append-only. Corrections produce new rows with corrected timestamps; the original "wrong" row stays.

Why: Two reasons. Audit reproducibility — a regulator (or a careful internal reviewer) reconstructs decisions made on day X using the Bronze that existed on day X, not the "fixed" Bronze of today. Idempotent rebuild — the §8.2 Silver rebuild contract requires Bronze to be deterministic input; mutable Bronze breaks the contract.

Trade-off rejected: Mutable Bronze (with audit columns recording the old value). Cheaper storage; harder reproducibility. The win-rate on this trade-off shifts toward append-only as soon as a regulated workload is in scope (HLS/HIPAA · TMT/SOX · ER/PHMSA · etc.) — and 5 of 7 practices in the catalog are regulated.

1.3.2 Silver contract

  • Write. Idempotent rebuild from Bronze; deletions only by re-derivation, never by direct DELETE.
  • Schema. A canonical APEX schema (SCML, MERML, etc.) — the schema library is the contract surface.
  • Conformance. Type stability, naming consistency, foreign keys resolved to canonical IDs, units stabilized (every monetary field is the same currency, every weight is the same unit).
  • Lineage. Every Silver row is traceable to its Bronze origin row(s) via Microsoft Purview lineage.
  • Read. By Gold-build pipelines and by analysts. Agents do not read Silver directly.

1.3.3 Gold contract

  • Write. Materialized from Silver on a schedule (Microsoft Fabric Pipelines) or on event (Microsoft Fabric Eventstream → notebook trigger).
  • Schema. Per-Service. The Gold mart is shaped exactly to the question the agent asks; it is the answer-shape, not the entity-shape.
  • Latency. The agent reads Gold in < 200 ms. If a Gold query takes longer, the Gold mart is wrong-shaped — denormalize further.
  • KPI. The Service's KPI envelope is computed on Gold and emitted to the LEDGER for attribution.
  • Read. By agents (via MCP tools) and operators (via Power BI semantic models).
Note — Real-time Bronze for streaming sources

For real-time signals (POS terminals, refrigeration telemetry, network fault feeds), Bronze is Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence Eventstream sinking to a KQL DB rather than a Lakehouse. The contract is the same — append-only, full fidelity, no agent reads — but the substrate is event-grade. Silver builds run on a windowed cadence (every 1–5 minutes) rather than a daily batch. Chapter 5 walks the RTI pattern in detail.

1.4 What Lives Where, Concretely

For RC-E2E-03 (the running example):

FILE LAYOUT — RC-E2E-03 medallion zones
Microsoft.Fabric/capacities/contoso-fabric-lab01/workspaces/rc-e2e-03/
  bronze/
    br_pos_transactions/        # raw POS events from client point-of-sale
    br_refrigeration_telemetry/ # raw IoT sensor stream (RTI Eventstream)
    br_erp_inventory/           # raw ERP inventory snapshots (daily)
    br_competitor_pricing/      # raw competitor feed (CSV ingest)
  silver/
    SCML.Inventory              # conformed inventory positions
    SCML.Lot                    # FSMA 204 lot provenance
    MERML.Markdown              # conformed markdown decisions
    MERML.Elasticity            # SKU-store elasticity coefficients
    MERML.Competitor            # conformed competitor pricing
  gold/
    g_excursion_decision_panel  # per-event mart for The Analyst
    g_markdown_proposal_basis   # per-SKU-store mart for Demand Checker + Finance
    g_kpi_rc_e2e_03_daily       # KPI mart — GM, DoH, markdown-to-clear

Notice the asymmetry: there are four Bronze tables (one per source), five Silver entities (canonical conformance and joins), three Gold marts (one per agent question, plus the KPI mart). The fan ratio — Bronze:Silver:Gold — is roughly 1.3:1.5:1 for RC-E2E-03 and is similar across the catalog. Silver is where conformance work concentrates; Gold is where Service shape concentrates.

Key Takeaways

  • Bronze is fidelity (append-only, no agent reads). Silver is conformance (canonical APEX schemas). Gold is decision-readiness (denormalized, agent-queryable).
  • Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse hosts Bronze (raw zone) and Silver (curated zone); Microsoft Fabric Warehouse + Direct Lake hosts Gold; Real-Time Intelligence handles streaming Bronze.
  • LEDGER and Cosmos DB sit outside the medallion — the medallion is the analytics-data plane; LEDGER is the audit plane; Cosmos DB is the agent-state plane.
  • Each tier carries a different commitment: fidelity, conformance, latency. Don't collapse the tiers.

1.5 OneLake Security in User Identity Mode (GA)

The medallion contracts in §1.3 carry security policy with them — Bronze can hold raw lot data with PII or PHI tokens; Silver canonicalizes; Gold projects per agent question. Until April 2026 the SQL analytics endpoint enforced read security via SQL GRANT / REVOKE on tables. Microsoft Fabric's OneLake Security in user identity mode (now GA) replaces that pattern.

In user identity mode, the SQL analytics endpoint passes the signed-in user's Microsoft Entra identity through to OneLake, which evaluates row-level, column-level, and object-level security defined inside the OneLake Lakehouse — not by SQL GRANT / REVOKE. SQL permissions still cover non-data objects (views, stored procedures, functions); table reads are governed exclusively by OneLake security roles.

  • Centralized governance. Define policies once in OneLake; Power BI, notebooks, lakehouse, and SQL analytics endpoint all enforce identically. APEX-M's Tier-3 and Tier-4 PII / PHI rules live in OneLake roles, not in SQL.
  • 1:1 identity mapping across producer and consumer. When an APEX-M service workspace consumes the canonical Silver via OneLake shortcuts (per §1.6), the principal referenced in a producer's OneLake security role must have Fabric Read on the consumer artifact too — group memberships do not resolve across the boundary.
  • Writes still happen through the Lakehouse page. All writes are governed by workspace roles (Admin / Member / Contributor); user identity mode is a read-time enforcement model.

The apex-m.data_lake_fabric.DataLakeFabric implementation honors user identity mode by always opening the SQL analytics endpoint with the operator's Entra Agent ID OBO token (per Deployment Guide §5.1.1), never with the deployment-time delegated identity. This guarantees that an HITL approver looking at customer-facing data sees only what their workforce identity is entitled to see, not what the agent's runtime identity could read.

1.6 The Primary-Workspace Pattern

APEX-M deployments adopt the primary-workspace pattern documented in Microsoft's OneLake security best practices: one primary workspace per practice owns the canonical Silver entities; per-service workspaces consume that data via OneLake shortcuts.

Microsoft.Fabric/workspaces/
  rc-canonical/                               (primary workspace — RC practice)
    Lakehouses/
      scml-silver/                            (SCML.Inventory · SCML.Lot · SCML.Movement · SCML.Receipt)
      merml-silver/                           (MERML.Markdown · MERML.Elasticity · MERML.Competitor · MERML.Promotion)
      proml-silver/                           (PROML.Pricing · PROML.DiscountRule)
      crmml-silver/                           (CRMML.Customer · CRMML.Loyalty · CRMML.Interaction)
    OneLake security: roles defined here, propagate via shortcut

  rc-e2e-03/                                  (per-service consumer)
    Lakehouses/
      service-bronze/                         (Bronze landing for RC-E2E-03 sources)
      service-gold/                           (Gold marts: g_excursion_decision_panel etc.)
    OneLake shortcuts → rc-canonical Silver tables (read-only consumer)

  rc-e2e-04/                                  (per-service consumer)
    same pattern; shortcuts into rc-canonical for shared Silver entities

  ...one workspace per featured RC scenario...

This is the recommended baseline architecture for OneLake security at scale. Three properties matter:

  1. Single source of security. Roles defined on the primary workspace propagate via shortcuts — there is no "secondary" copy of the security policy that can drift.
  2. Cross-service composition without cross-service writes. RC-E2E-03's cold-chain agents read SCML.Lot via a shortcut into rc-canonical (where RC-E2E-09 owns Silver writes per §17.4). RC-E2E-09's MCP tool surface remains the public interface; the shortcut is the data path. No service writes Silver tables it doesn't own.
  3. Scale. Per Microsoft's GA limit (Jan 2026), Fabric workspace identities scale to 10,000 per tenant — well beyond what a typical APEX-M deployment needs, but the headroom matters for enterprise rollouts.

The wizard's render endpoint emits Bicep that creates the primary workspace first, then per-service workspaces with shortcuts wired into the primary. Operators do not provision workspaces by hand. See apex-m/infra/bicep/platform/fabric.bicep (TBD — Phase I.4 follow-up).

Best Practice — primary-workspace ownership

Use the same Fabric capacity owner for the primary workspace and every consumer workspace under it. Mixed-owner setups break user identity mode for cross-workspace reads — the consumer workspace's SQL analytics endpoint cannot run in user identity mode if its owner differs from the producer's.

Chapter 2

Canonical Schema Families

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Inventories the APEX schema families that all 38 Services draw from.
  • Maps each family to the practices and Services that use it.
  • Establishes naming conventions and entity-relationship principles that propagate through Part VI.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • ~14 canonical schema families serve all 38 Services across 7 practices — domain-keyed not industry-keyed.
  • Naming is uniform: Bronze br_*, Silver schema-prefixed PascalCase, Gold g_*; three timestamps everywhere (event_ts · ingest_ts · silver_ts).
  • Cross-schema joins materialize in Silver→Gold pipelines; agents see Gold, never multi-table joins.

2.1 The Schema Families

APEX defines roughly a dozen canonical schema families. Each family corresponds to a functional domain (supply chain, merchandising, pricing, electronic health records, billing & revenue, etc.) and is shared by every Service in any practice that touches that domain. The families are not industry-specific in the conventional sense; SCML serves Retail's RC-E2E-05 (on-shelf availability) and Energy's ER-OG-07 (pipeline leak inventory) and Industrial's ICE-Aftermarket-05 (predictive parts replenishment).

FamilyDomainPracticesHeadline entities
SCMLSupply ChainRC · ER · AXLE · ICEInventory · Lot · Movement · Receipt · Shipment
MERMLMerchandisingRC · ICEMarkdown · Elasticity · Competitor · Promotion · Assortment
PROMLPricingRC · TH · TMTPricing · PriceList · DiscountRule · Yield
EHRMLElectronic Health RecordsHLSEncounter · Diagnosis · Procedure · Medication · LabResult
CMSMLClaims · Member · SubmissionHLSClaim · Member · Authorization · Denial · Appeal
WLMLWarranty LifecycleAXLE · ICEWarranty · Claim · Recovery · SupplierResponsibility

NETMLNetwork & InfrastructureER · TMTAsset · Outage · Topology · TicketEvent
BRMMLBilling · Revenue · MediationTMTUsageEvent · RatedTransaction · Invoice · Variance
GLMLGeneral LedgerTMT · cross-cuttingAccount · Posting · TrialBalance · Adjustment
CRMMLCustomer RelationshipRC · TH · TMT · cross-cuttingCustomer · Interaction · Loyalty · Sentiment
ECRMLEnvironmental · Compliance · RegulatoryER · HLSPermit · Emission · Violation · AuditTrail

Appendix A indexes every entity referenced in this book with its family, its source SOR systems, and its consuming Services.

2.2 Naming and Layout Conventions

  • Schema name. Uppercase prefix (3–5 chars) ending in ML for "model layer." SCML, MERML, PROML, EHRML, etc.
  • Entity name. PascalCase, singular: SCML.Lot, MERML.Markdown. Plural-by-default lakehouse table names use lowercase snake_case: scml_lots, merml_markdowns.
  • Bronze table name. br_ prefix + source-system identifier + entity hint: br_pos_transactions, br_sap_inventory_movements.
  • Gold mart name. g_ prefix + question shape: g_markdown_proposal_basis, g_kpi_rc_e2e_03_daily.
  • Foreign keys. APEX-canonical IDs prefixed by entity. customer_id, sku_id, store_id. Source-system IDs preserved as src_*_id for Bronze→Silver lineage.
  • Time fields. event_ts (when it actually happened) vs ingest_ts (when APEX received it) vs silver_ts (when Silver materialized). Always preserve all three.

2.3 Cross-Schema Relationships

The schemas are not islands. RC-E2E-03 reads SCML.Inventory, SCML.Lot, MERML.Markdown, MERML.Elasticity, MERML.Competitor — three different schemas in one Service. Cross-schema joins happen in Silver-to-Gold materialization, not in agent queries.

The relationship graph is documented in Appendix E (Cross-Service Composition Map). The most common joins:

  • SCML.Inventory ⨝ MERML.Markdown on (sku_id, store_id, event_ts) — used in every RC pricing/markdown Service.
  • EHRML.Encounter ⨝ CMSML.Claim on (member_id, encounter_id) — used in every HLS denial-prevention or care-gap Service.
  • WLML.Warranty ⨝ QMSML.Defect on (equipment_id, claim_ts) — used in AXLE / ICE warranty and quality Services.
  • BRMML.UsageEvent ⨝ BRMML.RatedTransaction on (usage_id) with reconciliation against GLML.Posting — TMT-TEL-RAA-04 revenue assurance.

Key Takeaways

  • ~14 canonical schema families serve all 38 Services across 7 practices. SCML, MERML, PROML, EHRML, and the rest are domain-keyed, not industry-keyed.
  • Naming conventions are uniform: Bronze br_, Silver schema-prefixed PascalCase, Gold g_, three timestamps preserved everywhere.
  • Cross-schema joins materialize in Silver→Gold pipelines, not in agent queries; agents see Gold marts, never multi-table joins.
Chapter 3

MCP and the Service Tool Catalog

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Explains how Model Context Protocol (MCP) is the contract between agents and Gold-tier data.
  • Maps the per-Service MCP tool catalog to the Gold marts that back each tool.
  • Walks the access boundary — every MCP call is a managed-identity-authenticated query against Microsoft Fabric Warehouse.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • MCP is a typed RPC contract between agents and Gold; agents call MCP tools, MCP queries Microsoft Fabric Warehouse under mi-apex-merch-mcp.
  • ~165 tools across the 38-Service catalog; Service-scoped role assignments make cross-Service tool access structurally impossible from the wrong agent.
  • Every MCP call writes a LEDGER row with a response row hash; agents verify the hash before reasoning. No agent talks to Fabric directly.

3.1 What MCP Is, Operationally

The Model Context Protocol is a typed RPC contract between an agent and a tool host. Each tool has a name, a typed input schema, a typed output schema, and a description string the agent uses for tool selection. Agents call MCP tools instead of writing SQL because MCP gives auditable, scoped, type-safe access — the agent cannot "free-text" a query that pulls more data than the tool definition allows.

In APEX, the MCP host runs as a sidecar to the agent's Container App or as a shared Container App per Service. The host authenticates as the Layer 1 mi-apex-merch-mcp user-assigned managed identity, and every tool's underlying query runs against Microsoft Fabric Warehouse (Gold) under that identity's role assignments.

JSON — example MCP tool definition (RC-E2E-03)
{
  "name": "rc_e2e_03.get_excursion_decision_panel",
  "description": "Fetch the per-event decision panel for a cold-chain excursion: SKU lot, current inventory position, downstream risk, recent markdown history.",
  "input_schema": {
    "type": "object",
    "properties": {
      "store_id":     { "type": "string", "pattern": "^[A-Z0-9-]{3,32}$" },
      "excursion_id": { "type": "string", "format": "uuid" }
    },
    "required": ["store_id", "excursion_id"]
  },
  "output_schema": {
    "type": "object",
    "properties": {
      "lot_provenance":    { "$ref": "#/definitions/SCMLLot" },
      "inventory_at_risk": { "type": "number" },
      "markdown_history":  { "type": "array", "items": { "$ref": "#/definitions/MERMLMarkdown" } },
      "row_hash":          { "type": "string", "pattern": "^sha256:[0-9a-f]{64}$" }
    }
  },
  "service_code": "RC-E2E-03",
  "gold_mart":    "g_excursion_decision_panel",
  "audit": {
    "ledger_emit_on_call":   true,
    "row_hash_in_response":  true
  }
}

Every tool definition is per-Service. rc_e2e_03.get_excursion_decision_panel belongs to RC-E2E-03 and only to RC-E2E-03; HLS-E2E-04 has its own tools that read EHRML/CMSML Gold marts under HLS-E2E-04's name. The Layer 1 mi-apex-merch-mcp identity is granted scoped Microsoft.Authorization role assignments per Service, so cross-Service tool access is structurally impossible from the wrong agent.

3.2 The Tool Catalog Across the Catalog

Across all 38 Services, the APEX tool catalog runs to roughly 165 tools (Sellers Guide §5.3.4 cites that number). The distribution is uneven by design — pricing-rich Services (RC-E2E-03, TH-HOT-03, TMT-TEL-RAA-04) have more tools because their Gold marts are richer; simpler Services have fewer.

PracticeApprox toolsTool naming pattern
RC~30rc_e2e_03.*, rc_e2e_04.*, etc.
HLS~25hls_e2e_02.*, hls_e2e_04.*, hls_ls_03.*, etc.
ER~22er_og_*.*, er_pu_*.*, er_mn_*.*
AXLE~20axle_connected_factory_*.*, axle_supply_*.*, etc.
TH~22th_air_*.*, th_hot_*.*
TMT~26tmt_med_*.*, tmt_tel_*.*, tmt_tec_*.*
ICE~20ice_aftermarket_*.*, ice_connected_*.*, ice_eaas_*.*

Appendix B is the full MCP tool catalog with name, input/output schema reference, backing Gold mart, and consuming Services.

3.3 The MCP Host and the Microsoft Platform

The MCP host is a small FastAPI app (Python in the MVP) that registers tool definitions at startup and dispatches incoming MCP requests to backing Microsoft Fabric Warehouse queries. It runs as a Container App in the same Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments environment as the agents.

The data-plane path:

  1. Agent calls MCP host (HTTP, JSON-RPC framed).
  2. MCP host authenticates as mi-apex-merch-mcp via Microsoft Entra ID.
  3. MCP host issues a parameterized T-SQL query against Microsoft Fabric Warehouse (Gold mart).
  4. MCP host computes the response row hash (SHA-256 over canonical JSON), emits a LEDGER row recording (tool name, agent, input args, output row hash), returns the response.
  5. Agent reads the response, asserts the row hash matches what LEDGER recorded, proceeds with reasoning.
Best Practice — Agents check the row hash

The MCP response includes the row hash and LEDGER also has the row hash. The agent verifies they match before using the data. This catches a category of bugs (cache staleness, mid-flight mart rebuilds) early, while the LEDGER row is still warm. It is cheap to write the check; it pays for itself in the first cross-region post-mortem.

3.4 Why Agents Don't Talk to Microsoft Fabric Directly

An obvious shortcut: skip the MCP host, give the agent the Microsoft Fabric Warehouse connection string and let it write SQL. It works on day one and breaks on day ninety in three ways.

  • Audit becomes free-form. If an agent writes its own SQL, the LEDGER row records "agent ran a query" but the query text is the audit. Reconstruction is by string parsing. With MCP, the audit row is "agent called rc_e2e_03.get_excursion_decision_panel(store_id=BIGBOX-100, excursion_id=…)"; the call signature is structured.
  • Scope creep. An agent that can write SQL can read any Gold mart; an agent that calls MCP can only call the tools registered for its Service. The MCP catalog is the access policy.
  • Schema drift. An agent's SQL is fixed at prompt-write time; a MCP tool definition is versioned and updated centrally. When a Gold mart is reshaped, you reshape one MCP tool; the agents that call it keep working.
ADR · architecture decision
ADR-003-01 · 2026-04-18 · accepted

Decision: Agents access Gold-tier data only through MCP tools, never via direct Microsoft Fabric Warehouse SQL.

Why: The three reasons above (audit · scope · drift) compound rather than add. Free-form SQL inflates audit cost (string-parse reconstruction), inflates governance cost (every Gold mart is now in the access policy surface), and inflates maintenance cost (every Gold-mart shape change ripples into every consuming agent's prompt). MCP collapses all three to a single typed-RPC contract managed centrally.

Trade-off rejected: Agents authoring SQL with sandboxed Microsoft Entra ID identity scoping. Microsoft Entra ID conditional-access policies could enforce row-level scope; the agent's reasoning could include why it queried what it queried. But it doesn't solve schema drift, and the audit-narrative remains free-form. The cost of MCP is one extra network hop per tool call (~30ms); the benefits are structural.

Caveat: Direct Fabric access remains available for data engineers running Microsoft Fabric notebooks for Silver builds and Gold materialization (§8). The rule applies to agent runtime, not to the data-engineering plane.

Key Takeaways

  • MCP is a typed RPC contract between agents and Gold-tier data; agents call MCP tools, MCP queries Microsoft Fabric Warehouse under a Service-scoped managed identity.
  • Per-Service tool catalogs (~165 tools across the catalog); Layer 1 mi-apex-merch-mcp identity gets scoped role assignments so cross-Service tool access is structurally impossible.
  • Every MCP call writes a LEDGER row with a response row hash; agents verify the hash before reasoning over the data.
  • Don't let agents talk to Microsoft Fabric directly — audit, scope, and schema versioning all break.
Part II

Bronze Tier — Client Data Sources

Bronze is where client systems meet APEX. Every Service in the catalog draws on a specific set of source-of-record systems — point-of-sale, ERP, EHR, CRM, IoT telemetry, billing mediation, or general ledger — and lands raw rows into a Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse or a Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence eventstream. This part is the on-ramp: which SOR systems feed which Services, how Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence handles the streaming sources, what the ingestion + tokenization pipeline looks like, and how PII gets classified at the moment it crosses the boundary.

Chapters in this Part

  • Chapter 4Source-of-Record Inventory by Industry
  • Chapter 5Real-Time Hub Pattern (Microsoft Fabric RTI)
  • Chapter 6Ingestion and Tokenization
  • Chapter 7PII Classification and pii-unlock
Chapter 4

Source-of-Record Inventory by Industry

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Inventories the SOR systems that feed each practice and points each system at the schema family it lands into.
  • Explains the SOR-to-Bronze contract: who owns the connection, who owns the data, what gets persisted.
  • Maps the major SOR vendors (SAP, Oracle, Epic, Salesforce, Manhattan, Workday, etc.) to their Microsoft Fabric ingestion path.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • Five SOR categories — operational · master/ERP · customer/member · asset/IoT · external — repeat across all 7 practices.
  • Four Microsoft Fabric ingestion paths cover every SOR shape: Mirroring · Eventstream · Pipeline · OneLake Shortcut.
  • sor-connections.yaml per Service is the audit-grade record consumed by Microsoft Purview and the MCP catalog.

4.1 The SOR Pattern Across the Catalog

Every Service in the 38-Service catalog reads from a small number of client SOR systems. The systems differ by practice, but the categories repeat: operational transaction system (POS, EHR, billing mediation), master-data system (ERP, MDM), customer-or-member system (CRM, payer member master), asset/IoT telemetry (refrigeration, network, manufacturing line), and external feed (competitor pricing, weather, regulatory). RC-E2E-03 hits four of these categories; HLS-E2E-04 hits three; ER-OG-07 hits two with high IoT volume.

The SOR list per practice:

4.2 SOR-to-Bronze Connection Pattern

For each SOR, three pieces have to be settled before any Bronze row lands:

  1. Who owns the connection. The client owns the SOR; APEX owns the Bronze landing zone. The connection itself — a Microsoft Fabric Mirroring link, a Microsoft.DataFactory pipeline, an Eventstream connector — is deployed inside the client's Microsoft platform tenant under the Layer 1 substrate, but it reaches into client systems with client credentials. Independence-compliant.
  2. What gets persisted. Always the raw row, in the SOR's native schema, with no enrichment. The Bronze contract from §1.3.1 holds without exception.
  3. How often it lands. Three cadences are common. Streaming (POS, refrigeration, network probes) lands continuously through Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence Eventstream. Near-real-time (1–15 min batches via change data capture) lands through Microsoft Fabric Mirroring or Microsoft.DataFactory. Daily / weekly (master data, competitor feeds, regulatory rate sheets) lands through scheduled Microsoft.DataFactory pipelines.

4.3 Microsoft Fabric Ingestion Paths

Microsoft Fabric exposes four ingestion paths into a Lakehouse, each appropriate for a different SOR shape:

PathMicrosoft Fabric componentSOR shapeTypical APEX use
MirroringMicrosoft Fabric Mirrored DatabaseDatabase SOR with CDC support (Azure SQL, Cosmos DB, Snowflake, Databricks)Mirror SAP S/4HANA tables · payer claims engines · PMS
EventstreamMicrosoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence EventstreamStreaming events (Kafka, Event Hubs, IoT Hub)POS terminal events · refrigeration telemetry · network probes
PipelineMicrosoft Fabric Data Pipeline (Microsoft.DataFactory under the hood)Files, REST APIs, scheduled extractsCompetitor pricing CSVs · daily ERP exports · regulatory rate sheets
ShortcutOneLake Shortcut (Microsoft Fabric)Existing data in another Fabric workspace, ADLS Gen2, S3, GCSCross-tenant Deloitte reference data · shared synthetic fixtures (Lab only)

Most Services use a mix. RC-E2E-03's Bronze layer combines an Eventstream (refrigeration), a Mirrored Database (SAP S/4HANA inventory), and a Pipeline (competitor pricing daily CSV). The medallion contract is uniform regardless of path; the path is just an ingestion choice.

4.4 The SOR Connection Inventory File

For every Service onboarded into a tenant, the delivery team commits a sor-connections.yaml alongside the Service Bicep. This file is the audit-grade record of which client systems feed the Service.

YAML — sor-connections.yaml example (RC-E2E-03)
service_code: RC-E2E-03
tenant_code:  contoso-na
sor_connections:

  - sor_id:        contoso-pos
    description:   "Point-of-sale events from 2,400 stores · NCR Aloha"
    landing_path:  bronze/br_pos_transactions
    fabric_component: Eventstream
    eventstream_name: es-contoso-pos
    cadence:       streaming
    schema_family: SCML
    pii_classification: tier-3   # see §7
    owner:         contoso-store-ops

  - sor_id:        contoso-sap-s4
    description:   "SAP S/4HANA inventory + master data"
    landing_path:  bronze/br_erp_inventory
    fabric_component: MirroredDatabase
    cdc_source:    "SAP S/4HANA · /APEX/inventory_position · CDC"
    cadence:       near-real-time
    schema_family: SCML
    pii_classification: tier-2

  - sor_id:        contoso-refrigeration
    description:   "Walk-in refrigeration telemetry · 8,400 sensors"
    landing_path:  bronze/br_refrigeration_telemetry
    fabric_component: Eventstream
    eventstream_name: es-contoso-refrigeration
    cadence:       streaming
    schema_family: SCML
    pii_classification: tier-1

  - sor_id:        nielsen-competitor-pricing
    description:   "Daily competitor pricing feed · top-12 competitor set"
    landing_path:  bronze/br_competitor_pricing
    fabric_component: DataPipeline
    schedule:      "0 5 * * *"  # daily 05:00
    cadence:       daily
    schema_family: MERML
    pii_classification: tier-1
    owner:         contoso-merchandising

The MCP catalog editor and the Microsoft Purview catalog both consume this file. The first registers MCP tools that depend on these Bronze landings. The second registers Purview assets so lineage shows up before any Silver build runs.

Key Takeaways

  • Five SOR categories — operational, master/ERP, customer/member, asset/IoT, external — repeat across all 7 practices with practice-specific vendors.
  • SOR connections live in the client tenant under Layer 1 substrate and reach into client systems with client credentials; Independence-compliant by construction.
  • Four Microsoft Fabric ingestion paths (Mirroring, Eventstream, Pipeline, Shortcut) cover every SOR shape; a Service typically uses two or three.
  • sor-connections.yaml is the audit-grade record per Service — Microsoft Purview and the MCP catalog both consume it.
Chapter 5

Real-Time Hub Pattern · Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence (RTI) components used as streaming Bronze.
  • Maps Eventstream → KQL DB → Lakehouse for the streaming Services in the catalog.
  • Shows how the W2-pilot trigger pattern ("Excursion Event Fires · DAG Orchestrator") materializes operationally.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • Microsoft Fabric RTI (Eventstream + KQL DB + Eventhouse) is the streaming-Bronze substrate for any event-shaped source.
  • Eventstream routes events to KQL DB (streaming Bronze) and Lakehouse (batch Silver path) — same events, two consumers.
  • KQL update-policy rules detect trigger conditions; the orchestrator subscribes via Event Grid. Trigger lives in data, not in agent code.

5.1 The RTI Components

Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence is the streaming-data substrate inside Microsoft Fabric. For APEX, three RTI components matter:

  • Eventstream (Microsoft.Fabric/eventstreams) — the entry point. Receives events from Azure Event Hubs, Azure IoT Hub, Kafka, Confluent Cloud, or AWS Kinesis. Routes events to one or more sinks.
  • KQL Database (Microsoft.Fabric/kqldatabases) — the streaming-Bronze store. Holds events with high ingestion rate and fast query (KQL) access. Acts as Bronze for any Service whose source is event-shaped.
  • Eventhouse — the workspace container for KQL DBs in Fabric. Logical grouping per Service or per tenant.

5.2 The Pattern

For every streaming Service in the catalog (RC-E2E-03 cold-chain, ER-OG-07 pipeline leak, TMT-TEL-NET-02 network fault prediction, ICE-Connected-06 dealer telemetry, etc.), the pattern is:

  1. Client streaming SOR (POS, IoT, network probe) emits events to an event broker (Azure Event Hubs, Kafka, etc.). The broker is in the client tenant; APEX does not own it.
  2. Microsoft Fabric Eventstream consumes the broker, routing events to two sinks:
    • KQL DB as streaming Bronze — full-fidelity event store with high cardinality, queryable in KQL.
    • Lakehouse via the Eventstream-to-Lakehouse sink — same events, written as Delta Parquet for batch Silver builds.
  3. An Eventstream rule (KQL-language predicate) detects the trigger condition (e.g., temperature exceeds threshold for >15 minutes) and emits a derived event to a downstream Eventstream sink — typically Azure Service Bus or another Eventstream feeding the orchestrator.
  4. Azure Logic Apps Hybrid (or n8n on the laptop) receives the trigger and starts the W2-pilot DAG.
  5. The DAG calls agents in sequence, each agent reading Gold via MCP, each writing LEDGER rows.

5.3 The Excursion-Trigger Example

RC-E2E-03's "Cold-chain excursion mid-shift" scenario exercises the pattern end to end. The event chain:

KQL — Eventstream rule (excursion detection)
// Eventstream rule: emit an excursion event when a sensor exceeds threshold
// for >= 15 minutes within a single store-zone.
br_refrigeration_telemetry
| where temperature_c > threshold_c
| extend window = bin(event_ts, 1m)
| summarize
    breach_minutes = count(),
    first_breach   = min(event_ts),
    last_breach    = max(event_ts),
    peak_temp      = max(temperature_c)
    by store_id, zone_id, lot_id, hin(event_ts, 30m)
| where breach_minutes >= 15
| project
    excursion_id   = strcat(store_id, "-", zone_id, "-", format_datetime(first_breach, "yyyyMMddHHmm")),
    store_id, zone_id, lot_id,
    duration_min   = breach_minutes,
    peak_temp,
    fired_ts       = now()

This rule is registered as an Eventstream KQL transformation. When it fires, it emits to a downstream Eventstream that Azure Logic Apps Hybrid subscribes to (via Event Grid). Logic Apps starts the workflow, calls The Analyst via HTTP with the excursion payload, and the W2-pilot DAG runs.

Note — Why the rule is at Eventstream, not in the agent

The rule is the trigger. The agent is the decision. Putting the trigger in Eventstream keeps three properties: (1) the agent only runs when something is worth deciding (cost-efficient on Container Apps Consumption profile); (2) the trigger logic is declarative KQL the data team can reason about; (3) the trigger event itself is a row in the KQL DB and on LEDGER, so audit reconstruction starts at the firing condition, not the agent invocation.

5.4 Streaming-Bronze vs Batch-Bronze in the Same Service

RC-E2E-03 has both streaming Bronze (refrigeration telemetry, POS) and batch Bronze (ERP inventory snapshots, daily competitor pricing). Silver builds run on a hybrid cadence — a continuous 5-minute window for the streaming side, joined against the most recent daily snapshot of the batch side. The Gold mart materializes after both sides are fresh.

This hybrid cadence is the norm, not the exception. About 18 of the 38 Services have at least one streaming source plus at least one batch source. The orchestrator does not see the cadence difference; it just calls MCP tools, and the MCP tools query Gold marts that are fresh.

Key Takeaways

  • Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence (Eventstream + KQL DB + Eventhouse) is the streaming-Bronze substrate for any Service whose source is event-shaped.
  • Eventstream routes events to both KQL DB (streaming Bronze) and Lakehouse (batch Silver build path) — same events, two consumers.
  • Eventstream KQL rules detect trigger conditions and emit derived events that orchestrators (Logic Apps Hybrid · n8n) subscribe to. The trigger lives in data, not in agent code.
  • Hybrid streaming-plus-batch is the norm: ~18 of 38 Services combine both.
Chapter 6

Ingestion and Tokenization

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the ingestion pipeline from SOR to Bronze landing, with Microsoft Fabric component-level detail.
  • Explains tokenization at ingestion time: the deterministic-token pattern that lets Silver/Gold operate on tokens, not raw PII.
  • Maps the tokenization service to its Microsoft.KeyVault HSM root of trust.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • Five-stage ingest: connect · tokenize · land · stamp · emit. Tokenize human-recognizable PII at ingest; opaque join keys pass through.
  • HMAC-SHA256 with Microsoft.KeyVault HSM key — same input → same token; original unrecoverable without HSM access.
  • mi-apex-merch-ingest identity has minimum scope: write Bronze · use tokenization key · read SOR connections · nothing more.

6.1 The Ingestion Pipeline, in Stages

For any single SOR-to-Bronze flow:

  1. Connect. Microsoft Fabric component (Eventstream, Mirrored Database, Pipeline) reaches the SOR with client credentials.
  2. Tokenize. If the source row contains PII fields (per the sor-connections.yaml classification), each PII field is replaced by a deterministic token before persistence. The original value never lands in Bronze.
  3. Land. Tokenized row written to Bronze: KQL DB (streaming) or Lakehouse Delta (batch).
  4. Stamp. Three timestamps and the Microsoft Purview lineage tag added.
  5. Emit. If the row triggers a downstream Eventstream rule, the rule fires and the orchestrator gets notified.

6.2 The Tokenization Pattern

Tokenization replaces a PII value with a deterministic token derived from the value plus a per-tenant secret key held in Microsoft.KeyVault Premium HSM. The same input produces the same token (so joins still work), but the original value is unrecoverable from the token without HSM access.

PYTHON — tokenization sketch
import hmac, hashlib
from azure.identity import DefaultAzureCredential
from azure.keyvault.keys import KeyClient
from azure.keyvault.keys.crypto import CryptographyClient, EncryptionAlgorithm

# Init once per pipeline; cached cryptography client, MI-authenticated.
credential = DefaultAzureCredential()  # mi-apex-merch-ingest
kv_uri     = os.environ["KEYVAULT_URI"]
key_client = KeyClient(vault_url=kv_uri, credential=credential)
token_key  = key_client.get_key("apex-tokenization-key")  # AES-256-HSM
crypto     = CryptographyClient(token_key, credential=credential)

def tokenize_pii(field_name: str, raw_value: str) -> str:
    """Produce a deterministic token for raw_value, scoped by field_name."""
    if raw_value is None or raw_value == "":
        return raw_value
    msg = f"{field_name}:{raw_value}".encode("utf-8")
    # HMAC-SHA256 with HSM-held key — the digest is the token.
    digest = crypto.sign(SignatureAlgorithm.hs256, hashlib.sha256(msg).digest())
    return f"tok-{digest.signature.hex()[:24]}"

The function runs inside the Microsoft Fabric Pipeline (or the Eventstream transformation) under the mi-apex-merch-ingest identity from Layer 1. The HSM key never leaves the HSM; the tokens are deterministic and joinable across rows and across Services.

Warning — Don't tokenize foreign keys you need to join

The pattern looks attractive for every "ID-shaped" field, but if a field is a join key for downstream Services, tokenizing it here breaks cross-Service joins. The rule of thumb: tokenize anything that is human-recognizable PII (name, email, phone, address). Don't tokenize internal opaque IDs (customer_id, member_id, equipment_serial); they are already identifier surrogates and should join straight through.

6.3 The Ingest Identity

Layer 1 provisions four user-assigned managed identities, one of which is mi-apex-merch-ingest. This is the identity Microsoft Fabric Pipelines and Eventstream transformations authenticate as. It is granted:

  • Storage Blob Data Contributor on the per-Service Bronze landing storage account (or Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse).
  • Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse Writer on the per-Service workspace.
  • Key Vault Crypto User on the tenant tokenization key (AES-256-HSM).
  • Reader on the SOR connection objects (the connection itself stays under client ops).

It is not granted access to the LEDGER, to the runtime managed identity scope, or to the PII unlock key. Ingest's job is one-directional: read from SOR (with client-supplied credentials), tokenize, write to Bronze. Anything beyond that is a different identity's responsibility.

6.4 Microsoft.DataFactory Pipeline Patterns

For batch SOR sources, the Microsoft Fabric Data Pipeline activity used most often is Copy Data, augmented by a Notebook activity that runs the tokenization on the landed rows before they are committed to Bronze.

PIPELINE — daily competitor pricing ingest (sketch)
1. Copy Data:    nielsen-pricing-feed → staging/raw/{ds}/competitor_pricing_{ds}.csv
2. Notebook:     run tokenization notebook against staging/raw/{ds}/...
                 writes Delta to bronze/br_competitor_pricing partitioned by date
3. Notebook:     emit Microsoft Purview lineage event
4. Trigger:      kick off Silver-build pipeline for MERML.Competitor

The pipeline is parameterized by date ({ds} in Microsoft Fabric expression language) and runs on a Microsoft.Fabric/dataPipelines-equivalent schedule. Failures route to a Microsoft Sentinel alert via Eventstream → Microsoft Sentinel connector.

Key Takeaways

  • Ingestion is five stages: connect, tokenize, land, stamp, emit. Tokenization is non-negotiable for human-recognizable PII; opaque join keys pass through.
  • Tokenization uses HMAC-SHA256 with a Microsoft.KeyVault HSM-held key (AES-256-HSM); same input → same token, original value unrecoverable without HSM access.
  • The mi-apex-merch-ingest identity has the minimum scope to do its job — write to Bronze, use the tokenization key, read SOR connections — and nothing more.
  • Microsoft Fabric Pipelines + Notebook activities cover batch ingestion; Eventstream transformations cover streaming.
Chapter 7

PII Classification and the pii-unlock Identity

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Establishes the four-tier PII classification scheme APEX uses on every Bronze field.
  • Explains the mi-apex-merch-pii-unlock identity — why it exists, when it is used, how every use is auditable.
  • Walks the just-in-time unlock motion that lets HITL approvers see re-identified data without standing privilege.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • Four PII tiers — Public · Internal · Confidential PII · Restricted PHI/regulated. Tier 3+ tokenized at ingest; Tier 4 adds dual-control unlock.
  • mi-apex-merch-pii-unlock is no-standing-privilege; acquired JIT at the HITL approval moment; every acquisition LEDGER-attributed.
  • Tokenization key (HMAC, ingest-side) and unwrap key (RSA-HSM, JIT) are intentionally separate — different attack surfaces, different lifetimes.

7.1 The Four Tiers

TierField examplesBronze handlingWho can read
Tier 1 — Publicstore_id · sku_id · public-list price · weather observationsStored as-isAll identities
Tier 2 — Internalemployee_id · internal cost · supplier_code · sensor_idStored as-isService identities only
Tier 3 — Confidential PIIcustomer email · phone · loyalty_id · IP address · device fingerprintTokenized at ingestTokenized form to all; raw form requires pii-unlock + LEDGER row
Tier 4 — Restricted PHI / regulated PIISSN · DOB · clinical diagnosis · patient name · payment card number · drivers-license numberTokenized at ingest with separate per-field key · stricter rotationTokenized form only inside Service runtime; raw form gated by HITL approval + dual-control + LEDGER

The classification is set in sor-connections.yaml at field level (or at Bronze-table level if a coarse default is sufficient). Microsoft Purview consumes the classification and applies sensitivity labels to the lineage view.

7.2 The pii-unlock Identity

The fourth Layer 1 user-assigned managed identity, mi-apex-merch-pii-unlock, is intentionally not bound at deployment time to any agent Container App. It lives in Microsoft Entra ID with no standing privileges other than:

  • Key Vault Crypto User on the per-tenant PII-unwrap key (separate from the tokenization key — a different RSA-HSM key in the same Microsoft.KeyVault Premium vault).
  • Storage Blob Data Reader on the per-Service silver layer where re-identification mappings live (encrypted).

An agent that needs to surface real PII to a HITL approver acquires a Microsoft Entra ID token for this identity just-in-time using acquireTokenForUserAssignedIdentity. The acquisition itself writes a LEDGER row before the unlock fires. The token is short-lived (minutes), the unlock is for a single record, and the access window closes when the HITL decision is recorded.

Independence — No standing privilege over PII

The pii-unlock identity is never standing on a Container App. Every replica that started without it cannot escalate; every just-in-time acquisition is audit-attributed. This is the structural enforcement that an APEX deployment running in a regulated client tenant (HLS, financial services, public sector) cannot inadvertently expose PII via prompt injection or agent hallucination — the bytes simply aren't reachable to the agent process unless an audited motion has fired.

7.3 The Just-in-Time Unlock Motion

For RC-E2E-04 (Loyalty Churn) — a Service that touches Tier 3 PII (customer email, loyalty_id) — the motion when a HITL approver wants to see a real customer:

  1. Agent prepares a winback proposal using tokenized customer rows. Decision-grade fact: "this tokenized customer at risk of churn worth $X."
  2. Agent emits a HITL adaptive card to the approver via Microsoft Teams. Card carries the tokenized identifier, not the email.
  3. Approver clicks "see customer details" in the card — UI calls back into the Service's MCP host.
  4. MCP host writes a LEDGER row: pii_unlock_requested with (tokenized id, requesting approver, business reason).
  5. MCP host acquires a mi-apex-merch-pii-unlock token for the next ~5 minutes.
  6. MCP host queries the encrypted re-identification mapping under that token, decrypts the email/loyalty_id for the single tokenized id.
  7. Decrypted values returned to the UI; immediately a second LEDGER row records pii_unlock_completed.
  8. The token expires; the approver decision (apply / decline winback) is captured against the LEDGER row pair.

For Tier 4 PHI, the motion adds a dual-control step: the unlock requires two distinct approver identities to consent before the unwrap key issues a token. This is a Microsoft.KeyVault HSM access policy expressed as a JIT approval workflow.

7.4 Why the Tokenization Key and the Unwrap Key Are Different

One key for both would simplify the architecture by one HSM key. It would also collapse two distinct attack surfaces into one. The tokenization key is used at ingest by the ingest identity on every row; if that key is compromised, the attacker can produce tokens but cannot reverse them (HMAC is one-way). The unwrap key is used at HITL approval time by the pii-unlock identity for one record; it is the only thing that can re-identify. Different lifetimes, different blast radii, different rotation cadences. Two keys.

ADR · architecture decision
ADR-007-01 · 2026-04-22 · accepted

Decision: Tokenization key (HMAC-SHA256, ingest-side) and unwrap key (RSA-HSM, JIT) are intentionally separate Microsoft.KeyVault HSM keys.

Why: Compromise containment. The tokenization key sees every Bronze row at ingest; high-throughput, low blast-radius (HMAC is one-way · stolen key cannot reverse tokens). The unwrap key sees one row per HITL approval; low-throughput, catastrophic blast-radius if compromised (re-identifies all PII). Splitting puts the catastrophic key behind dual-control + JIT acquisition · §7.3, leaving the high-throughput key on a less-restrictive path.

Trade-off rejected: Single shared key with role-based gating. Easier rotation; one HSM rate-limit envelope; but a single compromise reverses every Bronze row ever ingested. Splitting is non-negotiable when the data is Tier-4 PHI.

Cross-volume: Cross-ref Deployment Guide §4.4 (HSM CMK provisioning) · §5 (per-identity Microsoft.KeyVault role assignments).

Key Takeaways

  • Four-tier PII classification (Public · Internal · Confidential PII · Restricted PHI) drives Bronze tokenization decisions and Microsoft Purview sensitivity labels.
  • mi-apex-merch-pii-unlock is a no-standing-privilege identity acquired just-in-time for HITL approval moments; every acquisition is LEDGER-attributed.
  • Tokenization key (HMAC, ingest-side) and unwrap key (RSA-HSM, JIT) are intentionally separate — different attack surfaces, different lifetimes.
  • Tier 4 unlocks add dual-control via Microsoft.KeyVault HSM access policy.
Part III

Silver Tier — Canonical Models

Silver is where APEX absorbs client variance. Bronze rows from forty different POS systems, six different EHRs, and a dozen ERPs all converge here onto the same canonical entity shapes — SCML, MERML, EHRML, the schema library. This part covers the conformance work that turns raw ingestion into a stable, joinable, lineage-tracked layer that Gold marts can build on without surprise. It is the layer most architects underestimate at engagement-start; it is also the layer that determines whether the next ten Service onboardings will be cheap or expensive.

Chapters in this Part

  • Chapter 8From Raw to Silver — The Conformance Layer
  • Chapter 9Schema Library Deep Dive
  • Chapter 10Quality, Lineage, Microsoft Purview
Chapter 8

From Raw to Silver — The Conformance Layer

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the four conformance moves that turn Bronze rows into canonical Silver entities.
  • Establishes the idempotent-rebuild contract that lets Silver be regenerated from Bronze at any time.
  • Maps the conformance work to Microsoft Fabric notebooks, Microsoft Fabric Warehouse, and the per-tenant Fabric workspace.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • Four conformance moves: type stabilization · naming/unit conformance · canonical-ID resolution · dedup+ordering — all in one Microsoft Fabric notebook per entity.
  • Idempotent rebuild is a hard contract: drop Silver, re-run, get byte-identical output. No out-of-band fixes.
  • Per-Service workspaces hold Silver; cross-Service entity reuse via OneLake Shortcuts + scoped role assignments.

8.1 The Four Conformance Moves

For any Bronze → Silver transformation, four moves do the conformance work. They are usually colocated in a single Microsoft Fabric notebook (PySpark or T-SQL) per Silver entity, but they are conceptually distinct and the notebook is structured by them.

8.1.1 Type stabilization

Bronze preserves whatever the SOR sent. SAP integers may be strings; Epic dates may be MM/DD/YYYY strings; refrigeration sensors may emit Celsius as floats with seven decimal places. Silver gets canonical types: int, decimal(18,4), timestamp. The type table is the schema family's contract.

8.1.2 Naming and unit conformance

SAP calls it MATNR; Oracle Retail calls it item_id; Toshiba POS calls it SKU_NUMBER. APEX SCML calls it sku_id. The renaming happens in Silver. Same for units: temperatures in Celsius, weights in kilograms, currencies stabilized to a per-tenant base currency with the FX rate as of event_ts.

8.1.3 Foreign-key resolution to canonical IDs

Bronze rows reference SOR-internal IDs. Silver entities reference APEX-canonical IDs. The mapping table — id_map — is itself a Silver-tier reference table maintained per tenant per SOR, and the Silver build joins through it. Source IDs are preserved as src_*_id columns for Bronze→Silver lineage but are not used as join keys downstream.

8.1.4 Deduplication and ordering

Streaming SOR sources sometimes deliver the same event twice. CDC sources sometimes produce out-of-order updates. Silver applies a deterministic deduplication rule (typically "max event_ts per natural key, ties broken by max ingest_ts") and emits a single canonical row per business fact. The Bronze layer keeps every duplicate; Silver keeps the last-writer-wins.

8.2 The Idempotent-Rebuild Contract

Silver is rebuildable from Bronze. Always. At any time. This is a contract, not an aspiration:

  • Drop the Silver entity table and run the build notebook. The result must be byte-identical to the running Silver.
  • The build notebook is parameterized by date range; partial rebuilds are routine.
  • No manual fixes, no out-of-band UPDATEs, no Silver rows that aren't traceable to Bronze rows.

Three reasons this matters. First, schema evolution: when MERML adds a column, the existing Silver gets dropped and rebuilt with the new column. Second, conformance fixes: when a unit-conversion bug is found, the fix lands in the build notebook and the back-population is a re-run, not a migration. Third, audit: regulators occasionally ask "show me what Silver looked like on day X with code version Y" — the rebuild contract is what makes that question answerable.

Warning — A Silver row that can't be regenerated is a bug

If you ever find a Silver row that the build notebook doesn't reproduce, that is the bug to investigate, not "an interesting historical artifact to keep." Either Bronze is missing data the original build saw, or the build notebook has drifted, or someone wrote into Silver out-of-band. Diagnose and either back-fill Bronze, fix the notebook, or remove the orphan. The contract has to hold.

8.3 Microsoft Fabric Components

Silver builds run on Microsoft Fabric notebooks against the same Lakehouse that holds Bronze. Three patterns dominate:

PatternMicrosoft Fabric componentWhen to use
PySpark notebookMicrosoft Fabric Spark · NotebookLarge fan-out joins, complex deduplication, ML-feature engineering inside the Silver build
T-SQL on WarehouseMicrosoft Fabric Warehouse · Stored ProcedureSchema-stable, foreign-key-rich entities; analyst-readable build logic
KQL update policyMicrosoft Fabric KQL DB · Update PolicyStreaming Silver from streaming Bronze (KQL-DB Bronze with continuous KQL-update-policy materialization)

The choice is per-entity, not per-Service. RC-E2E-03's Silver layer mixes all three: SCML.Inventory is a PySpark notebook, MERML.Markdown is a T-SQL stored procedure, and a streaming-derived SCML.LotMovement is a KQL update policy.

8.4 The Per-Service Silver Workspace

Silver entities live in Microsoft Fabric workspaces named per Service. rc-e2e-03 workspace holds the SCML/MERML Silver entities used by RC-E2E-03. hls-e2e-04 holds the EHRML/CMSML Silver entities used by HLS-E2E-04.

Where multiple Services use the same Silver entity, the entity lives in one Service's workspace and is exposed to others via Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse Shortcuts. SCML.Lot, owned by RC-E2E-09 (Product Tracking), is shortcut-exposed into RC-E2E-03's workspace where the cold-chain agents read it. The Layer 1 mi-apex-merch-runtime identity is granted Storage Blob Data Reader on the source-of-truth workspace by the owner Service's Bicep — this is the Chapter-9-of-Deployment-Guide cross-Service authorization pattern surfacing here.

Key Takeaways

  • Four conformance moves: type stabilization, naming/unit conformance, foreign-key resolution to canonical IDs, deduplication + ordering. They live in a per-entity Microsoft Fabric notebook.
  • Idempotent rebuild is a hard contract: drop Silver, re-run the build, get byte-identical output. No out-of-band fixes.
  • Silver builds use PySpark (Spark notebooks), T-SQL (Warehouse stored procs), or KQL update policies — choice per entity.
  • Per-Service Microsoft Fabric workspaces hold Silver; cross-Service entity reuse via Lakehouse Shortcuts and scoped role assignments.
Chapter 9

Schema Library Deep Dive

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks SCML, MERML, PROML, and EHRML entity-by-entity at the column level.
  • Shows the join shape across schemas that Gold marts build on.
  • Establishes the schema-evolution rules that keep the library stable across Service onboardings.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • SCML · MERML · EHRML · CMSML are concrete column-level entities — DDL-grade, not conceptual. Each is rendered as both DDL and ERD.
  • Every Silver row carries three timestamps · source-system lineage (src_*) · and where appropriate a decision_ledger_id closing the audit loop.
  • Schema evolution is additive by default; practice-specific needs add nullable columns, never fork the schema.
  • The §9.5 cross-schema view is the materialization spec — keep it synced with Gold-build notebooks for accurate Microsoft Purview lineage.

9.0 How to Read the ERDs in This Chapter

Each schema family in this chapter is rendered as both DDL and an entity-relationship diagram (ERD). The ERDs use a compact convention that propagates across all five diagrams in this chapter and Appendix A. Read this section once and the rest is legible at a glance.

9.1 SCML — Supply Chain Model Layer

SCML is the most heavily reused schema family in the catalog. RC, ER, AXLE, and ICE all draw on it. The five headline entities:

DDL — SCML.Inventory
CREATE TABLE SCML.Inventory (
    inventory_id        VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,    -- canonical inventory-position ID
    sku_id              VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,    -- canonical SKU
    location_id         VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,    -- store, warehouse, well, plant
    location_type       VARCHAR(16)  NOT NULL,    -- 'store' | 'warehouse' | 'plant' | ...
    on_hand_qty         DECIMAL(18,4) NOT NULL,
    on_order_qty        DECIMAL(18,4) NOT NULL,
    reserved_qty        DECIMAL(18,4) NOT NULL,
    available_qty       AS (on_hand_qty - reserved_qty) PERSISTED,
    unit_of_measure     VARCHAR(8)   NOT NULL,    -- 'EA' | 'CS' | 'LB' | 'KG' | ...
    valuation_currency  VARCHAR(3)   NOT NULL,    -- ISO 4217
    valuation_amount    DECIMAL(18,4),
    event_ts            TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,    -- when the SOR observed it
    ingest_ts           TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,    -- when APEX received it
    silver_ts           TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,    -- when Silver materialized
    src_system          VARCHAR(32)  NOT NULL,    -- 'sap-s4' | 'oracle-retail' | ...
    src_inventory_id    VARCHAR(64),              -- source-system ID for lineage
    PRIMARY KEY (inventory_id, event_ts)
);
DDL — SCML.Lot · FSMA-204 lot provenance
CREATE TABLE SCML.Lot (
    lot_id              VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,    -- canonical lot ID
    sku_id              VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,
    supplier_id         VARCHAR(64),
    production_date     DATE,
    expiration_date     DATE,
    fsma204_status      VARCHAR(16),              -- 'compliant' | 'pending' | 'recall'
    chain_of_custody    JSONB,                    -- ordered list of custody events
    cold_chain_history  JSONB,                    -- list of (sensor, ts, temp_c)
    event_ts            TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,
    ingest_ts           TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,
    silver_ts           TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,
    src_system          VARCHAR(32)  NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (lot_id)
);

Other SCML entities — SCML.Movement, SCML.Receipt, SCML.Shipment — follow the same shape: canonical IDs, conformed types, three timestamps, source-system lineage columns. Appendix A lists every entity column.

9.2 MERML — Merchandising Model Layer

MERML is RC-anchored but reaches into ICE-Aftermarket-05 (parts replenishment) and parts of TH-HOT-03 (hotel pricing) where pricing-elasticity concepts apply.

DDL — MERML.Markdown · MERML.Elasticity (sketches)
CREATE TABLE MERML.Markdown (
    markdown_id         VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,
    sku_id              VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,
    location_id         VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,
    base_price          DECIMAL(12,4) NOT NULL,
    markdown_pct        DECIMAL(5,2)  NOT NULL,    -- 0.00–100.00
    markdown_price      AS (base_price * (1 - markdown_pct/100)) PERSISTED,
    effective_from      TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,
    effective_to        TIMESTAMP,
    reason_code         VARCHAR(32),               -- 'aging' | 'overstock' | 'excursion' | 'competitive'
    approved_by         VARCHAR(64),               -- HITL approver identity
    decision_ledger_id  VARCHAR(64),               -- LEDGER row that approved this markdown
    event_ts            TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,
    ingest_ts           TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,
    silver_ts           TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,
    src_system          VARCHAR(32)  NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (markdown_id, effective_from)
);

CREATE TABLE MERML.Elasticity (
    sku_id              VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,
    location_id         VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,
    elasticity_coef     DECIMAL(8,4)  NOT NULL,    -- demand-vs-price slope
    elasticity_r2       DECIMAL(5,4),              -- model fit quality
    estimation_window   VARCHAR(16)  NOT NULL,     -- 'last-90d' | 'last-180d' | ...
    estimated_at        TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,
    silver_ts           TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (sku_id, location_id, estimation_window, estimated_at)
);

Note decision_ledger_id on MERML.Markdown. Every approved markdown carries the LEDGER row that authorized it, which closes the loop between the Service decision and the Silver entity that records the outcome. This is how KPI attribution travels back to the agent that proposed and the human that approved.

MERML.Markdown.sku_id ⛓VARCHAR(64)FK to canonical SKU · the product being marked down
