Catalog Drills

  1. ICE-EaaS-04 has OEM-controlled Bronze ingestion · the EaaS contract gives the OEM equipment-telemetry rights but the SLA-credit attribution writes to the customer's tenant. Sketch the two-tenant data flow and identify where Independence boundaries (Deployment Guide §3) constrain the architecture.
  2. ICE-Aftermarket-05 Predictive Parts Replenishment shares SCML.Inventory + MERML.Elasticity with RC-E2E-03. Diff the two Services' Pricing/Demand-Checker prompts — what RC-specific reasoning has to be removed for ICE, and what ICE-specific reasoning has to be added?
  3. ICE-Aftermarket-03 Warranty Intake + Fraud cross-references QMSML.Defect via §17.4 cross-Service composition (the data-owner is AXLE-QMS-02 in some onboardings, ICE-internal in others). Walk the role-assignment graph — when AXLE owns QMSML, who grants ICE-Aftermarket-03 access?
Part VII

Growing Superagents

A Service that ships in Year 1 and runs unchanged through Year 3 has not learned anything. The Sellers Guide promises a maturation arc — year one instincts, year two record, year three superagent — and that arc only happens if the architecture deliberately captures, rehydrates, and re-trains on every decision the Service makes. This part walks the two substrates that make it work: LEDGER as durable long-term memory, Azure Cache for Redis as hot working memory. Together they close the feedback loop that turns an instinct-driven Year-1 agent into a recall-driven Year-2 collaborator into a proactive Year-3 superagent.

Chapters in this Part

  • Chapter 25LEDGER + Redis Cache · The Superagent Learning Loop
Chapter 25

LEDGER + Redis Cache — The Superagent Learning Loop

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Reframes LEDGER from "audit log" to "long-term memory of every decision the Service ever made."
  • Introduces Azure Cache for Redis (Premium) as the working/episodic memory complement that closes the learning loop at runtime.
  • Maps the Sellers Guide three-year maturation arc to concrete artifacts and cadences within Microsoft platform services.
  • Shows how RC-E2E-03 specifically grows from a Year-1 rules-driven Service to a Year-3 superagent — anchored on the Pricing Agent introduced in §18.1.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • LEDGER is durable long-term memory · cryptographically anchored to the Microsoft.KeyVault HSM CMK · every decision · every outcome · every HITL response.
  • Azure Cache for Redis Premium is hot working/episodic memory · workflow session · last-N similar decisions · operator-preference cache.
  • Five learning cadences run in parallel (per-decision Redis through per-month Microsoft Fabric notebook) without conflict if each writes to the right substrate.
  • Superagent = Layer 3 SemVer bump · cosign attestation records LEDGER training corpus by Merkle root · Microsoft Purview tracks lineage.
  • The Year-1 → Year-3 arc is real; RC-E2E-03 GM lift moves from +3.2pp toward +4.5pp only if Year-1 LEDGER accrues without shortcuts.

25.1 The Three-Year Maturation Arc, Revisited

The Sellers Guide §6.12.7 (Runtime Addendum) commits the APEX architecture to a three-year trajectory. The arc is restated here because the Services Guide is where it becomes operational — the trajectory is realized in data, not in marketing slides.

StageWhat the agents haveWhat HITL looks likeWhat the data layer holds
Year 1 · instinctsHand-authored prompts · static rule libraries · initial Gold martsOperator reviews and approves most decisions; LEDGER row records the call & outcomeLEDGER fills with first-pass decisions · attribution marts begin to materialize
Year 2 · recordPrompts refined against LEDGER · MCP tool selection learned · per-persona personalizationOperator confirms most decisions; HITL becomes targeted exception-review rather than blanket approvalLEDGER has 12+ months × per-store × per-SKU history · attribution mart shows realized lift vs baseline · Redis caches frequent decisions for <100ms recall
Year 3 · superagentCross-Service generalization · adaptive orchestration · proactive recommendation that pre-anticipates operator preferencesOperator audits a sample; HITL is mostly confirmation; rare-event triage gets the human attention it deservesLEDGER is a multi-million-row training corpus · Microsoft Fabric notebooks mine cross-Service patterns · Redis holds millions of episodic memories indexed by similarity

The arc requires two things the data plane in Parts II–IV did not need: durable accumulation of every decision and its outcome (LEDGER) and fast access to the recent and the similar (Redis). Neither is novel; both are easy to underweight. This chapter gives them their proper weight.

25.2 LEDGER as Long-Term Memory

The Deployment Guide treats LEDGER as the audit chain — append-only, hash-chained, WORM-enforced on Azure SQL Database in PROD or Azure Database for PostgreSQL Flexible Server in DEV. Every property that makes LEDGER good for audit also makes it the right shape for long-term memory:

  • Append-only — the training record is never silently rewritten. A Year-2 prompt refinement that takes inspiration from Year-1 decisions is reasoning over data that has not been edited.
  • Hash-chained — a subset of LEDGER rows used as a training corpus is reproducibly identifiable; Microsoft Purview registers the corpus → agent version edge.
  • Cryptographically anchored to the Microsoft.KeyVault HSM CMK — a regulator (or a careful internal reviewer) can prove that the training data the Year-2 agent learned from is what it claims to be.
  • Per-Service scoping — every row carries service_code, so per-Service learning queries do not mix corpora across Services.

25.2.1 What LEDGER actually records

For the Year-3 superagent to exist, LEDGER must record more than "a decision happened." Each row carries the structure that makes learning possible:

SQL — LEDGER row shape (excerpt)
CREATE TABLE LEDGER (
    ledger_id            UNIQUEIDENTIFIER NOT NULL,
    service_code         VARCHAR(64)   NOT NULL,
    workflow_run_id      UNIQUEIDENTIFIER NOT NULL,
    step_id              VARCHAR(64)   NOT NULL,
    event                VARCHAR(64)   NOT NULL,    -- 'mcp.tool.called' · 'decision.committed' · 'hitl.staged' · 'hitl.resolved'
    agent_id             VARCHAR(64),
    tool_name            VARCHAR(128),               -- e.g., rc_e2e_03.get_excursion_decision_panel
    input_args           NVARCHAR(MAX),              -- JSON
    output_row_hash      VARCHAR(70),                -- 'sha256:' + 64 hex
    decision_payload     NVARCHAR(MAX),              -- JSON · for decision.committed events
    cost_per_decision    DECIMAL(12,4),              -- runtime token + compute cost
    safety_verdict       VARCHAR(32),                -- 'pass' · 'flagged' · etc.
    -- Outcome attribution (filled in after the fact by attribution mart pipeline)
    attribution_outcome  NVARCHAR(MAX),              -- realized KPI delta, JSON
    attribution_filled_at DATETIME2,
    -- Hash chain
    _hash                VARCHAR(70)   NOT NULL,
    _prev_hash           VARCHAR(70),
    -- Stamps
    event_ts             DATETIME2     NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (ledger_id)
);

Three columns make this row a memory rather than just a log: decision_payload (what the agent decided), attribution_outcome (what happened next, filled in by the attribution mart pipeline within hours-to-weeks of the decision), and cost_per_decision (the economic cost of producing the decision). With those three plus the tool_name/input_args/output_row_hash chain, a learning pipeline can answer "of decisions like this one, which produced KPI lift?" — the question that turns Year-1 instincts into Year-2 record into Year-3 anticipation.

25.3 Azure Cache for Redis as Working / Episodic Memory

LEDGER is durable, slow, and large. Agents reasoning at Container Apps latency cannot reach into a 50M-row LEDGER on every step. They need a fast working memory that holds the recently relevant: the in-flight workflow's earlier steps, the last N similar decisions, the hot KPI cache, and the current operator's revealed preferences. Azure Cache for Redis (Microsoft.Cache/Redis@2024-03-01, Premium tier) is that substrate.

25.3.1 The four Redis use patterns

PatternWhat it holdsKey shapeTTL
Workflow session stateEarlier-step outputs in the current workflow run · resumable across HITL waitswf:{run_id}:step:{step_id}24 h (or longer for HITL stages with extended waits)
Episodic memoryLast N similar decisions and their outcomes · indexed by Service + scenario + key dimensions for in-context retrievalSorted set: epi:{service_code}:{scenario}:{key}90 d rolling window · evicted by LRU
Hot Gold-mart cacheRecently-read Gold rows · pre-fetched by predictable triggers (e.g., when an excursion fires, the panel is cached before The Analyst even calls)gold:{mart}:{key}Mart-specific (5 min for decision-panel, 60 min for time-series, <1 min for point-in-time)
Operator revealed preferencesPer-persona HITL acceptance/decline patterns, surfacing as an in-prompt context the agent can readpref:{service_code}:{persona_id}Refreshed nightly from LEDGER batch

25.3.2 Microsoft platform wiring

BICEP — Azure Cache for Redis (Premium)
resource redis 'Microsoft.Cache/Redis@2024-03-01' = {
  name:     'apexredis-${deploymentSuffix}'
  location: location
  properties: {
    sku: {
      name:     'Premium'    // Premium required for VNet integration, persistence,
      family:   'P'          // RedisJSON / RediSearch modules, geo-replication
      capacity: 1            // P1 = 6 GB; scale per Service workload
    }
    enableNonSslPort: false
    minimumTlsVersion: '1.2'
    publicNetworkAccess: 'Disabled'
    redisConfiguration: {
      'maxmemory-policy': 'allkeys-lru'
      'maxmemory-reserved': '125'
      'rdb-backup-enabled': 'true'
      'rdb-backup-frequency': '60'
    }
    redisVersion: '6'
    subnetId: subnet.id    // VNet-integrated; private endpoint
  }
  tags: tags
}

resource redisAccessPolicy 'Microsoft.Cache/Redis/accessPolicyAssignments@2024-03-01' = {
  parent: redis
  name:   '${runtimeIdentityId}-policy'
  properties: {
    accessPolicyName: 'Data Contributor'
    objectId:         runtimePrincipalId
    objectIdAlias:    'mi-apex-merch-runtime'
  }
}

Redis is a Layer-1 platform resource (one per tenant) with per-Service logical-database scoping (Redis databases 0..15) or, at higher scale, key-prefix scoping with a single logical DB. The mi-apex-merch-runtime identity authenticates via Microsoft Entra ID; no connection-string secrets are kept in Microsoft.KeyVault — Microsoft Entra ID-aware Redis access policies replace static auth keys.

Note — Why Premium tier is non-negotiable

The Standard tier of Azure Cache for Redis lacks the three features APEX depends on: VNet integration with private endpoint (so Redis traffic never crosses the public internet), RedisJSON + RediSearch modules (so episodic memory can be queried by similarity, not just by exact key), and persistence + geo-replication (so a regional outage doesn't reset the working memory back to Year-1 instincts). Premium is what makes Redis a durable participant in the learning loop rather than a disposable cache.

25.4 The Feedback Loop Architecture

25.5 What Gets Learned, At Which Cadence

Different signals mature at different cadences. Mixing the cadences is what causes "the agents are learning but nothing changes" — the Year-1 prompt was being rewritten every minute on tiny working-set changes. Conversely, if everything waits for monthly batch, the loop is too slow to feel adaptive in the operator's day. Five cadences operate in parallel:

CadenceSubstrateWhat is learned / refreshed
Per-decision (sub-second)RedisWhich evidence row hashes to fetch first; in-context similar decisions for the agent prompt's few-shot block
Per-shift / hourRedis tail of LEDGEROperator preference snapshot; intra-shift HITL acceptance pattern
Per-dayLEDGER batch · Microsoft Fabric WarehousePer-Service prompt-variation A/B; MCP tool-selection weight tuning
Per-weekLEDGER + attribution martGold-mart shape refinement; new MCP tool registration; new few-shot exemplar set
Per-monthLEDGER + Microsoft Fabric notebookCross-Service pattern mining; superagent candidate behaviors that may warrant a new agent or a Layer 3 minor SemVer bump

25.6 Cross-Service Learning Within the Independence Boundary

Within a single client tenant, cross-Service learning is unconstrained — the data, the LEDGER, the Redis instance, and the Microsoft Fabric capacity all belong to the same client and are governed by the same Microsoft contract. RC-E2E-03's pricing decisions teach RC-E2E-04's loyalty winback offers what kinds of price levels operators tend to approve. HLS-E2E-04's prior-auth speed-of-resolution patterns inform HLS-E2E-02's denial-prevention pre-emptive actions. Within-tenant cross-Service learning is the leverage that makes "more Services per tenant" commercially compelling beyond the cost amortization in the Deployment Guide.

Across tenants, learning never crosses. Each client tenant is a closed superagent training universe. The Deloitte authoring tenant accumulates synthetic LEDGER for product-level superagent prototyping, but no client decision row, no client persona preference, no client KPI outcome ever enters another client's training corpus. Federated learning patterns are out of scope for v1; they may revisit when ≥ 5 client tenants are live and a careful cross-tenant aggregation proposal can be reviewed under the two-contract structure.

Independence — Each tenant is a closed universe

The temptation to "use lessons from Client A's RC-E2E-03 to bootstrap Client B's onboarding" is structural Independence violation. The bootstrap is the Deloitte-authored agent image, the Deloitte-authored prompts, the Deloitte-authored MCP tool definitions, the Deloitte-authored synthetic-fixture training corpus. Client A's actual decisions, actual personas, actual outcomes never propagate. The mirror boundary at Layer 3 (Deployment Guide §1.4) is also a learning-corpus boundary.

ADR · architecture decision
ADR-025-01 · 2026-05-09 · accepted

Decision: Cross-tenant learning is structurally forbidden. Each client tenant is a closed superagent training universe. Federated-learning patterns are out of scope for v1 of the Trilogy.

Why: The two-contract Independence model (Deployment Guide §3 · Sellers Guide §6.13) requires that each client's data — including the LEDGER + attribution-mart corpus that trains Year-2 prompts — stays bounded by the client's Microsoft platform contract. Aggregating across tenants is not technically hard; it is structurally incompatible with how APEX is sold and audited. Microsoft Fabric tenancy, the per-tenant Microsoft.ContainerRegistry, the Microsoft.KeyVault HSM CMK, and the Microsoft Purview audit graph all enforce the boundary at the platform level.

Trade-off rejected: Federated learning across tenants with differential privacy. Technically feasible (Microsoft research has working implementations); commercially defensible only after ≥ 5 client tenants are live, the regulatory landscape stabilizes (HIPAA federated-AI guidance is still nascent in 2026), and a careful Independence-review of the aggregation pattern lands. Defer to v2 of the Trilogy.

Within-tenant learning is the leverage. Cross-Service learning inside one tenant — RC-E2E-03 informing RC-E2E-04 winback offers · HLS-E2E-04 prior-auth speed informing HLS-E2E-02 denial-prevention timing — is unconstrained, audited, and the architectural reason multi-Service tenants are commercially compelling.

25.7 The Superagent Promotion Pattern

A "superagent" is not a separate Container App. It is an evolved version of an existing agent — for example, The Analyst v3.2 — whose prompt structure, MCP tool selection logic, and few-shot exemplar set have matured through the LEDGER + Redis feedback loop. The promotion is mechanically identical to any other Layer 3 minor SemVer bump (Deployment Guide §12.1) except that the cosign attestation predicate (Deployment Guide §12.3 / Appendix D) records the LEDGER training corpus the new version was tuned against.

JSON — superagent attestation predicate (extending the standard form)
{
  "predicateType": "apex.deloitte/agent-attestation/v1",
  "predicate": {
    "image_digest":    "sha256:...",
    "agent_id":        "the-analyst",
    "agent_version":   "3.2.0",
    "platform_compatible": ">=0.1.0,<0.4.0",
    "training_corpus": {
      "ledger_query":  "service_code='RC-E2E-03' AND event_ts BETWEEN '2026-01-01' AND '2027-12-31'",
      "row_count":     2_847_233,
      "row_hash_root": "sha256:...",     // Merkle root of all LEDGER rows in corpus
      "attribution_marts": ["g_markdown_outcome_attribution", "g_excursion_outcome_attribution"]
    },
    "scan_summary":    { "high": 0, "medium": 0, "low": 1 },
    "build_timestamp": "2027-04-12T09:30:00Z"
  }
}

Microsoft Purview registers the training_corpus.row_hash_root as a lineage edge from the agent version to the LEDGER subset that produced it. A regulator (or a careful internal reviewer) can later ask "what data shaped this agent's behavior?" and walk the lineage from The Analyst v3.2 → its training corpus → the LEDGER rows → the Gold marts → the Silver entities → the Bronze rows → the SOR systems. The audit chain that began as a compliance artifact becomes the credentialing system for the superagent itself.

25.8 RC-E2E-03 Across the Three-Year Arc — A Worked Example

RC-E2E-03 makes the arc concrete because all five (now six, counting the Pricing Agent introduced in §18.1) agents are well-defined and the KPI envelope is measurable.

25.8.1 Year 1 — Instincts

  • The Pricing Agent uses a static elasticity coefficient (estimated from 90 days of pre-pilot POS history) plus a competitor-price floor rule plus a per-category margin floor.
  • Most markdown proposals require Merchandising Director HITL.
  • Cold-chain excursion handling defers to a hand-authored decision tree based on lot type, peak temperature, and duration.
  • LEDGER fills with first-pass decisions and HITL responses.
  • Redis caches per-workflow session state and hot Gold-mart reads — but no episodic memory yet (no Year-2 corpus to draw from).

25.8.2 Year 2 — Record

  • The Pricing Agent's prompt now contains a few-shot block of recent similar markdowns approved by the Merchandising Director — pulled from Redis episodic memory at workflow start.
  • Elasticity is rebuilt monthly from the rolling 12-month LEDGER + POS join, per-store-per-category.
  • The cold-chain decision tree is replaced with a model whose training corpus is Year-1 LEDGER decisions × realized shrink/margin outcomes from g_excursion_outcome_attribution.
  • HITL becomes targeted exception-review: predictable cases auto-approve at Operations Lead level; exception-review goes to the Merchandising Director.
  • Redis caches operator preference snapshots refreshed nightly from LEDGER.

25.8.3 Year 3 — Superagent

  • The Pricing Agent has ingested ~18 months × 250 stores × thousands of SKUs of decisions and proactively proposes prices that pre-anticipate the operator's preferences. Confidence scores on proposals routinely exceed the threshold for auto-approval.
  • Cross-Service: lessons from RC-E2E-08 (Marketing & Growth) campaign-elasticity outcomes inform The Pricing Agent's promo-price recommendations.
  • The Analyst v3.x is a superagent: it categorizes excursions into refinement clusters that did not exist in Year-1 prompts; its MCP tool calls anticipate which evidence will matter and warm the Redis Gold-mart cache before The Pricing Agent even runs.
  • HITL is mostly confirmation; rare-event triage gets the Merchandising Director's full attention.
  • The KPI envelope shifts: GM lift moves from +3.2pp toward +4.5pp, days-on-hand toward −34%, markdown-to-clear toward +52%. The Sellers Guide commitments are met with margin.
Best Practice — Don't skip Year 1

The temptation to "ship a Year-2 prompt at Year-1 launch" is strong, especially after a few clients onboard and the Deloitte authoring corpus suggests good defaults. Resist. Year-1 LEDGER from this client tenant is what the Year-2 refinement is supposed to learn from; pre-loading prompts trained on synthetic or other-client data violates the Independence boundary in §25.6. Year 1 is where the Service learns the client. Skip it and you have a clever shipping product, not a maturing Service.

Key Takeaways

  • LEDGER is durable long-term memory — every decision, every outcome, every HITL response — anchored cryptographically to the Microsoft.KeyVault HSM CMK.
  • Azure Cache for Redis (Premium) is hot working/episodic memory: workflow session state, last-N similar decisions, hot Gold-mart cache, operator preference cache. Premium tier is non-negotiable for VNet integration, RedisJSON, and persistence.
  • Five learning cadences operate in parallel — per-decision (Redis) through per-month (LEDGER + Microsoft Fabric notebook) — without conflict if each writes to the right substrate.
  • Cross-Service learning within a tenant is the leverage; cross-tenant learning is structurally forbidden by the Independence boundary.
  • A superagent is a Layer 3 SemVer bump whose cosign attestation records the LEDGER training corpus by Merkle root, making the agent's behavior reproducible and the training data lineage walkable in Microsoft Purview.
  • The three-year maturation arc is real and is what turns RC-E2E-03 from a +3.2pp Year-1 result into a +4.5pp Year-3 outcome — but only if Year-1 LEDGER is allowed to accrue without shortcuts.

Service Drills

  1. Pick one Service from Part VI. List its existing MCP tools (Appendix B) and identify which become candidates for episodic-memory-augmented prompts in Year 2.
  2. For RC-E2E-03 specifically, sketch the operator-preference cache shape that the Merchandising Director would benefit from; what columns does Redis need to hold to make her HITL acceptance pattern legible to the Pricing Agent at decision time?
  3. Draft the LEDGER query that would build the training corpus for a Year-2 Pricing Agent in a tenant that has 12 months of operation. What date range, what filters, what minimum row count would make the corpus credible enough to ship a v2 image?
Part VIII

Practitioner Tracks

The previous seven parts taught the architecture: data tiers, schemas, MCP, orchestrations, the catalog, the learning loop. This part teaches the practice. Chapter 26 is a linear hands-on walkthrough — clone the repo at hour zero, watch a HITL approval at hour seven. Chapter 27 takes the same patterns and exercises them on a non-RC Service so the generalization is concrete. Chapter 28 is the agent-design deep-dive that the agent maintainer reader has been waiting for since Chapter 3. Chapter 29 is the cost-and-sizing math the architect needs to defend the design economically. Together they convert the reference book into a teaching book without altering any of the reference material.

Chapter 26

From Zero to RC-E2E-03 in a Day

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks a fresh laptop from empty repo to a working RC-E2E-03 Service in eight one-hour blocks.
  • Concretizes every pattern in Parts I–V against the MVP repository under Consumer/Merch/MVP.
  • Gives every chapter that came before a touchpoint — when reading those chapters again, the reader has a working system to refer to.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • One full day · eight hours · zero Azure spend · ends with a HITL approval and verified LEDGER chain.
  • Hours 0–3 stand up data: clone → compose → land Bronze → build Silver → materialize Gold.
  • Hours 4–7 stand up reasoning: register MCP tools → run the n8n workflow → approve via the HITL stub → verify LEDGER.
  • Hour 8 is reflection — what changed, what to do tomorrow, what to read next.

26.0 What You Need Before You Start

  • A laptop with Docker Desktop running (8 GB RAM allocated minimum) — Windows / macOS / Linux.
  • Git, your editor of choice, a terminal, and a browser.
  • The MVP repository cloned: git clone <org>/apex-merch-mvp Consumer/Merch/MVP.
  • No Azure subscription required — this entire chapter runs at zero cost (§0.5 zero-cost dev guarantee).

26.1 Hour 0 — Setup

From the repository root:

SHELL — bring up the laptop tier
cd Consumer/Merch/MVP
docker compose up -d

# Wait ~60 seconds for all containers to settle, then:
docker compose ps

Expected output — eight containers in running state: postgres · n8n · harness · hitl-stub · mcp-rc-e2e-03 · agent-the-analyst · agent-the-pricing-agent · agent-the-operations-lead. (Demand Checker · Finance Lead · Briefer come online in Sprints 2–5; Hour 1's smoke test does not require them.)

Browser smoke test — open three tabs:

  • localhost:5678 — n8n editor (login developer/devonly; see §15.1)
  • localhost:8080/healthz — harness health endpoint, returns 200
  • localhost:8090 — HITL stub form, blank approval queue
Warning · port collisions

If docker compose up fails with "port already in use," check for a local Postgres on 5432 or another n8n on 5678. Stop the conflicting service or change the port in docker-compose.yml before retrying.

26.2 Hour 1 — Land Bronze

Bronze is where client SOR data begins (Part II). For the laptop tier, the SOR is a set of synthetic fixture files under MVP/fixtures/. Run the ingestion script that lands them into Postgres:

SHELL — Bronze ingest
docker compose exec harness python -m harness.ingest \
    --service rc-e2e-03 \
    --fixture fixtures/rc-e2e-03/bronze-day-zero/

# Inspect what landed
docker compose exec postgres psql -U harness -d harness -c "
  SELECT table_name, n_live_tup AS row_count
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables
  WHERE schemaname = 'bronze'
  ORDER BY table_name;"

You should see four Bronze tables populated: br_pos_transactions (~12,000 rows) · br_erp_inventory (~3,200 rows) · br_refrigeration_telemetry (~58,000 rows) · br_competitor_pricing (~600 rows). These are synthetic — no real client PII, but Tier-3 fields are tokenized at ingest per §6.2 to exercise the same pipeline a production tenant would use.

What you just did — exercised every concept in Chapter 4 (SOR inventory), Chapter 5 (the streaming-vs-batch split — refrigeration is streaming-shaped here even if it lands in Postgres on the laptop tier), Chapter 6 (tokenization at ingest), Chapter 7 (PII classification — the loyalty-tier fixture is Tier 3).

26.3 Hour 2 — Build Silver

Silver conforms Bronze rows into canonical APEX schemas (Part III). On Azure this runs as Microsoft Fabric notebooks; on the laptop it runs as Python scripts under the harness. One command rebuilds every Silver entity for RC-E2E-03:

SHELL — Silver build
docker compose exec harness python -m harness.silver_build \
    --service rc-e2e-03 \
    --rebuild

# Verify the canonical entities
docker compose exec postgres psql -U harness -d harness -c "
  SELECT 'SCML.Inventory' AS entity, COUNT(*) FROM silver.scml_inventory UNION ALL
  SELECT 'SCML.Lot',                  COUNT(*) FROM silver.scml_lot       UNION ALL
  SELECT 'MERML.Markdown',            COUNT(*) FROM silver.merml_markdown UNION ALL
  SELECT 'MERML.Elasticity',          COUNT(*) FROM silver.merml_elasticity UNION ALL
  SELECT 'MERML.Competitor',          COUNT(*) FROM silver.merml_competitor UNION ALL
  SELECT 'PROML.Pricing',             COUNT(*) FROM silver.proml_pricing   UNION ALL
  SELECT 'PROML.DiscountRule',        COUNT(*) FROM silver.proml_discount_rule;"

Each entity should have a sensible row count (Inventory ~3,200 · Lot ~140 · Markdown ~280 · Elasticity ~3,200 · Competitor ~600 · Pricing ~3,200 · DiscountRule ~24). The build is idempotent (§8.2): drop a Silver table, re-run, get byte-identical output.

Try the rebuild contract right now. Drop one Silver table, re-run the build, confirm the row count comes back the same:

SHELL — exercise the rebuild contract
docker compose exec postgres psql -U harness -d harness -c "DROP TABLE silver.merml_competitor;"
docker compose exec harness python -m harness.silver_build --service rc-e2e-03 --entity MERML.Competitor
docker compose exec postgres psql -U harness -d harness -c "SELECT COUNT(*) FROM silver.merml_competitor;"

26.4 Hour 3 — Materialize Gold

Gold marts are the agent-queryable shape (Part IV). For RC-E2E-03 the five marts from §12.2 materialize from Silver:

SHELL — Gold materialization
docker compose exec harness python -m harness.gold_materialize \
    --service rc-e2e-03 \
    --mart all

# Inspect the marts
docker compose exec postgres psql -U harness -d harness -c "
  SELECT 'g_excursion_decision_panel'      AS mart, COUNT(*) FROM gold.g_excursion_decision_panel UNION ALL
  SELECT 'g_pricing_recommendation_basis',          COUNT(*) FROM gold.g_pricing_recommendation_basis UNION ALL
  SELECT 'g_markdown_proposal_basis',               COUNT(*) FROM gold.g_markdown_proposal_basis UNION ALL
  SELECT 'g_inventory_position_current',            COUNT(*) FROM gold.g_inventory_position_current UNION ALL
  SELECT 'g_kpi_rc_e2e_03_daily',                   COUNT(*) FROM gold.g_kpi_rc_e2e_03_daily;"

Now check that the row-hash convention from §12.3 is in effect:

SHELL — verify Gold row hashes
docker compose exec postgres psql -U harness -d harness -c "
  SELECT excursion_id, store_id, materialized_at, row_hash
  FROM gold.g_excursion_decision_panel
  ORDER BY fired_ts DESC
  LIMIT 3;"

Each row should carry a non-null sha256:... hash and a materialized_at timestamp within the last few minutes. These are the hashes the MCP tool will return alongside data and the agents will reconcile against LEDGER (§3.3).

26.5 Hour 4 — Register MCP Tools

The MCP host registers tool definitions at startup from mcp-manifest.yaml (§13.3). It's already running from Hour 0; let's verify its catalog:

SHELL — list MCP tools
curl -s http://localhost:8083/tools | jq '.tools[] | {name, pattern, gold_mart}'

Expected output — at least the four read tools and two HITL tools from §18.1: rc_e2e_03.get_excursion_decision_panel · rc_e2e_03.get_markdown_proposal_basis · rc_e2e_03.get_pricing_recommendation_basis · rc_e2e_03.get_similar_pricing_decisions · rc_e2e_03.commit_markdown_decision · rc_e2e_03.stage_markdown_approval.

Call one tool directly to make sure it actually queries the Gold mart:

SHELL — invoke an MCP tool
curl -s -X POST http://localhost:8083/tools/rc_e2e_03.get_excursion_decision_panel \
    -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
    -d '{
      "store_id":     "BIGBOX-100",
      "excursion_id": "BIGBOX-100-DAIRY-202605091400"
    }' | jq

Response includes the joined panel (lot context · at-risk inventory · recent markdowns) plus the row_hash. A LEDGER row was emitted recording the call (verify in Hour 7).

26.6 Hour 5 — Run the Workflow

Open n8n at localhost:5678. The excursion-triage workflow is pre-imported from MVP/agentic-merch/services/rc-e2e-03/workflows/excursion-triage/workflow.n8n.json (§15.3). Activate it.

Trigger the workflow with the Hour 1 fixture excursion event:

SHELL — fire the workflow

    -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
    -d @MVP/agentic-merch/services/rc-e2e-03/workflows/excursion-triage/fixtures/excursion-fired-001.json

In the n8n editor, watch the workflow execute. The trace should show: trigger fires → LEDGER workflow.start row → The Analyst /run returns severity 0.82 → branch fires "high" arm → fan-out to The Pricing Agent and The Demand Checker (or stubs if Sprint 2–5 agents are not yet swapped in) → The Operations Lead composes the HITL card → workflow pauses at the HITL stage.

26.7 Hour 6 — HITL Approval

Open the HITL stub at localhost:8090. The pending card from Hour 5 should be visible — store BIGBOX-100, dairy excursion, proposed action "markdown 35%," approver Marisol Reyes (Store Operations Lead).

Click Approve. Behind the scenes:

  • The HITL stub POSTs the approval back to n8n's resume webhook.
  • n8n's wait node releases; the workflow continues to rc_e2e_03.commit_markdown_decision.
  • MCP host writes the markdown row into silver.merml_markdown and emits a decision.committed LEDGER row referencing the evidence row hashes from Hour 5.
  • Workflow emits a final workflow.complete LEDGER row and ends.

Chapter 27

HLS-E2E-04 — A Second Worked Example

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the same eight-hour pattern from Chapter 26, but for HLS-E2E-04 Prior Authorization.
  • Demonstrates the architecture generalizes across practices — different schemas, different agents, different HITL persona, same shape.
  • Surfaces the deltas where Tier-4 PHI handling materially changes the motion (dual-control pii-unlock at HITL).
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • HLS-E2E-04 is a triage-and-decide Service like RC-E2E-03 — the workflow primitives are identical.
  • Schemas swap: SCML/MERML/PROML → EHRML/CMSML. Agents swap: Demand Checker/Pricing Agent/Operations Lead → Clinical Reviewer/UM-Policy-Match/Operations Lead.
  • HITL gets stricter: Tier-4 PHI requires dual-control unlock (§7.3); HITL persona is a clinician, not a store ops lead.
  • The eight hours run identically; only the fixtures, schemas, and prompts differ.

27.1 The Service in One Page

From the §19.2 profile: HLS-E2E-04 automates prior-authorization decisions on the payer side. KPI envelope: cycle time 6.2d → 2.6d, first-pass approval +28pp, auth admin time −71%. Bronze sources are EHR (Epic Resolute · Cerner · Allscripts) and the payer authorization platform; Silver entities are EHRML.Encounter, EHRML.Procedure, CMSML.Authorization, CMSML.Member; Gold marts are g_prior_auth_decision_panel, g_um_policy_match, g_kpi_hls_e2e_04_daily.

What's structurally different from RC-E2E-03:

ConcernRC-E2E-03HLS-E2E-04
Bronze SORPOS · ERP · refrigeration · competitor feedEHR · payer auth platform · UM policy library · clinical guidelines
Silver schemasSCML · MERML · PROMLEHRML · CMSML
Gold marts5 (decision-panel · point-in-time · time-series · attribution)3 (decision-panel · point-in-time · time-series)
PII tierTier 3 on customer/loyalty fieldsTier 4 PHI throughout · dual-control unlock
HITL personaMarisol Reyes · Store Operations LeadDr. Sandra Park · Utilization Management Clinician
HITL cadencePer-event (excursion) or per-batch (markdown)Per-auth-request · throughput-driven · <30s SLA per auth
AgentsAnalyst · Demand Checker · Pricing · Finance · Ops · BrieferAnalyst · UM-Policy-Match · Clinical Reviewer · Ops · Briefer
Workflow shapeTriage-and-decideTriage-and-decide (same primitives)
KPI attributionGM lift · DoH · markdown-to-clearCycle time · first-pass approval · admin time
ComplianceFSMA-204 (where lot-tracked)HIPAA · 21 CFR Part 11 audit trail

27.2 Hour 0 — Same Compose, Different Service Code

Bring up the laptop tier with the HLS-E2E-04 Service registered:

SHELL — switch to HLS Service
# From the same MVP repo
docker compose --profile hls-e2e-04 up -d

# Wait, then verify
docker compose ps | grep hls-e2e-04

The compose profile pulls in the HLS-E2E-04 MCP host, the Clinical Reviewer agent, and a HITL stub configured for clinician approval (with dual-control toggle exposed). Postgres, n8n, harness, and the four reusable agents (The Analyst, The Operations Lead, The Briefer, plus the cross-Service mi-apex-merch-pii-unlock stub) are shared with RC-E2E-03 if both services are running.

27.3 Hour 1 — Land Bronze (with PHI Tokenization)

SHELL — Bronze ingest, HLS fixtures
docker compose exec harness python -m harness.ingest \
    --service hls-e2e-04 \
    --fixture fixtures/hls-e2e-04/bronze-day-zero/

# Verify Tier-4 PHI is tokenized at landing
docker compose exec postgres psql -U harness -d harness -c "
  SELECT member_id, encounter_id, drg_code, los_hours
  FROM bronze.br_ehr_encounters
  LIMIT 3;"

member_id values in the output should look like tok-3a8f2c1d9b... — opaque tokens, not human-recognizable identifiers. The raw member-ID values never landed in Bronze. This is the ingest tokenization from §6.2 doing its job at Tier-4 strictness.

Warning · synthetic PHI on laptop

The HLS-E2E-04 fixtures are synthetic — names like "Test Patient 0042" — but the architecture treats them with full Tier-4 discipline so you exercise the same code path a production tenant would. Don't be tempted to "skip tokenization on laptop because it's synthetic"; you'd be drilling a happy-path that won't survive contact with real PHI.

27.4 Hour 2 — Build Silver (EHRML + CMSML)

SHELL — Silver build for HLS
docker compose exec harness python -m harness.silver_build \
    --service hls-e2e-04 \
    --rebuild

# Verify the canonical entities
docker compose exec postgres psql -U harness -d harness -c "
  SELECT 'EHRML.Encounter'      AS entity, COUNT(*) FROM silver.ehrml_encounter UNION ALL
  SELECT 'EHRML.Diagnosis',                COUNT(*) FROM silver.ehrml_diagnosis UNION ALL
  SELECT 'EHRML.Procedure',                COUNT(*) FROM silver.ehrml_procedure UNION ALL
  SELECT 'CMSML.Authorization',            COUNT(*) FROM silver.cmsml_authorization UNION ALL
  SELECT 'CMSML.Member',                   COUNT(*) FROM silver.cmsml_member;"

Notice the schema shape from the §9.3 ERD now becomes concrete data: EHRML.Encounter rows join to EHRML.Diagnosis on encounter_id; CMSML.Authorization joins to CMSML.Member and to EHRML.Encounter via the cross-schema member_id token. That cross-schema join is the entire substrate of the Service.

27.5 Hour 3 — Materialize Gold

SHELL — Gold materialization
docker compose exec harness python -m harness.gold_materialize \
    --service hls-e2e-04 \
    --mart all

# Inspect the prior-auth panel
docker compose exec postgres psql -U harness -d harness -c "
  SELECT auth_id, member_id_token, cpt_code, urgency_score,
         um_policy_match, materialized_at, row_hash
  FROM gold.g_prior_auth_decision_panel
  ORDER BY fired_ts DESC LIMIT 3;"

Notice member_id_token in the Gold mart — even Gold holds tokens, never raw PHI. The unlock happens only at HITL approval (Hour 6).

27.6 Hour 4 — Register MCP Tools

SHELL — list HLS MCP tools
curl -s http://localhost:8084/tools | jq '.tools[] | {name, pattern, requires_dual_control}'

Expected tools (§19.2): hls_e2e_04.get_prior_auth_decision_panel · hls_e2e_04.match_um_policy · hls_e2e_04.commit_auth_decision · hls_e2e_04.stage_clinician_approval (with requires_dual_control: true when the auth crosses the cost-or-complexity threshold). The HITL-stage tool's requires_dual_control flag is the mechanism that turns a single-clinician approval into a dual-clinician one.

27.7 Hour 5 — Run the Workflow

Same n8n editor at localhost:5678. The prior-auth-decision workflow is pre-imported from MVP/agentic-merch/services/hls-e2e-04/workflows/prior-auth-decision/workflow.n8n.json. Activate, then trigger:

SHELL — fire HLS workflow
curl -s -X POST http://localhost:5678/webhook/auth-requested \
    -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
    -d @MVP/agentic-merch/services/hls-e2e-04/workflows/prior-auth-decision/fixtures/auth-requested-001.json

The workflow runs the same primitive sequence as RC-E2E-03's excursion-triage (§14.3). The differences are inside each step:

  • The Analyst ranks urgency on a clinical scale (chief-complaint code · DRG · LOS prediction) instead of a margin-at-risk score.
  • UM-Policy-Match agent (HLS-specific specialist) searches the policy library for matching coverage rules — auto-approves when the policy match is unambiguous and the clinical criteria are satisfied.
  • The Clinical Reviewer handles ambiguous cases — surfaces evidence to the HITL clinician.
  • The Operations Lead composes the HITL adaptive card, including a dual-control flag for high-cost or experimental procedures.

27.8 Hour 6 — HITL Approval with Dual-Control Unlock

Open the HITL stub. The pending card looks structurally similar to the RC card but with HLS-specific fields (CPT code, member tier, clinical-evidence summary). Two buttons appear: Approve and Decline. For high-cost procedures, a third button appears: Request second opinion (dual-control gate).

Click Request second opinion. This:

  1. Writes a LEDGER row hitl.dual_control.requested with the requesting clinician's identity.
  2. Acquires a second clinician's queue position in the HITL stub (tabbed view).
  3. Pauses the workflow until both clinicians have responded.
  4. If both approve · the system acquires the mi-apex-merch-pii-unlock token (§7.3 just-in-time) and surfaces the actual member name and DOB to the second clinician for the final-confirmation moment.
  5. Writes pii_unlock_completed · hitl.resolved · decision.committed LEDGER rows in sequence.

The dual-control flow is what's unique to HLS — RC's HITL is single-approver. The mechanism is the same MCP HITL-stage primitive (§13.1.3); the policy is what differs.

SHELL — chain verify HLS
docker compose exec harness python -m harness.ledger_verify \
    --service hls-e2e-04 \
    --since '15 minutes ago'

Expected output now includes Tier-4-specific events:

  events:

27.10 What Generalized · What Specialized

The whole architecture from Chapter 26 ran unchanged. What specialized:

  • Schemas (Part III) — different families, but the conformance contract was identical.
  • Gold-mart shapes (Part IV) — different decision-panel columns, but the row-hash + materialized_at convention was identical.
  • Agent prompts (Ch 28) — different exemplars, but the four-archetype structure was identical.
  • HITL policy (§13.1.3) — dual-control vs single, but the staging primitive was identical.
  • PII handling (Ch 7) — Tier 4 dual-control vs Tier 3 single-unlock, but the JIT identity acquisition primitive was identical.
  • KPI envelope (Sellers Guide §10) — different metrics, but the attribution mart pattern was identical (or absent — HLS doesn't have an attribution mart in this scope, which is a Year-2 gap to fill).

If you can run Chapter 26 and Chapter 27, you can run any of the 38 Services in Part VI. Each one is a substitution of schemas, agents, prompts, and HITL policy onto the same eight-hour scaffold.

Independence · HLS specifics

HLS Services touch HIPAA-regulated PHI throughout. The Tier-4 dual-control mechanism, the never-in-Bronze tokenization, and the JIT pii-unlock identity together are what make APEX HLS-eligible. None of this is HLS-specific code; the policies that engage it are HLS-specific configuration. The same code paths exist in RC-E2E-04 (Tier 3 customer PII) and TMT-MED-03 (Tier 3 device fingerprints) — the dial is set differently for each Service.

Key Takeaways

  • HLS-E2E-04 ran on the same eight-hour scaffold as RC-E2E-03 — only schemas, prompts, and HITL policy differ.
  • Tier-4 PHI is the consequential delta: dual-control unlock, PHI never in Bronze/Silver/Gold (only tokens), JIT identity acquisition at the moment of approval.
  • The architecture generalizes; running Ch 26 and Ch 27 means you can run any of the 38 catalog Services.

Service Drills

  1. Run a third Service from Part VI (suggested: TMT-TEL-RAA-04 revenue-assurance variance close — it's neither RC nor HLS, and the SOX audit-trail requirement adds a third compliance frame). Confirm the eight-hour scaffold holds.
  2. Diff the workflow.n8n.json files for RC-E2E-03 excursion-triage and HLS-E2E-04 prior-auth-decision. The structural similarity should be obvious — same primitive types, same step ordering, different node parameters.
  3. Modify the HITL stub to emit a Microsoft Teams adaptive card preview (JSON only, no actual Teams). Compare to the §16.1 Logic Apps Hybrid card the production substrate would render.
Chapter 28

Agent Design Patterns

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Names the four agent archetypes that recur across all 38 cataloged Services.
  • Walks the standard prompt structure, tool-selection logic, and few-shot exemplar curation pattern.
  • Establishes the evaluation framework — golden sets, LLM-as-judge, regression detection — that gates promotion of a new agent version.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • Four agent archetypes — Analyst · Specialist · Composer · Briefer — cover the agent population in the catalog. Pick the archetype first; design the prompt second.
  • Prompt structure is uniform: system identity · schema-shaped context · few-shot exemplars · instruction · output JSON schema.
  • Few-shot exemplars come from LEDGER. Year-1: hand-curated. Year-2: top approved decisions per persona. Year-3: similarity-retrieved from Redis episodic memory at runtime.
  • Promotion is gated by golden-set regression (no skip), LLM-as-judge sample (statistical), and live-shadow run before traffic-split (operational).

28.1 The Four Archetypes

Across the catalog, every agent fits one of four archetypes. Knowing the archetype tells you the prompt shape, the tool-selection logic, the evaluation framework, and the version-bump cadence.

ArchetypeWhat it doesExamplesPrompt focus
AnalystSeverity-rank, classify, score, prioritize. Reads one or two Gold marts; emits a numeric score plus a recommended action class.The Analyst (in every Service) · UM-Policy-Match (HLS-E2E-04) · Fraud-Score (RC-E2E-07) · Churn-Risk (RC-E2E-04)Calibration · explainability · false-positive control
SpecialistApply domain-specific rules and reasoning the Analyst doesn't carry. Reads multiple Gold marts; emits a structured proposal (price · auth decision · refund · markdown amount).The Pricing Agent (RC-E2E-03) · Clinical Reviewer (HLS-E2E-02) · RCA Specialist (AXLE-QMS-02) · Reconciliation Specialist (TMT-TEL-RAA-04)Domain knowledge · constraint satisfaction · confidence scoring
ComposerSynthesize the Analyst + Specialist outputs into a HITL-ready proposal. Composes the adaptive card body, names the approver, structures evidence.The Operations Lead (in every triage-and-decide Service)Persona alignment · evidence presentation · tone calibration
BrieferCompose end-of-shift, end-of-day, or end-of-cycle digests. Reads time-series and attribution Gold marts; emits Markdown or adaptive-card summaries.The Briefer (in 32 of 38 Services · the most-reused agent in the catalog)Narrative coherence · KPI accuracy · brevity

Note the role of Demand Checker · Finance Lead · Outreach Strategist from the catalog — these are parameterized Specialists, not separate archetypes. The Demand Checker is a Specialist whose constraint is forward-demand forecasting; the Finance Lead is a Specialist whose constraint is margin-at-risk + commercial envelope; etc. The archetype is "Specialist with a domain-specific tool set"; the agent name is the domain.

ADR · architecture decision
ADR-028-01 · 2026-05-09 · accepted

Decision: Recognize four archetypes (Analyst · Specialist · Composer · Briefer) instead of creating a separate archetype per agent role.

Why: Earlier drafts had ten or twelve role-specific archetypes (Analyst, Demand Checker, Pricing, Finance, etc.). The taxonomy was unstable — every new Service introduced a new archetype. Collapsing to four means new Services pick from existing archetypes and parameterize, which keeps the framework finite. The dimension that was inflating the taxonomy — the domain — is captured in the prompt and tool catalog, not the archetype.

Trade-off rejected: A taxonomy with explicit "Demand-Forecasting Specialist" vs "Pricing Specialist" vs "Finance Specialist" archetypes. Easier to onboard, harder to maintain — every catalog addition forces a taxonomy review.

28.2 Prompt Structure — Five Sections, Always

Every APEX agent prompt has five sections, in this order. Variations exist; the five sections do not.

PROMPT — five-section structure
# 1. System identity (who is this agent, what is the bound)
You are The Pricing Agent for Service RC-E2E-03 (Pricing & Revenue Decision Service).
Your bound is to recommend a markdown percentage for a single SKU at a single store, given
elasticity, competitor price, current price register, and discount-rule constraints. You do
NOT decide whether the markdown is approved (that is the Operations Lead + HITL approver).
You do NOT consider scenarios outside RC-E2E-03 (cold-chain, dynamic markdown).

# 2. Schema-shaped context (what data the agent has, by Gold mart)
The following Gold-mart row is in scope:
{
  "sku_id": "...",
  "location_id": "...",
  "elasticity": { "coef": -1.42, "r2": 0.81 },
  "competitor_price_median_7d": 12.45,
  "base_price": 14.99,
  "discount_floor_pct": 35.0,
  "recent_markdowns": [...],
  "row_hash": "sha256:..."
}

# 3. Few-shot exemplars (3-7 prior approved decisions of similar shape)
Example 1 — same category, similar elasticity, approved by Daniel Chen on 2026-04-22:
<input> ... </input>
<output> { "markdown_pct": 18.0, "confidence": 0.88, "reasoning": "..." } </output>

Example 2 ...
Example 3 ...

# 4. Instruction (what to produce, with constraints)
Recommend a markdown_pct that:
- Stays at or above the discount_floor_pct
- Beats the competitor_price_median_7d by at least 2% (configurable)
- Maximizes expected gross-margin lift given elasticity
Emit your reasoning briefly. Do not exceed 80 words of reasoning.

# 5. Output JSON schema (typed, validated by the runner)
Respond ONLY with this JSON shape:
{ "markdown_pct": <decimal between 0 and 60>,
  "confidence":   <decimal between 0 and 1>,
  "reasoning":    <string <= 80 words>,
  "evidence_row_hashes": [<sha256: hashes from the input>] }

The runner (build_agent_app from the Deployment Guide §10.1) validates the LLM response against the output schema before returning. Schema-violation responses are rejected and re-tried with a corrective system message.

28.3 Tool-Selection Logic

Within an agent's /run call, the agent has access to a scoped MCP tool catalog. Tool selection follows a three-step pattern:

  1. Read the decision-panel mart first. This is the agent's primary fact source. The MCP tool name maps to the agent's question shape (§13.1.1). Always called.
  2. Optional read tools for context — episodic memory (Redis-backed similarity retrieval, §25.3), cross-Service tool calls (§17.2). Called when the agent's prompt indicates the context is needed (a low-confidence first-pass might trigger an episodic-memory lookup; a cross-Service composition might trigger the data-owner Service's tool).
  3. Decision-emit or HITL-stage tool last (Composer archetype only). Reads-only agents (Analyst, Specialist, Briefer) never call decision-emit tools directly — that's the Composer's responsibility.
Best Practice · tool count

Keep an agent's accessible tool count small — ideally 2–4 tools per agent. Many agent failures trace to tool over-selection (the LLM picks a less-relevant tool because more options confuse selection). The MCP manifest (§13.3) is where you constrain this; the runner enforces it.

28.4 Few-Shot Exemplar Curation — Year by Year

The few-shot block (prompt section 3) is the single biggest predictor of agent quality. Its curation evolves through the three-year arc from §25.1.

YearSourceSelectionRefresh cadence
Year 1 · instinctsHand-authored synthetic exemplars by the agent maintainer3–5 hand-picked covering the expected varianceManually, when prompts evolve
Year 2 · recordTop-N HITL-approved decisions from LEDGER, ranked by realized KPI lift in attribution_outcome5–7 selected by per-persona acceptance + per-category coverageNightly batch; freezes the next morning's exemplar set per Service per persona
Year 3 · superagentRedis-backed similarity retrieval at runtime: nearest-N decisions to the current input across the LEDGER training corpusTop-K by RedisJSON / RediSearch similarity scorePer-decision · <100ms retrieval inside the agent's /run call
ADR · architecture decision
ADR-028-02 · 2026-05-09 · accepted

Decision: Few-shot exemplars come from LEDGER decisions that produced realized KPI lift, not from a hand-curated "gold set."

Why: A hand-curated gold set encodes the maintainer's prior beliefs about good decisions. LEDGER + attribution mart encodes what the operator-plus-system actually decided and the realized outcome. The latter is what we want to reproduce; the former is at best a Year-1 placeholder. Once Year-2 LEDGER is rich enough, the hand-curated set becomes deadweight.

Caveat: Selection bias — exemplars are drawn from approved decisions only, which over-represents the operator's prior preferences. The Year-3 episodic-memory retrieval mitigates this by including declined decisions as negative few-shots in some prompt variants.

28.5 Evaluation Framework — Three Gates

Promoting a new agent version (Layer 3 SemVer bump per Deployment Guide §12.1) is gated by three evaluation phases, each catching a different class of regression.

28.5.1 Gate 1 — Golden-set regression (deterministic, no skip)

Every agent has a frozen golden set of (input, expected-output) pairs maintained by the agent maintainer. The CI pipeline runs the candidate agent against the golden set and fails the build on any mismatch. Golden sets are augmented (never edited) when prompts intentionally change behavior.

Golden-set size: ~30–60 pairs per agent. Coverage: every output-schema field, every branch in the agent's reasoning, every persona-specific adaptation, every cross-Service composition path.

28.5.2 Gate 2 — LLM-as-judge sample (statistical)

A separate "judge" LLM evaluates a sample of 200–500 (input, candidate-output, prior-version-output) triples. The judge is given the rubric (e.g., "for The Pricing Agent, prefer the response with stronger elasticity reasoning, valid markdown_pct within constraints, and shorter reasoning at equal quality"). Statistical preference for the candidate > 60% gates the next phase.

The judge is not the same model family as the candidate (avoid same-family bias). Per the Deployment Guide §10.4 cosign attestation, the judge model and rubric version are recorded as part of the candidate's training-corpus attestation.

28.5.3 Gate 3 — Live shadow + canary (operational)

The candidate runs in shadow on a percentage of live traffic — receives the same input as the production agent, but its output is recorded only, not committed. Shadow telemetry shows divergence: how often does the candidate disagree with the production version, and on which decision class? Material divergence (> 5% on high-cost decisions) triggers maintainer review before canary.

Canary follows the Deployment Guide §15.2 traffic-split pattern (95/5 → 25 → 50 → 100). At each stage, Microsoft.Insights queries verify cost-per-decision, safety-verdict distribution, and HITL-acceptance-rate match production within bounds.

Warning · agent regression is silent

Container Apps will report 100% healthy and Microsoft Defender for Containers will report no anomalies for an agent whose reasoning has regressed. The three evaluation gates are how regression gets caught — there is no platform-level detector for "the agent's recommendations got worse." Skip the gates and you're shipping silent regressions to operators who will quietly stop trusting the system.

28.6 When to Fork an Image vs Evolve a Prompt

The Deployment Guide §11.2 establishes that agent images are reused across Services and parameterized by Service config. Within a Service, when does a prompt evolution warrant a new image vs a same-image config update?

ChangeMechanismLayer 3 version impact
New few-shot exemplars (Year-2 nightly refresh)Config update · Microsoft.KeyVault secret rotationNone · runtime read
Tone / persona adjustment in system identityConfig update or minor image bumpPatch
New constraint added to instruction (e.g., "minimum 2% above competitor")Minor image bumpMinor
New output-schema fieldMinor image bump · MCP tool output schema also evolvesMinor
Different reasoning architecture (e.g., chain-of-thought → tree-of-thought)Major image bump · evaluation gates rerun against full golden setMajor
Different model family (e.g., GPT-class → Anthropic-class)Major image bump · also potentially new agent identity (the-analyst-claude vs the-analyst-gpt) for parallel rolloutMajor or new agent

Key Takeaways

  • Four archetypes — Analyst · Specialist · Composer · Briefer — cover every agent in the catalog. Pick the archetype first.
  • Prompt structure is uniform: identity · schema-shaped context · few-shot · instruction · output schema. Runner validates the JSON.
  • Tool count per agent stays small (2–4); few-shot exemplar source evolves Year-1 hand-curated → Year-2 LEDGER-mined → Year-3 Redis-retrieved.
  • Three evaluation gates — golden set · LLM-as-judge · live shadow + canary — catch regression that platform health checks cannot.

Service Drills

  1. Pick one agent from your favorite Service in Part VI. Identify which of the four archetypes it is and verify by reading its prompt structure (in the MVP repo). If it doesn't match cleanly, the agent is either misclassified or carrying responsibility from a second archetype.
  2. Build a 30-pair golden set for one agent. Run it through the agent. How many mismatches? Each mismatch is either a prompt-quality bug, a golden-set bug, or a real bound-of-the-agent issue worth flagging.
  3. Inspect the LEDGER for one Service over the last 30 days. Pick the top-5 highest-realized-KPI-lift decisions for one persona. Would those make better Year-2 few-shots than what's currently in the prompt?
Chapter 29

Performance and Cost Modeling

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Sizes the Microsoft platform footprint for three workload scenarios — Small, Mid, Enterprise — with monthly cost estimates.
  • Maps cost levers per tier (Bronze ingest · Silver build · Gold materialization · agent runtime · LEDGER · Redis · orchestration).
  • Establishes when to right-size up and when to prune — the cost-engineering mental model.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • Three sized scenarios: Small (1 tenant · 10K events/day · 1 Service) · Mid (1 tenant · 5M events/day · 3 Services · 250 stores) · Enterprise (1 tenant · 100M events/day · 8 Services · multi-region).
  • Layer 1 platform footprint dominates Small. Microsoft Fabric capacity dominates Mid. LLM-call cost-per-decision dominates Enterprise.
  • Right-size when per-decision latency exceeds the sub-200ms Gold target or when Microsoft Fabric F-SKU saturates above 70% sustained.
  • Prune when Container Apps consumption replicas idle ≥80% of the day or when LEDGER chain growth exceeds Azure SQL DB performance tier headroom.

29.1 The Three Sizing Scenarios

The catalog spans engagements from a single-Service pilot to multi-region multi-Service enterprise rollouts. Three scenarios bracket the range:

DimensionSmallMidEnterprise

HITL approvals/day~10~400~6,000
Operators5–1050–100500+

LEDGER row growth/year~50K~3M~80M
Typical clientPilot · single-banner retailerMid-market chain · regional payerTier-1 retailer · global manufacturer · national payer

The Layer 1 substrate from the Deployment Guide §4 is sized once per tenant. Costs change with scenario but the resource list is invariant.

ComponentSmallMidEnterprise
Microsoft.ContainerRegistry · Premium1 region · ~$170/mo1 region · ~$170/mo3 regions geo-rep · ~$510/mo

The Layer 1 cost grows sub-linearly with the scenario size — that's the amortization argument from the Deployment Guide §9.1. Adding a fourth, fifth, sixth Service to a Mid-sized tenant doesn't materially change Layer 1 cost; only the workspace and security posture grow.

29.3 Microsoft Fabric Capacity

Microsoft Fabric capacity (F-SKU) sizes the Lakehouse + Warehouse + RTI + Pipelines + Notebooks holistically. F-SKUs follow a 2× cost step at each tier; the hard part is choosing the tier that gives ~30% headroom over peak load without over-buying.

ScenarioF-SKU recommendationMonthly capacity cost (pay-as-you-go)Drives
SmallF4~$520/moBronze ingest + Silver builds + 1 Service Gold marts. Eventstream for any streaming Bronze sources.
MidF32~$4,200/moMulti-Service Silver builds in parallel · Direct Lake serving · RTI eventstream rate to ~5K events/sec sustained.
EnterpriseF128 (single region) or 2× F64 (multi-region)~$16,800/mo to ~$17,200/moSustained 50K events/sec RTI · 8-Service Silver parallelism · sub-100ms Gold serving · Sentinel correlation.
Best Practice · F-SKU sizing

Microsoft Fabric F-SKU is reservation-eligible at 1-year and 3-year terms. Mid and Enterprise scenarios should reserve at 1-year (15–25% saving); Small scenarios should stay pay-as-you-go until usage stabilizes (~3 months of telemetry). Reserve too early and you're locked into a tier you may need to outgrow; reserve too late and you've left 20% on the table for a year.

29.4 Per-Service Layer 2 Sizing

Per-Service costs scale with decision volume. Microsoft.App/containerApps consumption pricing (Mid + Enterprise mix Consumption with Dedicated D4 for The Operations Lead) governs the agent-runtime line.

Component (per Service)SmallMidEnterprise
Container Apps · agents (4–6 apps)~$110/mo~$680/mo~$4,200/mo
Logic Apps Hybrid orchestrator~$40/mo~$240/mo~$1,800/mo
Azure Cache for Redis · Premium P1P1 ($380/mo, shared by all Services)P2 ($770/mo, shared)P3 ($1,500/mo, shared)
Azure SQL Database · LEDGER (S2 → P2 → P4)S2 · ~$75/moP2 · ~$930/moP4 · ~$1,860/mo
Azure Database for PostgreSQL Flexible Server (DEV) · Storage + Cosmos DB (PROD)~$80/mo~$540/mo~$3,600/mo
Per-Service runtime subtotal~$305/mo~$1,460/mo~$11,460/mo
Note · LLM call cost

The biggest single line item at Enterprise scale is not in the Microsoft platform price list — it's the LLM API call cost per agent decision. At ~80K decisions/day × ~5 LLM calls per decision × ~$0.0008 per call (frontier model, mid-2026 pricing) ≈ ~$320/day or ~$9,600/mo per Service. Aggregate across 8 Services and the LLM line approaches the Microsoft Fabric line. Cost-per-decision recorded in LEDGER (§25.2) is what makes this measurable, not estimated.

29.5 Total Monthly Cost Bands

Layer 1 + Microsoft Fabric capacity + per-Service runtimes + LLM calls. These are bands, not quotes — actual numbers depend on region, reservation discounts, peak-vs-baseline ratio, and the LLM model family in use.

ScenarioMicrosoft platform infraLLM API estimateTotal monthly cost band
Small (1 Service)~$1,200/mo~$60/mo~$1,250–$1,500/mo
Mid (3 Services)~$10,500/mo~$3,600/mo~$13,500–$16,000/mo
Enterprise (8 Services)~$120,000/mo~$76,000/mo~$190,000–$230,000/mo

Per-Service unit-economics-relevant view:

ScenarioPer-Service per monthPer decision (avg)
Small~$1,400~$0.93
Mid~$5,000~$0.06
Enterprise~$26,000~$0.011

The cost-per-decision drops two orders of magnitude from Small to Enterprise. That's the engagement-economics curve that makes APEX defensible commercially — first Service expensive, fortieth Service cheap.

29.6 Latency Targets and Right-Sizing Triggers

Performance is measured per tier. Sub-target performance is the right-size signal.

Tier · OperationTargetRight-size triggerRight-size action
Bronze ingest (streaming)< 5s end-to-endp95 > 12s for 30 minF-SKU bump or Eventstream parallelism
Silver build (per entity)< 8 minWall-clock > 18 min consistentlyF-SKU bump or partition-aware build
Gold materialization< 10 min for the most expensive martMicrosoft Fabric capacity sustained > 70%F-SKU bump or mart redenormalization
Agent /run · MCP Gold read< 200 msp95 > 800 msRedis Premium tier bump · or mart shape problem (Ch 11)
Agent /run · LLM call total< 8 sp95 > 20 sPrompt size · model choice · or Container Apps replica scaling
HITL approval round-trip< 30 s p95 (operator action time)p95 > 90 sPersona attention · adaptive-card simplification · not a platform fix
LEDGER append< 50 ms p99p99 > 200 msAzure SQL DB performance tier bump
LEDGER chain verify (week)< 2 min> 8 minIndex rebuild · or hash-checkpoint cadence (§25.2)

29.7 Cost Optimization Patterns

  1. Container Apps Consumption + scale-to-zero. All read-only agents (Analyst · Specialist · Briefer) on Consumption profile. Only the Composer (Operations Lead) on Dedicated D4 because steady-state HITL throughput justifies it. Saves 30–50% on agent runtime vs all-Dedicated.
  2. Microsoft Fabric capacity reservation (1y at Mid, 3y at Enterprise). 15–25% off pay-as-you-go.
  3. Log Analytics retention tiers. Hot 30d · Cold 90d · Archive 7y for regulated workloads. Archive tier is ~5× cheaper than hot.
  4. Microsoft Sentinel data ingestion volume control. Send only LEDGER mirror events + correlated authority logs to Sentinel. Skip raw container logs (already in LAW). Saves 40–60% of Sentinel cost.
  5. Shared Redis across Services in a tenant. One Premium Redis (P1 / P2 / P3) shared across all Services with key-prefix isolation. Saves 70%+ vs per-Service Redis.
  6. LLM model right-sizing. Frontier models for the Specialist (Pricing Agent · Clinical Reviewer); lighter models for the Analyst (severity scoring); rules-based path for the Briefer (templated digest with model-augmented narrative). Mixed-model agents save 40–70% of LLM-call cost vs all-frontier.

29.8 When to Prune

Cost optimization isn't only sizing-up. Three prune signals:

  1. Container Apps replicas idle ≥80% of the day. Reduce maxReplicas · or move from Dedicated to Consumption profile. Often saves $200–800/mo per Service.
  2. Bronze tables that no Silver build reads. Bronze retention is "≥1 year STAGE, ≥7 years PROD" (§1.3.1) but a Bronze table that's ingested and never read is dead weight on Microsoft Fabric capacity.
  3. MCP tools called < 3× per quarter. Either the tool is misnamed (LLMs don't pick it because the description is unclear), redundant (another tool is doing the same job), or genuinely unused. Audit and prune. Tool-catalog over-population degrades agent tool-selection performance (Ch 28).

Key Takeaways

  • Three sized scenarios (Small / Mid / Enterprise) bracket the cost-and-performance envelope. Cost-per-decision drops two orders of magnitude from Small to Enterprise.
  • Layer 1 amortizes; Microsoft Fabric capacity is the dominant Mid-scenario lever; LLM API calls dominate Enterprise.
  • Sub-200ms Gold reads, <8s LLM calls, <50ms LEDGER appends — eight latency targets to hold for the system to feel responsive.
  • Six cost-optimization patterns and three prune signals — apply them after the first ~3 months of telemetry, not at deployment time.

Service Drills

  1. For your live engagement, calculate the actual cost-per-decision from the LEDGER's cost_per_decision_usd column over the last 30 days. How does it compare to the §29.5 unit-economics row for your scenario? Where's the variance coming from?
  2. Pull the latency table from §29.6 and compare each row against your tenant's Microsoft.Insights p95s for the last week. Any sub-target rows? Pick the worst and propose the right-size action.
  3. Audit your tenant's MCP tool catalog: invocation count per tool over 90 days. Any tool below the prune threshold? Propose retirement (with replacement path documented if there's still a need).
Part IX

Agent Stack & Services Era

The previous parts established the architecture and the practitioner motion. This part zooms out to two broader perspectives the architect needs but couldn't be told earlier in the book without context. Chapter 30 is the Service envelope view from the Sellers Guide Runtime Addendum — six concentric envelopes (Commercial · Scope · Data · Schema · Trust · Cost) governing every Service — and the Experience plane (Layer 08 of the APEX stacked architecture) where humans meet agents through Microsoft 365 Copilot, Copilot Studio, Power BI Direct Lake, and Microsoft Teams adaptive cards. Chapter 31 is the Microsoft agent stack — Semantic Kernel, Agent 365, Agent-to-Agent (A2A), Copilot Studio, Azure AI Foundry — and how each composes within an APEX Service. Chapter 32 is the deep contrast: what makes APEX agent context different from raw LLM SDKs (OpenAI Apps SDK, Claude Agent SDK, LangGraph, AutoGen). Chapter 33 is the organizational shift — moving from the application era to the services era — that delivery leads must navigate while the architecture is being adopted.

Chapters in this Part

  • Chapter 30Service Envelopes & the Experience Plane

  • Chapter 32Agent Context · Secure · Audited · Decision-Driving
