Chapter 30

Service Envelopes & the Experience Plane

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Walks the six concentric Service envelopes (Commercial · Scope · Data · Schema · Trust · Cost) from the Sellers Guide Runtime Addendum and operationalizes each in Microsoft platform terms.
  • Maps the Experience plane — Layer 08 of the APEX stacked architecture — where human personas meet APEX agents through Microsoft 365 Copilot, Copilot Studio, Power BI Direct Lake, and Microsoft Teams.
  • Establishes how envelopes constrain the experience plane and how the experience plane realizes the envelopes for operators.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • Six envelopes nest from outside-in: Commercial (money & time, Wave 1/2/3) → Scope (which Services/agents/tools/Gold views/KPIs) → Data (what the agent may read/write, enforced at MCP + Microsoft Purview + Microsoft Entra ID).
  • Three envelopes overlay the nest: Schema (canonical-entity subset · derives the data envelope), Trust (classification + identity + DLP + HITL · governs everything above), Cost (FinOps boundary · metered per service_code, bound to cost_per_decision).
  • Layer 08 Experience plane is where humans meet agents: Microsoft 365 Copilot · Copilot Studio · Power BI Direct Lake · Microsoft Teams adaptive cards. Each surface is identity-scoped to the signed-in principal.
  • The envelopes constrain the experience plane: a Microsoft Teams approver only sees what the Trust envelope allows; Copilot only invokes MCP tools the Scope envelope registers.

30.1 The Six Service Envelopes

The Sellers Guide §6.12 (Runtime Addendum) introduced the envelope framework as the structural shape of every commercial offering. The Services Guide operationalizes it: each envelope is enforced by specific Microsoft platform components, and the architectural choices in Parts II–VII are deliberate satisfactions of these constraints.

30.1.1 Commercial Envelope · the outermost

The money & time boundary. Wave 1 (Foundation · 4–8 weeks · ~$500K–$2M). Wave 2 (Pilot · 6–15 months · ~$5–15M). Wave 3 (Scale & Fuse · multi-year · ~$8–30M). Every Service deployed in a tenant carries a commercial envelope; the envelope sets the maximum cost the Service can incur and the maximum time before realized KPI lift must be visible.

Microsoft platform realization: Microsoft Cost Management aggregation by apex:service-code tag; Microsoft Fabric capacity reservation pricing (Ch 29 §29.3); per-tenant Bicep parameters expressing Wave selection. Cost dashboards in Power BI Direct Lake surface the Commercial envelope's burn rate to the engagement lead.

30.1.2 Scope Envelope · nested in Commercial

The functional-scope boundary. Within the money/time bound, which Services run · which agents compose those Services · which MCP tools each agent may call · which Gold views are exposed · which KPIs are committed. The Scope envelope determines what the Service does; the Commercial envelope determines how much it can do.

Microsoft platform realization: Service Bicep modules deploy only the Container Apps named in the Scope envelope (Deployment Guide Ch 7); the MCP manifest (§13.3) lists only the Scope-permitted tools; Gold marts are materialized only for Scope-named questions; Microsoft Purview registers only Scope-relevant assets.

30.1.3 Data Envelope · nested in Scope

The read/write boundary. What Bronze sources may land · what Silver entities are accessible · what Gold marts are queryable · what decision tables the Service may write to · what cross-Service tools may be invoked. The Data envelope is the most fine-grained of the nested set.

Enforced at three Microsoft platform layers: MCP tools (each tool's input/output schema is the data interface boundary) + Microsoft Purview (sensitivity labels, classification rules, asset-level access policies) + Microsoft Entra ID (managed-identity scoping; the four user-assigned identities from Deployment Guide §4.3 each carry different Data-envelope grants). The triple enforcement is what makes Data-envelope violation structurally impossible — no single component is the whole defense.

30.1.4 Schema Envelope · derives Data

The canonical-entity subset the Service depends on. RC-E2E-03 derives from SCML.Inventory · SCML.Lot · MERML.Markdown · MERML.Elasticity · MERML.Competitor · PROML.Pricing · PROML.DiscountRule. HLS-E2E-04 derives from EHRML.Encounter · EHRML.Procedure · CMSML.Authorization · CMSML.Member. The Schema envelope is the subset of the §2.1 canonical schema library the Service participates in.

Microsoft platform realization: Per-Service Microsoft Fabric workspace contains exactly the Silver entities the Schema envelope names. Cross-Service entity reuse via OneLake Shortcut is permitted only when both Schema envelopes intersect on the entity (and the data-owner Service grants access via Microsoft.Authorization role assignment per Ch 17).

30.1.5 Trust Envelope · governs all above

The governance overlay. PII tier classification (Ch 7) · Microsoft Entra ID conditional-access policies · DLP (Microsoft Purview Information Protection) · HITL gating policy. Trust propagates downward through every envelope: a Tier-4 PHI field at the bottom of the Data envelope causes Microsoft Defender for Cloud alerts at the Commercial envelope if exfiltration is suspected.

Microsoft platform realization: Microsoft Purview sensitivity labels (Ch 10) · Microsoft Entra ID Conditional Access (HLS Tier-4 dual-control · §27.8) · Microsoft Defender for Cloud (subscription-level alerting) · Microsoft Sentinel UEBA (cross-identity behavioral anomaly detection). The Trust envelope is the only envelope with platform-level enforcement spanning every Microsoft service in scope.

30.1.6 Cost Envelope · the FinOps boundary

The per-decision cost ceiling. Tokens consumed by LLM calls · Microsoft Fabric Capacity Units (CUs) consumed by Silver/Gold builds · MCP invocation count · Microsoft Storage I/O · Microsoft Network egress. Metered per service_code, bound to cost_per_decision on every LEDGER row (§25.2). Service P&L is a query, not a reconciliation — pull SUM(cost_per_decision) grouped by service_code and the answer is exact, not estimated.

Microsoft platform realization: Microsoft Cost Management exports tagged by apex:service-code (Ch 29 §29.4) · LEDGER's cost_per_decision column populated at runtime · Microsoft Fabric Capacity Metrics app for CU breakdown · Power BI Direct Lake "Service P&L by month" semantic model.

ADR · architecture decision
ADR-030-01 · 2026-05-09 · accepted

Decision: Cost is treated as a first-class envelope, not a measurement after the fact. cost_per_decision is a mandatory column on every LEDGER row; apex:service-code is a mandatory tag on every Microsoft platform resource a Service deploys.

Why: Reconciling cost after the fact (the "FinOps reconciliation" practice common in cloud-native applications) is incompatible with W3 outcome-based commercial envelopes. The W3 contract requires a defensible per-decision cost at decision time, not three months later in a billing review. The audit row records cost; the audit row is queryable; therefore P&L is a query.

Trade-off rejected: Cost-attribution as a downstream Microsoft Cost Management report. Easier to implement (no LEDGER schema change); incompatible with the commercial commitment. The decision is structural: the Cost envelope is built into the audit row, not bolted onto Microsoft Cost Management.

30.2 The Experience Plane · Layer 08 · Where Humans Meet Agents

The APEX stacked architecture is eight layers. Layers 01–07 are everything this book has covered: data plane, semantic plane, agent plane, runtime plane, Service plane, deployment plane, governance plane, learning plane. Layer 08 is the experience plane — the surfaces where human personas (operators, approvers, executives, analysts) actually interact with APEX agents and the Services they compose.

Four Microsoft surfaces realize Layer 08:

30.2.1 Microsoft 365 Copilot · persona-shaped, channel-adaptive

Microsoft 365 Copilot surfaces APEX agent outputs inside Outlook · Microsoft Teams · Word · Excel. A Merchandising Director asking Copilot in Outlook "what markdowns are pending my approval?" gets a Copilot response that invokes RC-E2E-03's stage_markdown_approval via MCP — same agent, same Service, different surface.

Every Copilot surface is identity-scoped: the signed-in Microsoft Entra ID principal determines what the user sees. Daniel Chen sees pending markdowns for his stores; Marisol Reyes sees excursion alerts for hers. Identity-scoping is enforced at the Microsoft Graph layer; the APEX MCP host receives the scoped identity claim and applies it to the Data envelope.

30.2.2 Microsoft Copilot Studio · custom copilots embedded in LOB apps

Microsoft Copilot Studio is the low-code surface for building custom copilots. Where the operator workflow lives inside a non-Microsoft line-of-business surface (Salesforce · Workday · ServiceNow · a homegrown app), Copilot Studio hosts a custom copilot that speaks MCP to the same Gold-view tools. One governance plane, many experiences.

Why this matters for APEX: the Service architecture (Layers 01–07) is invariant; the experience the operator sees is a deliberate choice per persona per channel. A Store Operations Lead might prefer Microsoft Teams adaptive cards (next subsection); a Field Service Dispatcher might prefer a Salesforce-embedded custom copilot. Both invoke the same MCP tools; both write to the same LEDGER; both stay within the Service envelopes.

30.2.3 Microsoft Teams · HITL approvals via adaptive cards

The HITL stage primitive (§13.1.3 · §15.4 · §27.8) renders in production as a Microsoft Teams adaptive card. Approvers see the card in a Teams channel · approve · modify · or deny inline. The decision writes back through the agent's manifest into the LEDGER row, closing the workflow's HITL pause (§14.2).

Adaptive cards are identity-bound: the approver's Microsoft Entra ID identity is captured at card-resolution time and recorded as approved_by in the decision row (e.g., MERML.Markdown.approved_by). For Tier-4 PHI dual-control unlocks, two adaptive cards in two Teams channels (or two Microsoft Entra ID principals' personal queues) gate the workflow until both approve.

30.2.4 Power BI Direct Lake · semantic models over Gold

Operators and executives consume the system's outputs through Power BI dashboards built on semantic models served via Microsoft Fabric Direct Lake. Sub-second queries · zero data duplication · audit-row reviews live here. The Steering Committee and the CFO walk attribution-mart rollups in Power BI before signing W3 outcome-based milestones.

The Gold marts the agents read (§11.2) are the same Gold marts Power BI semantic models materialize from. One Gold tier, two consumers — agents via MCP, humans via Direct Lake. The convergence is the key economic insight: building Gold once serves both consumers, and the unification is what makes the §29.5 cost-per-decision math defensible.

30.3 How Envelopes Constrain the Experience Plane

The two abstractions in this chapter — six envelopes and four experience surfaces — are not independent. The envelopes constrain what the experience plane can show; the experience plane realizes the envelopes for the operator.

EnvelopeConstraint on the Experience planeMicrosoft platform mechanism
CommercialCost-aware UI · "this Copilot answer cost $0.18" optionally surfaced for high-cost queries · cost dashboards visible to engagement lead onlyMicrosoft Cost Management role assignments · Power BI row-level security
ScopeCopilot only invokes Scope-listed MCP tools · Power BI dashboards only show Scope-listed Gold views · Custom copilots cannot reach beyond ScopePer-Service MCP manifest · Microsoft Purview asset registration · Microsoft Entra ID app registration scopes
DataPersona sees only the data their identity is granted · Tokens stay tokens unless an HITL pii-unlock fires · Power BI semantic model honors RLSMicrosoft Entra ID conditional access · Microsoft Purview sensitivity labels · Microsoft.KeyVault JIT pii-unlock
SchemaCross-Service Power BI dashboards must compose at the data-owner's permission level · Copilot grounding stays within the Schema-envelope subsetOneLake Shortcut + Microsoft.Authorization role assignments at workspace scope
TrustHITL adaptive cards include the Trust-envelope policy summary · approver's Microsoft Entra ID identity captured · DLP runs on the Copilot outputMicrosoft Purview Information Protection · Microsoft Defender for Cloud Apps · Microsoft Sentinel UEBA
CostPer-decision cost surfaced to the engagement lead's Power BI · cost-bound queries blocked when Cost envelope exhaustedMicrosoft Cost Management thresholds · Microsoft Fabric Capacity throttling

The reverse — how the experience plane realizes envelopes for operators — is what the human actually sees:

  • An operator never sees a token they shouldn't (Trust envelope realized via tokens-in-Bronze + JIT unlock).
  • An approver never approves a decision the Service shouldn't make (Scope envelope realized via Microsoft Teams adaptive-card content restrictions).
  • An executive never quotes a cost they can't reproduce (Cost envelope realized via LEDGER-backed Power BI P&L).
  • A regulator never finds an audit gap (Trust envelope realized via Microsoft Purview lineage walks).

Key Takeaways

  • Six Service envelopes: Commercial (money/time) · Scope (functional-scope) · Data (read/write) · Schema (canonical-entity subset) · Trust (governance overlay) · Cost (FinOps boundary).
  • Each envelope is realized by specific Microsoft platform components — none stays at the level of policy slogans.
  • Layer 08 Experience plane has four surfaces: Microsoft 365 Copilot · Copilot Studio · Microsoft Teams adaptive cards · Power BI Direct Lake. Each is identity-scoped to the signed-in Microsoft Entra ID principal.
  • The Gold tier serves two consumers — agents via MCP, humans via Direct Lake — and the convergence is the cost-per-decision economics from §29.5.

Catalog Drills

  1. For RC-E2E-03 in your Lab tenant, draw the six envelopes with concrete numbers (Commercial $ band · Scope tool list · Data Bronze sources · Schema entities · Trust PII tiers · Cost monthly run-rate). Compare to the §29.5 sized-scenario table.
  2. Pick one Microsoft 365 Copilot persona (Daniel Chen Merchandising Director). Trace what they would see in Outlook Copilot when asking "what's pending?" — which MCP tool fires, which Gold mart it reads, which Trust-envelope tokens stay tokens.
  3. Sketch a Microsoft Teams adaptive-card payload for the RC-E2E-03 markdown HITL approval. Identify the fields that come from the Gold mart, the fields from the Trust envelope (tokens vs unlocked PII), and the approver-identity capture mechanism.
Chapter 31

The Microsoft Agent Stack

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Inventories the Microsoft agent stack components — Semantic Kernel · Agent 365 · Agent-to-Agent (A2A) · Copilot Studio · Azure AI Foundry — and what each contributes.
  • Maps each component to where it composes within an APEX Service (agent runtime · inter-agent calls · operator surface · inference plane).
  • Establishes the rule: APEX picks which component for which job, not which framework wins. Multiple components compose; none replaces the others.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • Five Microsoft agent components plug into APEX Services: Semantic Kernel (agent runtime SDK · alternative to plain FastAPI) · Agent 365 (Microsoft 365 surface integration) · A2A (agent-to-agent protocol · complement to MCP) · Copilot Studio (low-code custom copilots over MCP) · Azure AI Foundry (model hosting + agent runtime · the inference plane).
  • MCP (Ch 3, Ch 13) is the agent↔tool protocol; A2A is the agent↔agent protocol. They complement, not compete.
  • APEX picks components per job: Semantic Kernel for agents needing function-calling planners, Agent 365 for M365 surface integration, A2A for inter-agent within a workflow, Copilot Studio for non-Microsoft LOB embedding, Foundry as the always-on inference plane.
  • None of these components replace the Service architecture (Layers 01–07); they realize it in different surfaces and runtimes.

31.1 The Stack at a Glance

The Microsoft agent stack is not one framework; it is five components that compose. Each addresses a different concern. APEX uses each where the concern fits.

ComponentConcernAPEX role
Semantic KernelCross-language agent SDK · function calling · planners · memory connectorsAgent runtime SDK alternative to plain FastAPI · used when planner-style reasoning is needed
Microsoft 365 Agents (Agent 365)Surface integration with Outlook · Microsoft Teams · Word · Excel · PowerPointBring APEX agent outputs into the Microsoft 365 surfaces operators already live in
Agent-to-Agent (A2A)Standardized protocol for agent collaboration across runtime boundariesInter-agent calls within a Service workflow · cross-Service composition through a common protocol
Microsoft Copilot StudioLow-code custom copilot builder · plugs into any MCP-speaking backendBuild Service-specific custom copilots · embed in non-Microsoft LOB apps · governance plane stays unified
Azure AI FoundryModel hosting · evaluation · agent runtime · groundingThe inference plane · where the LLM calls happen · evaluation framework integration (Ch 28 §28.5)

31.2 Microsoft Semantic Kernel · Cross-Language Agent SDK

Semantic Kernel is Microsoft's open-source SDK for building AI agents in .NET, Python, and Java. Its primitives — plugins, functions, planners, memory connectors — give an agent a structured way to reason over tools and accumulated context.

How APEX uses it:

  • Agent runtime alternative. The MVP's build_agent_app(runner=..., agent_id=...) contract from Deployment Guide §10.1 produces a FastAPI app exposing /run and /healthz. For .NET-shop clients or for agents needing planner-style reasoning, the same contract is satisfiable by a Semantic Kernel agent in C# or Python — the HTTP surface is identical, the LEDGER row shape is identical, the MCP tool consumption is identical. The runtime SDK changes; the agent contract does not.
  • Function-calling planners. Where The Pricing Agent's reasoning needs to compose multiple MCP tool calls in a derived sequence (read-elasticity · evaluate · read-similar-decisions · refine · emit-recommendation), Semantic Kernel's FunctionChoiceBehavior.Auto planner can pick tools dynamically. The Year-2 LEDGER few-shot exemplars (Ch 28 §28.4) feed the planner's context.
  • Memory connectors. Semantic Kernel's memory abstraction adapts to Azure Cache for Redis (the §25.3 working/episodic memory). The agent's memory store is Redis · the connector is Semantic Kernel · the read pattern is identical regardless of underlying store.

When to choose Semantic Kernel over plain FastAPI: when the team's primary language is .NET; when the agent's reasoning genuinely needs a planner (most APEX agents are simpler than this); when the same agent must run in both Container Apps and a desktop / non-server context.

31.3 Microsoft 365 Agents · Surface Integration

Microsoft 365 Agents (the agent infrastructure that lives inside the Microsoft 365 ecosystem and that operators sometimes refer to as "Agent 365") brings APEX agent outputs into the Microsoft 365 surfaces — Outlook · Teams · Word · Excel · PowerPoint. The agent definition lives in the Microsoft 365 admin center and respects all of Microsoft 365's existing identity, compliance, and licensing models.

How APEX composes with it:

  • Surface registration. An APEX Service registers a Microsoft 365 Agent that points at the Service's MCP host as its tool source. From the Microsoft 365 user's perspective, asking Copilot in Outlook for "show me pending markdowns" produces a Copilot answer that invokes rc_e2e_03.stage_markdown_approval via the registered M365 Agent.
  • Identity flow-through. The Microsoft Entra ID identity of the signed-in user passes through Microsoft Graph to the M365 Agent to the MCP host. The agent's /run sees the user's Entra ID claims in its request context · scope-checks against the Service's Trust envelope · returns content the user is allowed to see.
  • HITL surfaces in Microsoft Teams. The HITL adaptive card from §15.4 / §16.2 is rendered by Microsoft 365 Agents when the workflow stages an approval. Same orchestration; native Teams surface.

When to use Microsoft 365 Agents vs Copilot Studio: M365 Agents for first-party Microsoft 365 surfaces (Outlook · Teams · Word). Copilot Studio for custom or non-Microsoft surfaces (next section). Both can speak MCP to the same Service.

31.4 Agent-to-Agent (A2A) · The Inter-Agent Protocol

Where MCP standardizes how an agent calls a tool, A2A standardizes how an agent calls another agent. The two protocols are complementary: an agent reads its data via MCP, then collaborates with peer agents via A2A, then writes its decision via MCP again.

How APEX uses A2A:

  • Within a Service workflow. The Operations Lead (Composer archetype, §28.1) needs the Specialist's proposal · the Demand Checker's forecast · the Finance Lead's commercial scoring before composing the HITL adaptive card. Classic implementation routes these through the orchestrator's parallel-step primitive (§14.2). With A2A, the Operations Lead can directly invoke peer agents under the same protocol, with the orchestrator providing only the workflow shell.
  • Cross-Service composition. RC-E2E-03's Pricing Agent invokes RC-E2E-09's Lot Provenance agent (cross-Service example from §17.4). The cross-Service call traverses A2A; the per-Service authorization (data-owner grants role assignment) holds through Microsoft Entra ID claims passed in the A2A envelope.
  • Multi-tenant agent collaboration is forbidden by ADR-025-01. A2A within a tenant: yes. A2A across tenants: no. The protocol is capable; the policy is the binding.

The choice between A2A and orchestrator-mediated calls (n8n / Logic Apps Hybrid) is per-pattern:

PatternUse orchestratorUse A2A
HITL gating · cross-Service scheduling · branch-on-result✓ (workflow shape needs the orchestrator)
Agent-pair collaboration mid-decision · low latency · same-Service✓
Parallel fan-out where each branch is independent✓
Synchronous reasoning across two agents that must converge before HITL✓ (orchestrator's parallel + merge is heavier)

31.5 Microsoft Copilot Studio · Custom Copilots over MCP

Copilot Studio is the low-code surface for building custom copilots. Where the operator's natural workflow lives in a non-Microsoft LOB application — Salesforce · Workday · ServiceNow · a homegrown app — Copilot Studio hosts a copilot embedded in that app, speaking MCP to the same Gold-view tools the M365 Copilot speaks to.

How APEX composes:

  • Per-Service custom copilots. Each Service can ship a Copilot Studio definition that registers the Service's MCP tools as its action set. The custom copilot is identity-scoped through Microsoft Entra ID app registration; it inherits the Service's Scope and Trust envelopes.
  • One governance plane, many experiences. The Trust envelope (§30.1.5) propagates regardless of the surface. Microsoft Purview tracks lineage from Bronze through to the Copilot Studio response. Microsoft Defender for Cloud Apps watches for anomalous prompt injection.
  • Same Year-1 → Year-3 trajectory. A Copilot Studio custom copilot still calls MCP tools, still emits LEDGER rows, still feeds the §25.3 Redis episodic memory. The maturation arc is invariant under surface choice.

31.6 Azure AI Foundry · The Inference Plane

Azure AI Foundry is where the LLM calls actually happen. Model hosting · grounding · evaluation framework · prompt-flow tooling. APEX's agent runtimes (FastAPI, Semantic Kernel) all eventually issue chat-completion requests to a model deployed in Azure AI Foundry.

How APEX uses Foundry:

  • Model deployments per Service. The Cost envelope (§30.1.6) constrains which model family each agent uses. Frontier models for the Specialist (Pricing · Clinical Reviewer); lighter models for the Analyst (severity scoring); rules-based path for the Briefer where viable. Foundry hosts the deployments; the agent code names them in its config (Microsoft.KeyVault secret · per Service).
  • Evaluation framework. Foundry's built-in evaluation tooling integrates with the §28.5 three-gate framework. Golden-set regression runs as a Foundry batch evaluation; LLM-as-judge sampling runs as a Foundry evaluator on shadow traffic; canary metrics export to Microsoft.OperationalInsights for the §28.5.3 telemetry-driven promotion gate.
  • Grounding to Microsoft Fabric Gold. Foundry's grounding feature lets the model reason over a specified data source. APEX uses Foundry grounding selectively — most agent reasoning is over Gold-mart MCP responses (the canonical APEX path), but for unstructured-document grounding (clinical guidelines for HLS-E2E-02 oncology CDS · regulatory text for ER-MN-02 environmental compliance), Foundry's grounding pulls from Microsoft Fabric OneLake unstructured stores into the agent's context window at runtime.

31.7 The Composition Pattern · A Worked Example

RC-E2E-03's cold-chain excursion workflow uses several stack components together. Trace the data flow once:

  1. Eventstream fires the excursion event (§5.3 KQL rule).
  2. Azure Logic Apps Hybrid orchestrator (§16) starts the workflow · emits LEDGER workflow.start row.
  3. The Analyst agent runs in Container Apps · runtime SDK is FastAPI (could be Semantic Kernel) · LLM call lands at Azure AI Foundry.
  4. The Analyst calls MCP tool rc_e2e_03.get_excursion_decision_panel · Gold mart query · row hash returned.
  5. The Analyst's severity score triggers the parallel fan-out. The Operations Lead invokes The Pricing Agent and The Demand Checker via A2A for synchronous collaboration · they converge on a recommendation.
  6. Operations Lead composes a Microsoft Teams adaptive card via Microsoft 365 Agents · Marisol Reyes sees it in her Teams channel.
  7. Marisol approves · the resolution flows back through Microsoft 365 Agents → Logic Apps → MCP commit_markdown_decision.
  8. Daniel Chen the Merchandising Director reviews the day's outcomes in Power BI Direct Lake over the same Gold marts the agents read. He can also ask Outlook Microsoft 365 Copilot for the day's KPI-attribution summary; the answer is grounded by another MCP call to rc_e2e_03.get_kpi_daily.

Six Microsoft components compose · the agent contract is invariant · LEDGER captures every step · the operator never knows which framework runs where.

Best Practice · pick per concern, not per fashion

The temptation to "standardize on Semantic Kernel" or "go all-in on Copilot Studio" recurs in every engagement. Resist. The five components solve five different concerns; standardizing on one means re-implementing the others poorly. Pick Semantic Kernel for planner-needing agents · M365 Agents for first-party surfaces · Copilot Studio for non-Microsoft LOB · A2A for agent collaboration · Foundry for inference. The agent contract (Ch 28) is what stays uniform; the realization is what flexes.

Key Takeaways

  • Five Microsoft agent components plug into APEX: Semantic Kernel (runtime SDK) · Microsoft 365 Agents (M365 surfaces) · A2A (inter-agent protocol) · Copilot Studio (custom copilots) · Azure AI Foundry (inference plane).
  • MCP and A2A are complementary protocols — agent↔tool and agent↔agent — not competitors.
  • APEX picks components per concern, not per framework loyalty. Six components in one workflow is normal.
  • The agent contract from Ch 28 (five-section prompt structure · output schema validation · LEDGER emission) is invariant under runtime/surface choice.

Catalog Drills

  1. For one Service in your engagement, identify which of the five Microsoft agent components compose into its delivery. Which agents are FastAPI and which are Semantic Kernel? Which surface gets M365 Agents and which gets Copilot Studio?
  2. Trace one cross-Service composition (e.g., RC-E2E-03 calling RC-E2E-09's Lot Provenance) and identify where MCP fires vs A2A fires. What's the latency cost of each protocol hop?
  3. Audit your Foundry model deployments: which agent uses which model family · what's the cost-per-decision Cost envelope budget · is there a smaller model that would still meet the §28.5.1 golden-set bar?
Chapter 32

Agent Context — Secure · Audited · Decision-Driving

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Defines the ten dimensions on which APEX agent context differs from raw LLM agent SDK calls (OpenAI Apps SDK · Claude Agent SDK · LangGraph · AutoGen).
  • Walks each dimension with the Microsoft platform mechanism that enforces the difference.
  • Establishes why these differences are not optional features but the architectural distinction between a chatbot wrapper and a decision system.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • Raw LLM agent SDKs give you tools, memory, and orchestration. APEX agent context adds identity · scope · audit · governance · attribution · cost containment · cross-Service authorization · maturation substrate — ten dimensions, each enforced by Microsoft platform components.
  • The difference is not a feature gap; it is the difference between a chatbot wrapper and a decision system that operators trust, regulators audit, and a CFO defends in a board meeting.
  • None of the ten dimensions can be retrofitted onto a raw LLM SDK without rebuilding most of APEX. Pick the architecture upfront or rebuild later.

32.1 The Comparison Frame

Raw LLM agent SDKs — OpenAI's Apps SDK, Anthropic's Claude Agent SDK, LangGraph, AutoGen, CrewAI, and the dozen other open-source frameworks released between 2024 and 2026 — all converge on a similar abstraction. An agent has a system prompt, can call tools, accumulates memory across turns, and produces output. This is enough to build chatbots. It is not enough to build decision systems.

The gap is the difference between "the agent answered a question" and "the agent committed a decision the operator approved that produced an attributable KPI outcome a regulator can reconstruct three years later." Both can call tools. Only the latter belongs in a Service.

Ten dimensions separate the two:

32.2 The Ten Dimensions of APEX Agent Context

#DimensionRaw LLM SDKAPEX agent context
1IdentityAPI keys or service-principal secrets in env varsMicrosoft Entra ID user-assigned managed identity · no secrets · per-Service scoped roles
2Tool boundaryFree-form function definitions in codeTyped MCP tools · Microsoft Purview-registered · Service-scoped manifest
3AuditRuntime logs · stdout · best-effort tracingLEDGER hash chain · cryptographically anchored to Microsoft.KeyVault HSM CMK · WORM-enforced
4GovernancePolicy in code · linting · reviewTrust envelope (§30.1.5) enforced by Microsoft Purview Information Protection · Microsoft Defender for Cloud · Microsoft Entra ID Conditional Access
5HITL patternConversational approval (chat-style) · operator answers in proseStructured stage primitive (§13.1.3) · Microsoft Teams adaptive card · approver identity captured · resume webhook closes the loop
6Outcome attributionThe agent answered; what happened next is an exercise for the readerLEDGER row carries attribution_outcome backfilled by attribution mart pipeline · Year-2 few-shots derive from realized KPI lift
7Cross-Service compositionTool calls cross any boundary code allowsData-owner Service grants role assignment to consumer's identity (§17.2 · ADR-025-01 · Deployment Guide §9.4) · cross-tenant forbidden by construction
8Cost containmentCost per call discoverable from billing logs days latercost_per_decision column on every LEDGER row · Cost envelope (§30.1.6) · Service P&L is a query
9Microsoft platform integrationOptional · the SDK works without itRequired · Microsoft.KeyVault HSM · Microsoft Sentinel correlation · Microsoft Purview lineage · Microsoft Defender protection · Microsoft Cost Management attribution all pre-wired
10Maturation substrateThe agent ships; further improvement is a manual prompt-tuning choreLEDGER + Azure Cache for Redis substrate (Ch 25) · Year-1 → Year-2 → Year-3 trajectory · Layer 3 SemVer + cosign attestation records training corpus by Merkle root

32.3 Walking the Dimensions

32.3.1 Identity · the secrets-free contract

A raw LLM agent typically authenticates with an API key in an environment variable. The key is rotatable in principle, accidentally checked into a repo in practice. Multiply by every backend system the agent's tools touch — database password, vendor API key, internal-service secret — and the agent is a credential aggregator.

An APEX agent authenticates as a Microsoft Entra ID user-assigned managed identity. No secret is on disk; the IMDS endpoint at 169.254.169.254 issues short-lived tokens. The four Layer 1 identities (runtime · MCP · ingest · pii-unlock per Deployment Guide §4.3) split blast radii; conditional-access policies apply per identity, not per Container App. Compromise containment is structural.

32.3.2 Tool boundary · MCP-typed, Purview-registered

A raw LLM agent's tools are functions defined in the host process. The function signature is the only contract; schema drift, scope creep, and access-policy gaps are all unenforced. APEX MCP tools are typed (input + output JSON schemas), registered (Microsoft Purview asset · versioned), and scoped (per-Service manifest determines which agents may call which tools). The boundary is platform-enforced, not code-enforced.

32.3.3 Audit · the cryptographic chain

Raw LLM SDKs produce runtime logs. They are searchable but not tamper-evident. The auditor's question "show me what the agent decided on date X with the data version Y" is answered by best-effort log reconstruction.

APEX LEDGER (§25.2) is hash-chained — every row's _hash is a SHA-256 over the row plus the prior row's hash. A periodic Microsoft.KeyVault HSM checkpoint signs the chain head. Tampering breaks the chain; verify_chain returns ok=false and Appendix H.6 fires Sev-1. The audit is cryptographic, not aspirational.

32.3.4 Governance · the Trust envelope made architectural

A raw LLM agent's governance is whatever the developer remembered to implement. APEX's Trust envelope (§30.1.5) propagates through every layer: PII tier classification at ingest (Ch 7) · Microsoft Purview sensitivity labels through lineage (Ch 10) · conditional-access policies on the managed identities (Ch 5 · cross-ref Deployment Guide §5) · Microsoft Defender for Cloud monitoring · Microsoft Sentinel UEBA. Governance is enforced at the platform level so that an individual agent can be developer-naive without being governance-naive.

32.3.5 HITL pattern · structured stage, not chat-style

A raw LLM agent's HITL pattern is "assistant says here's what I'd do, type yes if you approve." It works for low-stakes flows; it falls apart at scale, in regulated industries, and across personas with different approval authorities. APEX's HITL primitive (§13.1.3) stages a structured approval card via Microsoft Teams adaptive cards (§30.2.3), captures the approver's Microsoft Entra ID identity, supports dual-control for Tier-4 PHI (§7.3 · §27.8), and resumes the workflow on the typed approval webhook · not on a chat reply.

32.3.6 Outcome attribution · KPI is derived, not declared

Raw LLM SDKs end at the agent's response. APEX agent context closes the loop: every decision LEDGER row gets attribution_outcome backfilled by the attribution-mart pipeline (§11.2.4) within hours-to-weeks of the decision. Year-2 few-shots derive from realized KPI lift (Ch 28 §28.4 · ADR-028-02). The agent learns from outcomes, not from prompts revised on intuition.

32.3.7 Cross-Service composition · authorized outward

A raw LLM agent's tool catalog is whatever the host registers. There is no concept of "this tool is owned by another team and they grant me access." APEX cross-Service tools (§17.2 · Ch 31 §31.4 A2A) work by the data-owner Service granting Microsoft.Authorization role assignments to the consumer's MCP identity. Access flows outward from data ownership, never inward from consumer scope creep. The access graph is readable by walking from data owners · which is what auditors do.

32.3.8 Cost containment · the FinOps boundary on every row

Raw LLM SDKs treat cost as a billing-system concern reconciled monthly. APEX agent context records cost_per_decision on every LEDGER row at the moment the decision is committed. The Cost envelope (§30.1.6 · ADR-030-01) is a first-class structural constraint: the W3 outcome-based commercial envelope demands defensible per-decision cost at decision time, not in a quarterly review.

32.3.9 Microsoft platform integration · pre-wired, not optional

A raw LLM SDK works on any cloud or none. APEX agent context is structurally Microsoft-native: Microsoft.KeyVault for HSM key custody, Microsoft Sentinel for cross-source correlation, Microsoft Purview for lineage, Microsoft Defender for runtime protection, Microsoft Cost Management for attribution, Microsoft Entra ID for identity, Microsoft Fabric for the data tiers. The integration is the architecture; you cannot subtract Microsoft and have APEX.

This is not an accident. The two-contract Independence model (§30.1.5 · Sellers Guide §6.13 · Deployment Guide Ch 3) is built on Microsoft platform tenancy as the structural boundary. A "cloud-portable APEX" would be a different commercial structure with different audit guarantees · Microsoft-specificity is the binding.

32.3.10 Maturation substrate · LEDGER + Redis · Year 1→3

Raw LLM agents ship and then improve only by manual prompt revision. APEX agent context comes with the maturation substrate built in: LEDGER as durable long-term memory · Azure Cache for Redis as hot working/episodic memory · the §28.4 Year-1→Year-2→Year-3 evolution of the few-shot exemplars · the §25.7 superagent promotion pattern. The agent improves because the architecture trains it; the maintainer's job is curation and review, not from-scratch retraining.

32.4 What This Means at the Decision Moment

To make this concrete, contrast two systems handling the same RC-E2E-03 cold-chain excursion:

32.4.1 Raw LLM SDK implementation

A developer writes a Python script. It uses an OpenAI/Anthropic SDK, defines four functions for retrieving SAP inventory and Nielsen pricing, and calls a model with a prompt that says "given this excursion, recommend an action." The model recommends "mark down 35% on aging dairy at BIGBOX-100." The script writes a row to a Postgres "decisions" table. Done.

Six months later: did this script's recommendations actually produce margin lift? Unknown — the decisions table doesn't link to outcomes. Who approved the markdown? The script auto-applied (no HITL was wired). What if the elasticity model was stale? The script doesn't know its inputs' freshness. What did it cost? Reconcile the OpenAI billing CSV against decisions-table rows. How do we improve? Edit the prompt and hope.

32.4.2 APEX agent context implementation

The Pricing Agent runs in Container Apps · authenticates as mi-apex-merch-runtime · calls MCP tool rc_e2e_03.get_pricing_recommendation_basis which returns a Gold mart row with hash · reasons over five-section prompt structure with Year-2 few-shots from Redis · emits a recommendation with a typed output schema · workflow stages a Microsoft Teams adaptive card to Daniel Chen · he approves via the card · MCP commit_markdown_decision writes MERML.Markdown with decision_ledger_id · LEDGER row carries cost_per_decision = $0.0824 · attribution mart links the decision to realized GM lift over the next 14 days · next month's training corpus refresh promotes this exemplar into the few-shot set.

Six months later: did the Pricing Agent's recommendations produce GM lift? Query g_markdown_outcome_attribution. Who approved? The LEDGER row. Was elasticity stale? The mart's silver_max_event_ts records freshness. What did it cost? Sum cost_per_decision grouped by service_code. How do we improve? The Year-2 few-shot refresh already did.

Independence — and why it follows from the architecture

The dimensional difference is structurally tied to Independence. A raw LLM agent doesn't know whose tenant it's in, whose Microsoft contract pays for it, or whose audit committee will eventually walk its lineage. APEX agent context is bounded by tenant from the moment it starts: the managed identity is per-tenant · the Microsoft.KeyVault HSM is per-tenant · the LEDGER chain is per-tenant · the training corpus per ADR-025-01 is per-tenant. The two-contract model is enforced by the agent context, not laid on top after the fact.

32.5 Why You Cannot Retrofit

A reasonable question: "Can we bolt these ten dimensions onto our existing raw-LLM agent codebase?" Technically yes, if you rewrite most of the system. Practically, the bolt-on approach produces a system with the surface of APEX and the audit guarantees of a chatbot. Specifically:

  • Identity bolt-on means rewriting every credential path · the developer's mental model still expects API keys.
  • Tool-boundary bolt-on means MCP-wrapping every existing tool · the agent's prompt still references the old function shapes.
  • Audit bolt-on means hash-chaining a previously-mutable decision table · prior decisions can't be retroactively chained.
  • Governance bolt-on means classifying data after the fact · sensitivity labels can't be inferred backwards.
  • HITL bolt-on means routing existing chat-approvals through Microsoft Teams cards · operators trained on chat resist.
  • Attribution bolt-on means joining decisions to outcomes after the fact · outcome data wasn't shaped to attribute.
  • Cross-Service bolt-on means inverting access graphs · the team that built the consumer is asked to give up scope.
  • Cost bolt-on means re-shaping the audit row · prior rows lack cost_per_decision.
  • Microsoft-platform bolt-on means undoing every cross-cloud abstraction · cross-cloud was the design point.
  • Maturation bolt-on means training on a corpus that was never structured as a corpus · LEDGER is the corpus, and the legacy decision log isn't.

Each bolt-on is technically achievable; collectively they amount to rewriting the system. The architectural choice is upfront, or the rewrite is later. APEX is the upfront choice.

Key Takeaways

  • Ten dimensions distinguish APEX agent context from raw LLM agent SDKs: identity · tool boundary · audit · governance · HITL · attribution · cross-Service · cost · Microsoft integration · maturation substrate.
  • Each dimension is enforced by specific Microsoft platform components — none stays at the level of "best practice."
  • The §32.4 contrast (raw script vs APEX) is the architectural argument made concrete: same decision moment, different operator/regulator/finance experience six months later.
  • The dimensions cannot be retrofitted without rewriting the system. APEX is an upfront architectural choice.

Catalog Drills

  1. Pick an existing internal AI/agent system in your organization. Score it 0–3 on each of the ten dimensions in §32.2. The score gap to APEX is the rebuild cost.
  2. For one Service in your engagement, narrate the §32.4.2 walk-through against your live LEDGER. Pull a real decision; trace each of the ten dimensions through the actual rows and Microsoft platform telemetry.
  3. Identify the dimension that would most surprise a new agent maintainer trained only on raw LLM SDK frameworks. Write the onboarding exercise that surfaces it.
Chapter 33

Change Management — From the Application Era to the Services Era

What This Chapter Does for You
  • Names the organizational shift APEX adoption requires: from delivering applications to delivering Services that mature.
  • Maps each functional discipline (IT · Ops · Training · Procurement · Audit · Compliance · Security) to its Application-era role and its Services-era role.
  • Establishes a practical change-management framework anchored on the Year-1 → Year-2 → Year-3 maturation arc · operator-as-partner model · LEDGER-attributed continuous improvement.
TL;DR · 60 seconds
  • The Application era delivers a feature, trains users, runs uptime; the Services era delivers a Service, partners with operators, runs decision quality.
  • Seven disciplines re-shape: IT (app maintenance → Service ownership) · Ops (server uptime → data quality + agent regression watch) · Training (one-time → prompt curation + few-shot maintenance) · Procurement (license seats → Service envelope sizing) · Audit (annual SOX/ISO → continuous LEDGER chain verification) · Compliance (procedural → architectural) · Security (perimeter → identity-bounded scope).
  • Operators move from users to partners · HITL is collaboration, not workflow · Year-1 confirms-everything · Year-2 confirms-exceptions · Year-3 audits-samples.
  • The change-management framework: start with one Service in one tenant · ship Year-1 instincts · let LEDGER accumulate · refresh Year-2 from LEDGER · promote Year-3 superagents on cosign attestations. The architecture is the change plan.

33.1 The Two Eras, Side by Side

Most organizations entering APEX adoption have spent decades in the Application era. IT departments deliver applications · users adopt by training · the lifecycle is build → deploy → operate → sunset · the unit of change is a feature release. The vocabulary is so familiar that its assumptions are invisible.

The Services era is structurally different. A Service is not a feature; it is a continuously-improving capability with a KPI envelope (Sellers Guide §6.10), an audit chain (LEDGER · §25.2), a training substrate (LEDGER + Redis · Ch 25), a maturation arc (Year 1 → 3 · §25.1), and a commercial structure (Wave 1/2/3 envelopes · §30.1.1). Operators are partners in the maturation, not consumers of a finished product.

DimensionApplication EraServices Era
Unit of deliveryAn application · feature · releaseA Service · KPI envelope · maturation trajectory
User relationshipUser · trained on UI · adopts featureOperator · partner with the system · curates few-shots through approvals
LifecycleBuild → deploy → operate → sunsetDeploy Year-1 instincts → Year-2 record → Year-3 superagent
Unit of changeFeature release · sprint cycleDecision-by-decision (LEDGER) · continuous within Services-era cadence (Ch 25 §25.5)
Quality measureBug count · uptime SLODecision quality · realized KPI lift · HITL acceptance rate
Improvement modelRoadmap · backlog · prioritization meetingsLEDGER attribution mart · Year-2 few-shot refresh · Layer 3 SemVer promotion (Ch 28)
ROI measurementAdoption · usage · feature-flag rolloutAudit-attributed KPI lift · cost-per-decision · Service P&L (§30.1.6)
Failure modeOutage · bug · incidentSilent regression · attribution drift · decision-quality decline (Appendix H.5)
Audit postureAnnual review · evidence packs assembledContinuous chain verification · queryable LEDGER · Microsoft Purview lineage

33.2 Seven Disciplines That Re-Shape

Each functional discipline that operates an APEX engagement has to migrate its mental model. The migration isn't optional; the architecture exposes it whether the org prepares or not.

33.2.1 IT — Application Maintenance → Service Ownership

The application-era IT team owned servers, patched OS images, and chased uptime. The services-era IT team owns Services. The Layer 1 Platform team stands up Microsoft.App/managedEnvironments + Microsoft.KeyVault + Microsoft.OperationalInsights once per tenant; the per-Service team owns the Bicep, the MCP manifest, the Gold-mart pipelines, and the agent-image promotion (Ch 31). Service ownership replaces application maintenance · the unit of accountability is "the Service made < X bad decisions and > Y attributable KPI dollars this quarter."

33.2.2 Ops — Server Uptime → Data Quality + Agent Regression Watch

The application-era Ops team watched server CPU, network in/out, app logs. The services-era Ops team watches a different set: Microsoft Fabric F-SKU utilization (§29.6), Silver-build success rate (§10.3), Gold-mart staleness (Appendix H.3), MCP tool-call latency, and — most importantly — HITL acceptance-rate drop as the early warning of agent regression (Appendix H.5). Container Apps health is green when an agent's reasoning has gotten subtly worse; the platform doesn't catch it. Operators do, by accepting fewer recommendations. Ops watches that signal.

33.2.3 Training — User Training → Prompt Curation + Few-Shot Maintenance

The application-era training team produced LMS modules, walkthrough videos, and certification programs. The services-era equivalent is prompt curation and few-shot exemplar maintenance. Year-1 prompts are hand-authored; Year-2 few-shots are mined from LEDGER (Ch 28 §28.4 · ADR-028-02). The team that previously produced training content now reviews HITL-approved decisions for inclusion in the next-month few-shot set, and reviews HITL-declined decisions for negative few-shots. The skill is the same — synthesize a good example — applied to a different artifact.

33.2.4 Procurement — License Seats → Service Envelope Sizing

The application-era procurement team negotiated per-seat license costs. The services-era procurement team negotiates Service envelope sizing — Wave 1/2/3 selection, Microsoft Fabric F-SKU reservation, Container Apps consumption budget, LLM-call budget, the Cost envelope (§30.1.6). The unit of negotiation is per-decision cost (§29.5), not per-user license. Microsoft Cost Management dashboards become the procurement team's primary review artifact, replacing license-utilization reports.

33.2.5 Audit — Annual Review → Continuous Chain Verification

The application-era audit team assembled evidence packs once a year for SOX, ISO 27001, HIPAA, or sector-specific reviews. The services-era audit team operates against a continuously-verifiable LEDGER chain (§25.2 · Appendix H.6) and Microsoft Purview lineage (§10.4). The audit question shifts from "show me last year's evidence" to "show me the chain right now and let me walk it back to the source SOR row." Annual reviews don't disappear; they become spot-checks against a continuously-attestable substrate. This is faster, cheaper, and more defensible.

33.2.6 Compliance — Procedural → Architectural

Application-era compliance worked through procedural controls — sign-offs, manual reviews, exception logs. Services-era compliance is architectural. Tier-4 PHI cannot be exfiltrated because Bronze never holds raw PHI (§7.2); cross-tenant learning cannot happen because the architecture forbids it (ADR-025-01); decision-attribution cannot drift because the LEDGER is hash-chained to the HSM CMK. Compliance officers shift from "did the team follow the procedure?" to "is the architecture still in spec?" — which is a much more powerful (and much more delegate-able) question.

33.2.7 Security — Perimeter → Identity-Bounded Scope

Application-era security defended a network perimeter. Services-era security operates inside a tenant where every internal call is authenticated as a Microsoft Entra ID managed identity with scoped role assignments. The defense surface is identity-bounded scope — what role each identity can play, what tools each agent can call, what data each managed identity can read. Microsoft Defender for Cloud / Containers / Key Vault / App Service compose into a per-resource defense; Microsoft Sentinel correlates across them. The security team's primary artifact is the identity-and-scope inventory, not the firewall rule book.

33.3 Operators as Partners · The HITL Relationship

The biggest cultural shift is the operator's role. In the application era, the operator is a user — trained on the UI, expected to follow workflows. In the services era, the operator is a partner with the system. HITL approvals are not a gate the operator clears; they are the substrate that trains the next iteration of the agent.

This shifts the conversation from "is the agent right?" to "is the system learning?" — a healthier framing both for the operator (they have agency) and for the architect (they have a feedback loop).

YearOperator's roleWhat the operator is askedMental model
Year 1Confirms everything"Do you trust this recommendation?"The system has instincts; you're the calibration.
Year 2Confirms exceptions"Where do you disagree with the system?"The system has the average right; you're the edge cases.
Year 3Audits samples"Show me the cases where the system was right last quarter."The system has the cases; you're the auditor and the trainer for the next iteration.

Operators who entered an APEX engagement expecting an application become disengaged when Year-1 doesn't feel finished. The change-management work upfront is making the trajectory explicit — Year-1 is supposed to feel like instincts, Year-2 is supposed to feel like recall, Year-3 is supposed to feel like anticipation. Telling operators the trajectory is the contract.

33.4 The Change-Management Framework

A practical framework for delivery leads navigating the shift in a client engagement:

33.4.1 Frame the engagement on Service maturation, not feature delivery

The first conversation with the client (sponsoring CIO, line-of-business owner) sets the frame. APEX is not "deploy and done." APEX is "ship Year-1 instincts in 90 days; ship Year-2 record in 12 months; ship Year-3 superagent in 24–36 months." The W3 outcome-based commercial envelope (Sellers Guide §6.12) makes this the contract — KPI lift commitments are tied to the maturation arc, not to a static feature set.

33.4.2 Pick one Service in one tenant first

The change-management cost of the first Service onboarding is high; subsequent Services in the same tenant are cheap (Deployment Guide §9 · Ch 14 §14.3). Land RC-E2E-03 (or HLS-E2E-04, or whichever Service has clearest KPI envelope and most-engaged operator persona) first; let the org experience the Year-1 maturation trajectory once before scaling Service breadth.

33.4.3 Identify and brief the operator personas explicitly

Each Service has named HITL personas (e.g., RC-E2E-03's Marisol Reyes, Daniel Chen). Brief them explicitly on the Year-1 → 3 trajectory, on the LEDGER's role in capturing their decisions, on the Year-2 few-shot mechanism that turns their approvals into the next iteration's training. Operators who understand their role as training the system approve more thoughtfully; operators who think they are users complain when the system is "wrong" in Year 1.

33.4.4 Stand up the seven disciplines' role-shift in parallel

Don't wait for IT, Ops, Training, Procurement, Audit, Compliance, and Security to discover their role-shift mid-engagement. Brief each discipline's lead on the §33.2 mapping during onboarding; tie each to the specific Microsoft platform component they will be operating against. Audit leads need a Microsoft Purview tour; Compliance leads need an ADR walkthrough; Procurement needs a Microsoft Cost Management dashboard tutorial.

33.4.5 Make the Year-2 transition an explicit milestone

The transition from Year-1 instincts to Year-2 record is not automatic; it requires a deliberate prompt-refresh ceremony. Schedule it at the 12-month mark from Service go-live: pull the LEDGER's top-N approved decisions per persona, curate the Year-2 few-shot set, run Ch 28 §28.5 evaluation gates against the candidate prompt, ship a Layer 3 minor version. This ceremony is the cultural moment when the operator sees their accumulated approvals materialize as agent improvement; it is worth marking it as such.

33.4.6 Resist the "rebuild as features" temptation

Six months in, when Year-1 is still feeling instinctive (because it's supposed to), there will be pressure to "add features" that the operator wants. Resist by funneling that pressure into schema evolution (Ch 9 §9.4) and Gold-mart enrichment (Ch 12) instead. New columns in MERML.Markdown · new fields in g_excursion_decision_panel · new MCP tools · all of these are the right responses to operator feedback. New "features in the agent" are usually just a tell that Year-2 hasn't shipped yet; ship Year-2.

33.5 What Stays the Same

The change-management discussion can over-rotate into "everything is different now." It isn't. The disciplines that mature in the services era are still recognizable disciplines. IT still ships infrastructure; Ops still watches telemetry; Audit still verifies controls. The artifacts change; the disciplines don't.

What also stays the same: the commercial envelope is still a contract; SLA-and-margin economics still apply; the client still has a budget cycle. APEX engagements are not exotic; they fit standard enterprise commercial structures (W2 fixed-price → W3 outcome-based) that procurement teams already understand. The novelty is the architecture; the economics are familiar.

And what most stays the same: the Independence model. The two-contract structure (client ↔ Microsoft for platform, client ↔ Deloitte for services, ECIF flowing direct) is the same in the application era and the services era. The architecture realizes Independence more strictly (ADRs 003-01, 007-01, 025-01 · §30.1.5 Trust envelope), but the commercial doctrine is unchanged.

Independence — services era doesn't relax the boundaries

Some adopting clients hope that "moving to a learning system" might soften the two-contract model — surely a system that learns continuously must benefit from cross-tenant data sharing? It does not. ADR-025-01 codifies the answer: each client tenant is a closed superagent training universe, the architecture enforces the boundary at Microsoft Fabric tenancy and at the Layer 3 image-mirror. The change-management lift is the operator-as-partner shift inside one tenant; it is not a re-negotiation of the commercial contract across tenants.

Key Takeaways

  • The Application era delivers features; the Services era delivers Services that mature. Nine dimensions re-shape between the two eras (§33.1 table).
  • Seven disciplines re-shape — IT · Ops · Training · Procurement · Audit · Compliance · Security — each migrating to a Microsoft-platform-anchored mental model.
  • Operators move from users to partners: Year-1 confirms-everything · Year-2 confirms-exceptions · Year-3 audits-samples. The trajectory is explicit; explaining it is the change-management contract.
  • The framework: frame on maturation · pick one Service first · brief operator personas · parallelize the seven disciplines' role-shift · mark the Year-2 transition · resist the rebuild-as-features temptation.
  • What stays the same: the disciplines themselves · the commercial economics · the Independence model. The architecture is new; the economics and governance contracts are familiar.

Catalog Drills

  1. For an existing client engagement, score each of the seven disciplines (§33.2) on Application-era vs Services-era posture (1 = pure App-era, 5 = pure Services-era). The lowest-scoring discipline is the highest change-management priority.
  2. Draft the explicit Year-1 → 3 operator brief for one Service's HITL persona. What words make the maturation trajectory tangible to someone who has spent 20 years using applications?
  3. Write the §33.4.5 Year-2 transition ceremony agenda for one Service: who attends, what artifacts are reviewed, what decisions are made, what gets shipped at the end. This is the cultural-milestone artifact that changes operators from skeptical to invested.

    Files/raw/{source}/{ds}/        # raw landings, source-system schema

C.2 Bronze Table Template

DELTA — Bronze table conventions
-- One Bronze table per source × entity. Append-only. Native source schema preserved.
CREATE TABLE br_pos_transactions (
    -- source-system-shaped columns (whatever the SOR sent)
    pos_terminal_id      STRING,
    transaction_id       STRING,
    sku_number           STRING,
    qty                  DECIMAL(18,4),
    unit_price           DECIMAL(12,4),
    transaction_ts_local STRING,        -- raw timezone-aware string from POS
    -- canonical APEX columns added at ingest
    event_ts             TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,    -- parsed canonical UTC timestamp
    ingest_ts            TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,    -- when APEX received it
    src_system           STRING NOT NULL,
    src_payload_hash     STRING NOT NULL,       -- SHA-256 of original payload
    -- partitioning column
    ingest_date          DATE GENERATED ALWAYS AS (CAST(ingest_ts AS DATE))
)
USING DELTA
PARTITIONED BY (ingest_date)
TBLPROPERTIES (
    'delta.appendOnly' = 'true',
    'delta.minReaderVersion' = '2',
    'delta.minWriterVersion' = '5'
);

C.3 Silver Table Template

DELTA — Silver table conventions
CREATE TABLE SCML.Inventory (
    inventory_id        STRING NOT NULL,
    sku_id              STRING NOT NULL,
    location_id         STRING NOT NULL,
    location_type       STRING NOT NULL,
    on_hand_qty         DECIMAL(18,4) NOT NULL,
    on_order_qty        DECIMAL(18,4) NOT NULL,
    reserved_qty        DECIMAL(18,4) NOT NULL,
    valuation_currency  STRING NOT NULL,
    valuation_amount    DECIMAL(18,4),
    event_ts            TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,
    ingest_ts           TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,
    silver_ts           TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,
    src_system          STRING NOT NULL,
    src_inventory_id    STRING
)
USING DELTA
PARTITIONED BY (location_id)
ZORDER BY (sku_id, event_ts)
TBLPROPERTIES (
    'delta.minReaderVersion' = '2',
    'delta.minWriterVersion' = '5',
    'apex.schema_family' = 'SCML',
    'apex.entity'        = 'Inventory',
    'apex.lineage_uri'   = 'purview:assets/scml-inventory'
);

C.4 Gold Mart Template (decision-panel example)

T-SQL — Gold mart on Microsoft Fabric Warehouse
CREATE TABLE g_excursion_decision_panel (
    excursion_id        VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,
    store_id            VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,
    fired_ts            DATETIME2    NOT NULL,
    -- joined columns from SCML.Lot, SCML.Inventory, MERML.Markdown
    lot_id              VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,
    at_risk_qty         DECIMAL(18,4),
    at_risk_value_usd   DECIMAL(18,2),
    recent_markdowns    NVARCHAR(MAX),  -- JSON array
    duration_min        INT,
    peak_temp_c         DECIMAL(5,2),
    -- materialization metadata (mandatory on every Gold table)
    silver_max_event_ts DATETIME2    NOT NULL,
    materialized_at     DATETIME2    NOT NULL,
    row_hash            VARCHAR(70)  NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY NONCLUSTERED (excursion_id) NOT ENFORCED
);

-- Partitioning + distribution for Microsoft Fabric Warehouse
-- Fabric handles distribution automatically; partition pruning via fired_ts
Appendix D

Orchestration Pattern Library

Each of the four workflow patterns from Chapter 14 rendered as a primitive sequence, with example Service references.

D.1 Triage-and-Decide

PRIMITIVES
1.  Trigger (event)
2.  LEDGER emit (workflow.start)
3.  Agent call → The Analyst /run
4.  Branch on severity score
   ├─ high:
   |   5.  Parallel:
   |       a.  Agent call → The Demand Checker
   |       b.  Agent call → The Finance Lead
   |   6.  Agent call → The Operations Lead (synthesize action)
   |   7.  HITL stage (Teams adaptive card)
   |   8.  Branch on approval:
   |       ├─ approved:  MCP call → commit_decision (decision-emit)
   |       └─ declined:  LEDGER emit (declined)
   └─ low:  LEDGER emit (closed-without-action)
9.  LEDGER emit (workflow.complete)

Used by — RC-E2E-03 cold-chain · HLS-E2E-04 prior auth · ER-PU-04 outage triage · TMT-TEL-NET-02 fault prediction · TH-AIR-02 IROPS · ICE-Aftermarket-01 dispatch · ER-OG-07 pipeline leak · ER-OG-03 wellhead · AXLE-CF-01 stamping · AXLE-Supply-04 disruption · ICE-EaaS-04 uptime · TMT-TEL-CC-03 contact center.

D.2 Continuous-Optimization

PRIMITIVES
1.  Trigger (schedule · cron)
2.  LEDGER emit (workflow.start)
3.  MCP call → get_proposal_basis (read · Gold mart)
4.  Agent call → The Demand Checker (candidate generation)
5.  Agent call → The Finance Lead (commercial scoring)
6.  Agent call → The Operations Lead (batch HITL composition)
7.  HITL stage (batch approval card)
8.  Branch on approved candidates:
    └─ MCP call → commit_decisions (loop · decision-emit)
9.  LEDGER emit (workflow.complete)

Used by — RC-E2E-03 markdown · TH-HOT-03 dynamic pricing · TMT-MED-01 recommendations · RC-E2E-06 workforce · RC-E2E-08 marketing · AXLE-Ops-03 production scheduling · TH-AIR-06 crew · ER-OG-05 refinery yield · ICE-Aftermarket-05 parts · ICE-Connected-06 dealer forecast · TMT-TEC-04 expansion · HLS-E2E-06 HEDIS.

D.3 Detect-and-Attribute

PRIMITIVES
1.  Trigger (event · stream)
2.  Agent call → The Analyst (classify event)
3.  MCP call → get_attribution_basis (read · attribution mart)
4.  Branch on score / pattern match:
    ├─ above threshold:
    |   5.  HITL stage
    |   6.  MCP call → commit_action_decision (decision-emit)
    └─ below threshold:
        LEDGER emit (auto-cleared)
7.  LEDGER emit (workflow.complete)

Used by — RC-E2E-07 returns fraud · ICE-Aftermarket-03 warranty fraud · TMT-MED-03 ad fraud · TMT-TEL-RAA-04 RA variance · ER-MN-02 environmental compliance · TH-AIR-04 baggage · TH-HOT-05 sentiment · AXLE-QMS-02 quality · HLS-LS-03 trial matching · RC-E2E-09 recall.

D.4 Periodic-Digest

PRIMITIVES
1.  Trigger (schedule · cron)
2.  Parallel:
    a.  MCP call → get_kpi_panel (Gold time-series)
    b.  MCP call → get_attribution_panel (Gold attribution)
    c.  MCP call → get_outstanding_decisions
3.  Agent call → The Briefer (compose digest)
4.  Decide channel (Teams · Power BI · email)
5.  HTTP call → publish to channel
6.  LEDGER emit (workflow.complete)

Used by — every Service has at least one periodic-digest workflow.

Edges in the Service-to-Service consumption graph — a Service consumes another's MCP tool or its data via Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse Shortcut. Either edge is a cross-Service composition (Chapter 17).

E.2 Cross-Practice Compositions

HLS-E2E-04HLS-E2E-06Member benefit-design used in UM-policy match

ICE-Connected-06ICE-Aftermarket-05Parts demand for dealer forecasting
TMT-MED-03TMT-MED-01Recommendation impressions consumed for fraud detection

E.3 The Composition Rule (Recap)

  • Compose at the MCP layer when the consumer needs the data at decision time (low-latency, single-row).
  • Compose via Lakehouse Shortcut when the consumer needs the data at materialization time (high-volume, joined into Silver/Gold).
  • The data-owner Service's Bicep grants role assignments; never the consumer's. (Deployment Guide §9.4.)
Appendix F

Glossary

Attribution mart — Gold-tier mart that joins LEDGER decisions with downstream outcomes; produces the KPI envelope numbers the Service commits to (e.g., realized GM lift attributable to agent-suggested markdowns).

BRMML — Billing · Revenue · Mediation Model Layer. Telecom-anchored; primary entities are UsageEvent, RatedTransaction, Invoice, Variance.

Bronze — Medallion tier 1: append-only, full-fidelity record of what the SOR sent. Hosted in Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse (batch) or Microsoft Fabric KQL Database (streaming).

CMSML — Claims · Member · Submission Model Layer. Health-payer schemas: Claim, Authorization, Member, Denial, Appeal.

Conformance — The Silver-tier work of mapping raw Bronze rows to canonical APEX schemas: type stabilization, naming normalization, foreign-key resolution, deduplication.

CRMML — Customer Relationship Model Layer. Customer, Interaction, Loyalty, Sentiment.

Decision-panel mart — Gold mart shape: one row per triggering event with all the decision context joined. Most common shape across the catalog (~40% of marts).

ECRML — Environmental · Compliance · Regulatory Model Layer. Permit, Emission, Violation, AuditTrail.

EHRML — Electronic Health Records Model Layer. Encounter, Diagnosis, Procedure, Medication, LabResult.

Eventstream — Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence component for ingesting and routing streaming events. Microsoft.Fabric/eventstreams.

FSMA-204 — U.S. Food Safety Modernization Act §204 — lot-traceability rule; SCML.Lot's chain_of_custody column is the structured representation.

GLML — General Ledger Model Layer. Account, Posting, TrialBalance, Adjustment.

Gold — Medallion tier 3: decision-ready, denormalized, agent-queryable. Hosted in Microsoft Fabric Warehouse with Microsoft Fabric Direct Lake serving Power BI.

HEDIS — Healthcare Effectiveness Data and Information Set; care-quality measure set; HLS-E2E-06's KPI is closure rate against HEDIS measures.

HITL — Human-in-the-loop; the workflow primitive that pauses orchestration for human approval, typically rendered as a Microsoft Teams adaptive card.

KQL — Kusto Query Language; the query language for Microsoft Fabric KQL Database (streaming Bronze) and Microsoft Sentinel.

Lakehouse Shortcut — Microsoft Fabric mechanism to expose Lakehouse data from one workspace into another without copying; used for cross-Service entity reuse.

LEDGER — Append-only audit table on Azure SQL Database (or Azure Database for PostgreSQL Flexible Server in DEV); hash-chained per Deployment Guide §6.3.1.

Materialization — The act of building a Gold mart from upstream Silver (and other) sources. Triggered by schedule, by upstream Eventstream event, or by Microsoft Fabric Direct Lake auto-refresh.

MCP — Model Context Protocol; the typed RPC contract between agents and Gold-tier data through MCP tools.

Medallion — Bronze → Silver → Gold tier model. APEX uses the Microsoft Fabric medallion explicitly.

MERML — Merchandising Model Layer. Markdown, Elasticity, Competitor, Promotion, Assortment.

Microsoft Fabric Real-Time Intelligence (RTI) — The streaming substrate: Eventstream + KQL Database + Eventhouse.

Microsoft Fabric Warehouse — T-SQL surface over OneLake Delta; primary host of Gold marts. Microsoft.Fabric/warehouses.

Microsoft Purview — Catalog and lineage surface; registers every Bronze table, Silver entity, Gold mart, MCP tool, and LEDGER asset for unified audit-grade traceability.

NETML — Network & Infrastructure Model Layer. Asset, Outage, Topology, TicketEvent.

OEMML — OEM Connected Operations Model Layer. Equipment, Telemetry, Maintenance, OEMSerial.

PII Tier — Four-tier sensitivity model (Public, Internal, Confidential PII, Restricted PHI / regulated PII); drives Bronze tokenization and Microsoft Purview labels.

pii-unlock — The Layer 1 user-assigned managed identity acquired just-in-time at HITL approval moments to re-identify a single tokenized record.

Point-in-time mart — Gold mart shape: one row per key with current state, kept fresh by upstream Silver builds and Microsoft Fabric Direct Lake auto-refresh.

PROML — Pricing Model Layer. Pricing, PriceList, DiscountRule, Yield.

QMSML — Quality Management Model Layer. Defect, RootCause, Containment, Recurrence.

row_hash — SHA-256 over the canonical-JSON form of a materialized Gold row; returned by every MCP call alongside the data; recorded on the LEDGER row that consumed it.

SCML — Supply Chain Model Layer. Inventory, Lot, Movement, Receipt, Shipment.

Service — One of 38 productized envelopes from the APEX scenario chains catalog; the unit of deployment, commercial envelope, and audit attribution.

Silver — Medallion tier 2: canonical conformance to APEX schema families; idempotent rebuild contract from Bronze.

SOR — Source-of-Record; the client system that originates the data. APEX classifies SOR systems into five categories: operational, master/ERP, customer/member, asset/IoT, external.

SOX — Sarbanes-Oxley; controls framework that TMT-TEL-RAA-04's adjustment LEDGER rows map to for auditor reconstruction.

Time-series mart — Gold mart shape: rolling-window aggregations of metrics over time, partitioned by the question's dimension. KPI rollup marts are usually time-series.

Tokenization — Replacing a PII value with a deterministic HMAC-SHA256 token at ingest, using a Microsoft.KeyVault HSM-held key. Deterministic so joins still work; one-way so original is unrecoverable without HSM unwrap.

UM — Utilization Management; payer process for authorizing care; HLS-E2E-04's policy match runs against the UM rule library.

WLML — Warranty Lifecycle Model Layer. Warranty, Claim, Recovery, SupplierResponsibility.

Workflow parity — The contract that every orchestration is maintained as a single intent (workflow.intent.md) with substrate-specific JSON for both n8n (laptop) and Azure Logic Apps Hybrid (Microsoft platform).

WOSFML — Workforce Operations · Shift / Schedule Model Layer. Shift, Schedule, TaskAssignment, LaborCost.

Closing Independence Reminder

Throughout this guide and any deployment artifact derived from it, the Deloitte–Microsoft relationship is described as "Deloitte's Microsoft practice," "DMTSP," "Microsoft platform capabilities," or "Microsoft-native deployment." Never partner, alliance, or strategic alliance. The two-contract structure (client ↔ Microsoft for platform, client ↔ Deloitte for services, ECIF direct) is enforced architecturally — by Microsoft Fabric tenancy, the two Container Registries, the Microsoft.KeyVault HSM boundary, and the Microsoft Purview audit graph. End of book.

G.11 How to Find What This Appendix Doesn't Have

  1. Microsoft platform topics — search at learn.microsoft.com by resource type (e.g., "Microsoft.App/containerApps") or feature name. Microsoft Learn is the canonical index.
  2. Practitioner content — Microsoft Tech Community at techcommunity.microsoft.com by category (Azure · Microsoft Fabric · Microsoft 365 · Industry).
  3. Reference architectures — Azure Architecture Center at learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture; filter by industry and pattern.
  4. Standards / regulatory — search the standards body's site by name; specific document paths change far more often than the org's root URL.
  5. Open-source projects — GitHub search by the project name; the README links to current docs sites.
Independence — Cite, don't bind

Linking to external Microsoft content (Microsoft Learn, Microsoft Tech Community, Azure Architecture Center) reinforces the Microsoft-platform posture this book takes throughout. Linking to non-Microsoft open-source projects (MCP, Notation, cosign, Delta Lake, n8n, Redis) is part of the architecture and intentional. Linking to industry standards bodies cites the regulatory or commercial context — those orgs are not Deloitte or Microsoft alliances and the book's references to them carry no co-marketing implication. The two-contract Independence posture extends to citation hygiene: name the standard, link to the org, never imply a partnership where there is only a citation.

Six single-page cheat sheets — print them, pin them next to the workstation, keep the book open to the relevant chapter for the deep dive. Each sheet is the ~80% of the chapter you reach for most often, condensed.

Four workflow shapes · triage-and-decide (~18 Services) · continuous-optimization (~8) · detect-and-attribute (~6) · periodic-digest (every Service has one).

J.4 Service Profile Template

Every Service profile in Part VI uses this template. Copy this block when onboarding a new Service.

YAML — Service profile skeleton
service_code: ___-___-___
practice:     ___ (RC | HLS | ER | AXLE | TH | TMT | ICE)
domain:       ___
title:        ___

kpi_envelope:
  - ___ : +/- ___
  - ___ : ___

scenarios_in_scope:
  - ___

bronze_sources:
  - sor:       ___ (POS · ERP · EHR · IoT · etc.)
    landing:  br_____
    cadence:  streaming | near-rt | batch
    pii_tier: 1 | 2 | 3 | 4

silver_schemas:
  - ___.___  # canonical entity, e.g., SCML.Inventory

gold_marts:
  - name:    g_____
    shape:   point-in-time | decision-panel | time-series | attribution
    backs:   "<agent question>"

agents:
  - role:      Analyst | Specialist | Composer | Briefer
    name:      ___
    reads:    [g_____]
    archetype: ___ (Ch 28)

mcp_tools:
  - name:    ___.___
    pattern: read | decision-emit | hitl-stage
    consumed_by: [agent name]

workflow_shape: triage-and-decide | continuous-optimization | detect-and-attribute | periodic-digest

hitl_personas:
  - name:    ___
    role:    ___
    surface: Teams adaptive card

kpi_attribution_path: |
  Decisions → MERML/CMSML/etc. (with decision_ledger_id)
  → g_*_outcome_attribution (joins LEDGER × outcome)
  → g_kpi_*_daily (rollup)
  → Power BI Direct Lake (operator surface)

compliance: |
  ___ (FSMA | HIPAA | 21 CFR Part 11 | SOX | etc.)

J.5 LEDGER Row Shape

SQL — LEDGER essentials
CREATE TABLE LEDGER (
    ledger_id            UNIQUEIDENTIFIER NOT NULL,
    service_code         VARCHAR(64)   NOT NULL,
    workflow_run_id      UNIQUEIDENTIFIER NOT NULL,
    step_id              VARCHAR(64)   NOT NULL,
    event                VARCHAR(64)   NOT NULL,
        -- 'workflow.start' · 'mcp.tool.called' · 'hitl.staged' · 'hitl.resolved'
        -- · 'pii_unlock_requested' · 'pii_unlock_completed'
        -- · 'decision.committed' · 'workflow.complete' · 'ledger.chain.verify'
    agent_id             VARCHAR(64),
    tool_name            VARCHAR(128),
    input_args           NVARCHAR(MAX),    -- JSON
    output_row_hash      VARCHAR(70),      -- 'sha256:' + 64 hex
    decision_payload     NVARCHAR(MAX),
    cost_per_decision    DECIMAL(12,4),    -- USD
    safety_verdict       VARCHAR(32),
    -- Outcome attribution (filled after the fact by attribution mart pipeline)
    attribution_outcome  NVARCHAR(MAX),
    attribution_filled_at DATETIME2,
    -- Hash chain
    _hash                VARCHAR(70)   NOT NULL,
    _prev_hash           VARCHAR(70),
    -- Stamps
    event_ts             DATETIME2     NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (ledger_id)
);
-- WORM enforcement: REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE on LEDGER FROM ALL except break-glass.

Appendix I

Cross-Volume Reference Index

The APEX Trilogy was designed so each volume reads standalone but reinforces the others. This appendix is the practical lookup: "if you're reading Volume X §Y, here's where the same concept lands in the sibling volumes." The convention used by all three volumes is to cite siblings by chapter or section number — never by title alone — so the reference resolves even when titles are revised. This index makes that convention navigable.

Read Volume I as the Sellers Guide; Volume II as this book (Services Guide); Volume III as the Deployment Guide. Where a topic only appears in two volumes, the third column is left blank.
